# Disaster Management Flashcards

> One flashcard per Act, institution, scheme, plan, scale, framework, convention and landmark disaster in the repository Disaster Management owners (basic + advanced), plus concept cards per theme.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia, CDRI, UNDRR and other pages that loaded; confirmed items carry a ✅ tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. NDMA, MHA, PIB and WHO pages were mostly unreachable. IDs: DM-001–152 (Concepts to Tsunami), DM-201–310 (Epidemics to Governance), DM-311–410 (Cyclones to CBRN; renumbered from DM-101–200 to avoid clashing IDs).

---

## DISASTER MANAGEMENT — Concepts, Legal Architecture, Community DRR, Early Warning, Earthquake, Tsunami

Source key: `(✅ owner NN)` = grounded in the repository owner file NN (basic/advanced, Disaster-Management); `(✅ Wiki: …)` = confirmed on a fetched Wikipedia page; `(✅ UNDRR)` = fetched UNDRR terminology page; `⚠️` = not web-verified, owner-stated uncertainty, or memory-only. Web fetches attempted: UNDRR terminology, Wikipedia (DM Act 2005, ITEWC, Sendai Framework, NDRF, Early Warnings for All, Tsunami Ready, Seismic zone, Earthquake engineering in India). The NIDM Act 2025 PDF (nidm.mha.gov.in) returned unreadable binary, so DM-045 to DM-051 section numbers are owner-sourced only. Several .gov.in, PIB and Wikipedia URLs returned 404 and were not verified.

### GROUP — 01 Concepts, Risk, Resilience and Sendai

### Flashcard DM-001 — Disaster (UNDRR definition)
**Front:** How is a "disaster" defined, and how does it differ from a hazard?
**Definition (learn):** A serious disruption to the functioning of a community that causes human, material, economic and environmental losses beyond the community's ability to cope. It results from hazards interacting with conditions of vulnerability and insufficient capacity to reduce negative consequences (✅ owner 01, citing UNDRR).
**Breakdown:**
- Hazard = potential damaging event or condition; disaster = realised loss beyond coping
- Four loss types: human, material, economic, environmental
- Same hazard, different outcome, depending on exposure, vulnerability and capacity
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNDRR terminology; VisionIAS PDF p. 4 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-002, DM-004, DM-005, DM-006, DM-009
**Trap:** "A natural hazard is a natural disaster" is wrong. A hazard becomes a disaster only when it overwhelms a community's coping capacity.
**Mains hook:** Argue that disasters are socially produced. Qualify it: hazards are largely unavoidable, but the scale of loss is governable through exposure and vulnerability control.
**Back:** *"Hazard is the spark; the disaster is the fire the community cannot put out."*

### Flashcard DM-002 — Hazard (classification)
**Front:** What is a hazard, and how is it classified?
**Definition (learn):** A dangerous condition or event with the potential to cause injury to life or damage to property or the environment (✅ owner 01, VisionIAS PDF p. 4).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: geophysical, hydrological, climatological, meteorological, biological
- Anthropogenic: pollution, deforestation, chemical spillage
- Classification is used by source to route response; each hazard is developed in topics 05–13
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 4 (✅ owner 01); UNDRR terminology (✅ UNDRR).
**Links:** DM-001, DM-003, DM-008; hazard-specific cards in 05, 06 and 07
**Trap:** Do not treat "anthropogenic hazard" as the same as "socio-natural disaster." The owner classifies by origin; the socio-natural label refers to disasters where human action amplifies a natural trigger.
**Mains hook:** Use the natural/anthropogenic split to show that some risk is human-made (e.g., floodplain encroachment), so mitigation must target human decisions as well as the physical process.
**Back:** *"Geo, Hydro, Climate, Met, Bio — nature's five; then the human-made hazards: pollution, deforestation, spillage."*

### Flashcard DM-003 — Exposure
**Front:** What is exposure in the risk equation?
**Definition (learn):** The people, property, systems and assets located in places where hazards can occur and therefore be harmed (⚠️ owner 01 treats exposure as one of the four interacting components; the wording above is the standard UNDRR-style definition, not a fetched quote).
**Breakdown:**
- Exposure answers "who and what is in harm's way?"
- It is distinct from vulnerability: exposure is presence, vulnerability is susceptibility
- Rises with urban sprawl, floodplain settlement and coastal development
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNDRR terminology (✅ UNDRR, function of four elements); owner 01 Section 3 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-004, DM-005, DM-006, DM-026
**Trap:** Exposure is not vulnerability. A large population can be exposed yet less vulnerable, and a small poor group can be highly vulnerable yet barely exposed.
**Mains hook:** Use exposure growth to explain why disaster losses rise even when hazard frequency is unchanged. Qualify it with the caution that exposure data is incomplete in many districts.
**Back:** *"Exposure is who stands in the path; vulnerability is how badly the storm hits them."*

### Flashcard DM-004 — Vulnerability (five types)
**Front:** What are the five types of vulnerability in the VisionIAS typology?
**Definition (learn):** Conditions determined by physical, social, economic and environmental factors or processes that increase a community's susceptibility to the impact of hazards (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- E-P-S-E-A: **E**conomic (income diversity, access to means of production), **P**hysical (proximity, construction type), **S**ocial (poor, pregnant/lactating women, disabled, children, elderly), **E**nvironmental (ecosystem sensitivity), **A**ttitudinal (community disposition against change, dependence on outside support)
- Attitudinal vulnerability is the rarely used dimension
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF pp. 4–5 (✅ owner 01, owner 03).
**Links:** DM-003, DM-029, DM-069, DM-082
**Trap:** Vulnerability is not synonymous with poverty alone. Attitudinal and environmental vulnerability are separate categories. A 2019 PYQ on vulnerability (DM-029) rewards naming more than economic factors.
**Mains hook:** Use the five-part typology to disaggregate impact by group. Qualify it: vulnerability is historically produced and changes with development choices.
**Back:** *"EPSEA — Economic, Physical, Social, Environmental, Attitudinal."*

### Flashcard DM-005 — Capacity
**Front:** What is capacity in disaster risk, and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** The strengths, attributes and resources available within a community, society or organisation to manage and reduce disaster risk and strengthen resilience (⚠️ standard UNDRR-style wording; owner 01 describes capacity as "strengths/resources for managing and reducing risk").
**Breakdown:**
- Insufficient capacity or risk-reduction measures converts a hazard into a disaster (✅ owner 01)
- Capacity includes institutions, knowledge, resources and networks (✅ revision chart)
- Capacity is not a fixed divisor; it interacts with the other three components
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 4 (✅ owner 01); UNDRR terminology (✅ UNDRR).
**Links:** DM-001, DM-006, DM-009, DM-021
**Trap:** Capacity is not the same as resilience. Capacity is available strength; resilience is the ability to absorb and recover from impact (✅ revision chart).
**Mains hook:** Show that capacity gaps, not hazard size alone, explain differential losses across districts. Qualify it: capacity is measured inadequately, so its effect is often inferred.
**Back:** *"Capacity is the bench strength of the community; resilience is how well it plays through the shock."*

### Flashcard DM-006 — Disaster risk (probabilistic interaction, not arithmetic)
**Front:** How is disaster risk determined according to UNDRR, and what is the trap?
**Definition (learn):** Potential loss of life, injury, or destroyed or damaged assets in a specific period, determined probabilistically as a function of hazard, exposure, vulnerability and capacity (✅ UNDRR terminology page, fetched; owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Four inputs: hazard, exposure, vulnerability, capacity
- The relation is probabilistic and context-specific
- Owner 01 warns that a simple "hazard × vulnerability ÷ capacity" heuristic is only a limited teaching device
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNDRR terminology (✅ UNDRR); VisionIAS PDF p. 4 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-001 to DM-005, DM-009, DM-026
**Trap:** Do not present a formula as universal arithmetic. Do not omit exposure, and do not treat capacity as a fixed denominator.
**Mains hook:** Define the risk for the specific hazard in four-part terms. Close by noting that reducing risk means changing exposure, vulnerability or capacity, not the hazard alone.
**Back:** *"Four ingredients, one probability: Hazard, Exposure, Vulnerability, Capacity — never a fixed sum."*

### Flashcard DM-007 — Four ways of dealing with risk
**Front:** Name the four ways of dealing with risk.
**Definition (learn):** Informed acceptance of consequences; avoidance of risk-generating activity; reduction of likelihood or consequence; and transfer of the burden to another party, such as insurance (✅ owner 01, VisionIAS PDF p. 5).
**Breakdown:**
- Accept (retain the residual risk knowingly)
- Avoid (do not build or do the risky activity)
- Reduce (lower likelihood or consequence)
- Transfer (shift the financial burden, e.g. insurance)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 5 (✅ owner 01); risk transfer developed in topic 16 (✅ owner 16 cross-link).
**Links:** DM-006, DM-010, DM-041 (relief versus mitigation funds)
**Trap:** Risk reduction and risk transfer are not interchangeable. Insurance transfers financial loss but does not physically reduce hazard or vulnerability (✅ revision chart).
**Mains hook:** Show how the four strategies complement each other. Qualify: transfer is a financial tool and cannot substitute for reduction in exposed, unsafe settlements.
**Back:** *"ARTR — Accept, Avoid, Reduce, Transfer. Only Transfer moves the money, never the hazard."*

### Flashcard DM-008 — Rapid-onset versus slow-onset disasters
**Front:** How do rapid-onset and slow-onset disasters differ, and which examples are given?
**Definition (learn):** Rapid-onset disasters (earthquake, cyclone, flood, tsunami) have sudden acute impact. Slow-onset ("creeping") emergencies (climate change, desertification, soil degradation, drought) unfold over months or years and must be reflected in preparedness planning (✅ owner 01, VisionIAS PDF p. 6).
**Breakdown:**
- Rapid onset: short warning, acute impact, emergency response focus
- Slow onset: gradual erosion of livelihoods, anticipatory action needed
- Owner 01 notes institutional response is historically built around rapid-onset events
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 6 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-002, DM-009, DM-010; slow-onset treatment in topics 09 and 15 (✅ owner 01 cross-link)
**Trap:** Slow-onset does not mean low-impact. Drought and heat are slow-onset but cause heavy losses. Do not treat "creeping" as only a climate-change term.
**Mains hook:** Argue that slow-onset risk needs anticipatory social protection, not only emergency relief after a threshold declaration (✅ revision chart). Qualify: the classification is a planning tool, and many events are hybrid.
**Back:** *"Quick shock or slow squeeze — both drain the community; plan for the squeeze too."*

### Flashcard DM-009 — Resilience
**Front:** What is disaster resilience, and what are its elements? (2024 Q17 concept)
**Definition (learn):** The ability of a system, community or society to resist, absorb, accommodate and recover from the effects of a hazard in a timely and efficient manner. Owner 01 notes this is built from the cycle and DRR material rather than one source-defined line (⚠️ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Elements named for an answer: risk knowledge, robustness, redundancy, resourcefulness, rapid recovery, and adaptive/transformative capacity (✅ owner 01 Section 13.2; ✅ owner 01 advanced 12)
- Resilience is not restoration of the previous, possibly unsafe, state
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF pp. 4–5 (✅ owner 01); PYQ 2024 Q17 (DM-027).
**Links:** DM-001, DM-006, DM-010, DM-013, DM-027
**Trap:** Resilience is not "bouncing back." Recovery that recreates risk fails the resilience test. Owner 01 Section 8 gives this as a trap.
**Mains hook:** Define resilience, name the elements, then show how Build Back Better converts recovery into risk reduction. Qualify with the point that resilience has no agreed single metric.
**Back:** *"RRRRR+ — Robustness, Redundancy, Resourcefulness, Rapid recovery, Risk knowledge."*

### Flashcard DM-010 — Disaster management cycle; Risk Management Phase versus Crisis Management Phase
**Front:** How does the VisionIAS cycle divide the phases, and what replaced the "3 Rs"?
**Definition (learn):** The cycle is split into a pre-disaster Risk Management Phase (prevention, mitigation, preparedness) and a post-disaster Crisis Management Phase (relief, response, rehabilitation, reconstruction, recovery) (✅ owner 01, VisionIAS PDF p. 6; ✅ owner 00 Section 2).
**Breakdown:**
- Risk Management: prevention, mitigation, preparedness
- Crisis Management: relief, response, rehabilitation, reconstruction, recovery
- Traditional "3 Rs" (Rescue, Relief, Restoration) replaced with "3 Ps" (Prevention, Preparedness, Proofing) (✅ owner 01)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 6 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-011, DM-012, DM-030 (the Act), DM-054 (IRS)
**Trap:** The "3 Ps" is the framing used by the source. Do not state it as an NDMA or statutory categorisation without a separate source.
**Mains hook:** Route any hazard answer through the cycle and name the phase. Conclude by showing the answer feeds back into prevention. Qualify that the cycle is not strictly linear.
**Back:** *"Before impact: Prevent, Mitigate, Prepare. After impact: Relieve, Respond, Rehabilitate, Reconstruct, Recover. From Rescue-Relief-Restoration to Prevention-Preparedness-Proofing."*

### Flashcard DM-011 — DRR definition and the field of action
**Front:** What is Disaster Risk Reduction, and what are the components of DRR action?
**Definition (learn):** The concept and practice of reducing disaster risks through reduced exposure to hazards, lessened vulnerability of people and property, wise management of land and environment, and improved preparedness (✅ owner 01, VisionIAS PDF p. 8).
**Breakdown:**
- Field of action in sequence: policy/legal framework → risk assessment → risk awareness → plan implementation → early warning → use of knowledge (✅ owner 01, PDF p. 8)
- Owner 01 Advanced: this is a sequence with feedback, not a menu; skipping risk assessment undermines later stages
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 8 (✅ owner 01 basic and advanced).
**Links:** DM-010, DM-013, DM-014, DM-086
**Trap:** Do not present the six components as independent options. Early warning depends on risk knowledge.
**Mains hook:** Use the sequence to diagnose which stage fails in a particular state. Qualify that India's warnings may be strong while risk assessment or land-use control is weak.
**Back:** *"Policy, Assess, Aware, Plan, Warn, Use-knowledge — PAAPWU, in that order, with feedback."*

### Flashcard DM-012 — Hyogo Framework for Action (2005–2015)
**Front:** What was the Hyogo Framework for Action, and what were its five priorities?
**Definition (learn):** The predecessor international DRR framework covering 2005–2015, described by Wikipedia as the most encompassing international DRR accord before Sendai (✅ Wiki: Sendai Framework; years fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Five priorities (owner 01, PDF p. 61): make DRR a priority; improve risk information and early warning; build a culture of safety and resilience; reduce risk in key sectors; strengthen preparedness for response
- Sendai succeeded Hyogo
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2005–2015 (✅ Wiki); five priorities (✅ owner 01, PDF p. 61 only; not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-013, DM-028 (2018 PYQ), DM-021
**Trap:** Hyogo had five priorities; Sendai has four priorities and seven targets. Do not swap the counts.
**Mains hook:** Compare Hyogo and Sendai by structure: Hyogo's priorities versus Sendai's measurable targets. Qualify: the shift to targets does not itself prove better outcomes.
**Back:** *"Hyogo: five priorities, ten years. Sendai: four priorities, seven targets, fifteen years."*

### Flashcard DM-013 — Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030
**Front:** What is the Sendai Framework, when and where was it adopted, and what are its four priorities?
**Definition (learn):** The post-2015 global DRR framework, adopted at the Third UN World Conference on DRR in Sendai, Japan, in March 2015 (owner 01 gives 18 March 2015 citing UNDRR), and endorsed by the UN General Assembly in June 2015 (✅ Wiki: Sendai Framework).
**Breakdown:**
- Four priorities: (1) Understanding disaster risk; (2) Strengthening disaster risk governance; (3) Investing in DRR for resilience; (4) Enhancing preparedness for effective response and "Build Back Better" (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 00)
- Seven global targets (DM-015 to DM-021)
- Successor to Hyogo
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Adopted March 2015, Sendai, Japan (✅ Wiki); owner 01 date (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-012, DM-014, DM-015–DM-021, DM-022, DM-027
**Trap:** Sendai is not a treaty. "Priorities" are four, "targets" are seven. Do not describe the 2015 date as the start of a binding obligation.
**Mains hook:** Use the priorities as diagnostic categories. Qualify: the framework sets measurable goals but has no binding enforcement mechanism.
**Back:** *"Understand, Govern, Invest, Prepare — four priorities that all lead to seven targets, A to G."*

### Flashcard DM-014 — Legal status of Sendai: voluntary and non-binding
**Front:** Is the Sendai Framework legally binding?
**Definition (learn):** No. It is a voluntary, non-binding framework agreed at a UN World Conference; it creates no enforceable international legal obligation (✅ owner 01, owner 00 Section 6).
**Breakdown:**
- It is a framework agreed at a UN world conference
- Accountability is through reporting, monitoring and political declarations, not judicial enforcement
- Target E is the only one with an earlier deadline (2020)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 01 Section 8 (✅ owner 01); UNDRR implementation pages (not fetched, 404 for the targets page).
**Links:** DM-013, DM-019, DM-022, DM-023, DM-024
**Trap:** The most common Prelims trap is calling Sendai a binding treaty. The second is calling India's 2016 ten-point agenda legally binding; it is a policy agenda.
**Mains hook:** Explain the accountability gap: measurable targets with no enforcement. Qualify that voluntary frameworks still shape domestic plans, as with NDMP alignment.
**Back:** *"Sendai speaks, it does not sue — voluntary, not binding."*

### Flashcard DM-015 — Sendai Target A: mortality
**Front:** What does Sendai Target A require?
**Definition (learn):** Substantially reduce global disaster mortality by 2030, measured as the average per-100,000 mortality in 2020–2030 against 2005–2015 (✅ Wiki: Sendai Framework; ✅ owner 01 Section 5).
**Breakdown:**
- Target type: loss-reduction (outcome)
- Deadline: 2030
- Metric: per-100,000 decadal average
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Target A (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-016, DM-022, DM-024
**Trap:** Target A is a global target, not an India-specific one. Do not claim Indian compliance from a global trend.
**Mains hook:** Mention Target A in a Sendai answer as an outcome target that states cannot control unilaterally. Qualify with the 24 September 2026 SFM reported global mortality decline (DM-024).
**Back:** *"A = Alive: fewer deaths per hundred thousand."*

### Flashcard DM-016 — Sendai Target B: affected people
**Front:** What does Sendai Target B require?
**Definition (learn):** Substantially reduce the number of affected people globally by 2030, on the same per-100,000 decadal-average basis as Target A (✅ Wiki: Sendai Framework; ✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Loss-reduction target
- Affected people include injured, displaced, evacuated or otherwise directly affected persons (⚠️ standard UNDRR term; owner 01 does not define it)
- Deadline 2030
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Target B (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-015, DM-024, DM-079
**Trap:** The SFM reports the affected-person rate as having doubled (DM-024). Doubling of a reported rate does not by itself prove failure, since reporting coverage and exposure change.
**Mains hook:** Use Target B with displacement evidence (DM-079). Qualify with the caution that reported rates depend on reporting completeness.
**Back:** *"B = Burdened: how many people are hit, not just killed."*

### Flashcard DM-017 — Sendai Target C: economic loss relative to GDP
**Front:** What does Sendai Target C require?
**Definition (learn):** Reduce direct disaster economic loss in relation to global gross domestic product by 2030 (✅ Wiki: Sendai Framework; ✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Measured relative to GDP, not absolute rupees or dollars
- Loss-reduction target; deadline 2030
- Direct loss is distinct from indirect or long-run losses
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Target C (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-018, DM-024, DM-106
**Trap:** A rise in absolute losses can coexist with falling losses relative to GDP. Check which metric a question names.
**Mains hook:** Argue that rising exposure in coastal and urban assets pushes absolute losses up even as relative losses may fall. Qualify it with the revision chart point that improved warnings may reduce deaths while economic losses rise (✅ revision chart).
**Back:** *"C = Cash: loss measured against the world's GDP, not in absolute terms."*

### Flashcard DM-018 — Sendai Target D: critical infrastructure and basic services
**Front:** What does Sendai Target D require?
**Definition (learn):** Substantially reduce disaster damage to critical infrastructure and disruption of basic services, including health and educational facilities, by developing their resilience by 2030 (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Covers both physical damage and service disruption
- Health and education named explicitly
- Deadline 2030
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Target D (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01).
**Links:** DM-014 (non-binding), DM-013, DM-124 (retrofitting), DM-116 (BMTPC)
**Trap:** Target D is a resilience-building target for infrastructure, not only a damage count. A building that is repaired after damage is not evidence of Target D resilience.
**Mains hook:** Link Target D to lifeline and hospital resilience. Qualify with the point that retrofit listing is not completion (DM-118, DM-120).
**Back:** *"D = Dependable services: schools and hospitals keep working when the shake comes."*

### Flashcard DM-019 — Sendai Target E: national and local DRR strategies (2020 deadline)
**Front:** What does Sendai Target E require, and what is its deadline?
**Definition (learn):** Substantially increase the number of countries with national and local DRR strategies by 2020 (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Means-of-implementation target (states control this directly)
- Only target with a 2020 deadline; the others run to 2030
- Target E is the most straightforward target on which a country can be held to account
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Target E, deadline 2020 (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01); reported strategies figure in DM-024.
**Links:** DM-014, DM-022, DM-024, DM-055 (NDMP 2016 as India's national strategy)
**Trap:** A 2020 deadline is not evidence of achievement. Owner 01 says Target E's deadline passed; don't say all countries achieved it.
**Mains hook:** Use Target E to show that means targets are easier to verify than loss targets. Qualify: national strategies exist on paper; local adoption is the test.
**Back:** *"E = Enact by 2020: a national and local strategy in every country, the one target already past."*

### Flashcard DM-020 — Sendai Target F: international cooperation
**Front:** What does Sendai Target F require?
**Definition (learn):** Substantially enhance international cooperation to developing countries through adequate and sustainable support by 2030 (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Means-of-implementation target
- Developing countries are the named recipients
- Deadline 2030
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Target F (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01); see topic 18 for India's cooperation architecture (✅ owner 18 cross-link).
**Links:** DM-013, DM-021, DM-022
**Trap:** International assistance supplements national and local responsibility; it does not replace it (✅ revision chart).
**Mains hook:** Use Target F to frame India's role as a provider and recipient. Qualify that cooperation must fit domestic command and accountability.
**Back:** *"F = Friends: adequate and sustained support to developing countries."*

### Flashcard DM-021 — Sendai Target G: multi-hazard early warning and risk information
**Front:** What does Sendai Target G require, and what is its operational vehicle?
**Definition (learn):** Substantially increase the availability of and access to multi-hazard early warning systems and disaster risk information and assessments by 2030 (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01). Its operational initiative is Early Warnings for All (DM-099).
**Breakdown:**
- Means-of-implementation target
- Covers both warning systems and risk information
- Deadline 2030; EW4All target 2027
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Target G (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 01); EW4All (DM-099).
**Links:** DM-099, DM-086, DM-024
**Trap:** "Availability" is not "access." Platform availability does not prove the at-risk person received and acted on a warning (✅ owner 04).
**Mains hook:** Map Target G to the warning chain of topic 04. Qualify with the reach gap for vulnerable groups.
**Back:** *"G = Get warned: systems plus risk information for everyone, by 2030."*

### Flashcard DM-022 — Sendai's internal asymmetry: loss targets (A–D) versus means targets (E–G)
**Front:** What is the structural asymmetry between Sendai's targets?
**Definition (learn):** Targets A–D measure loss outcomes that no state controls unilaterally. Targets E–G measure means of implementation, such as strategies, cooperation and warning coverage, which states do control (⚠️ owner 01 Advanced Section 3 analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- A Mourn (mortality), B Move (affected), C Money (loss vs GDP), D Mend (infrastructure and services)
- E Enact (strategies), F Ally (cooperation), G Alert (warning and risk information)
- Only E carries 2020; the measurable commitments are also the least demanding ones
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 01 Advanced Section 3 (⚠️ analytical inference from owner, not a UNDRR statement).
**Links:** DM-015 to DM-021, DM-014, DM-023
**Trap:** Do not call A–D the "hard" targets merely because they are outcome-based. Their effect depends on conditions states do not control.
**Mains hook:** Diagnose the accountability problem: the controllable targets are the easy ones, and the outcome targets are the ones that matter. Qualify that this is an interpretive reading.
**Back:** *"Mourn, Move, Money, Mend — then Enact, Ally, Alert."*

### Flashcard DM-023 — Sendai Midterm Review (17–19 May 2023) and UNGA resolution 77/289
**Front:** What was the 2023 Sendai Midterm Review, and what did it produce?
**Definition (learn):** A High-Level Meeting held 17–19 May 2023 that adopted a Political Declaration, issued as UN General Assembly resolution 77/289 on 18 May 2023. It is a renewed political commitment, not a binding correction (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 17–19 May 2023 meeting
- Output: Political Declaration; resolution 77/289 (18 May 2023)
- Significance: acknowledged the accountability deficit without a binding fix
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNGA resolution 77/289 (✅ owner 01, both basic and advanced).
**Links:** DM-014, DM-022, DM-024
**Trap:** Do not present the 2023 declaration as the latest progress data. Use the SFM status (DM-024) for current figures.
**Mains hook:** Use 2023 as the political milestone and 2026 SFM for progress. Qualify with the non-binding status.
**Back:** *"2023 is the promise renewed; 2026 is the report card."*

### Flashcard DM-024 — Sendai Framework Monitor status (24 September 2026)
**Front:** What are the current reported global Sendai figures, and what are their limits?
**Definition (learn):** UNDRR's 24 September 2026 Sendai Framework Monitor status reports 176 countries submitting data, average mortality rate down 65%, affected-person rate doubled, reported direct losses above US$1 trillion, 151 national DRR strategies, and 128 multi-hazard early warning systems (✅ owner 01; UNDRR news link cited by owner; not fetched in this build).
**Breakdown:**
- Reporting completeness varies by country, target and year
- These are reported global trends, not proof of causation or uniform national performance
- Not an India-specific achievement claim
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNDRR SFM, 24 September 2026 (✅ owner 01 citing UNDRR; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-015, DM-016, DM-019, DM-021, DM-023
**Trap:** The affected-person rate doubling is not evidence of policy failure by itself. Do not cite these numbers as India's record.
**Mains hook:** Use the SFM as the current anchor and state the qualifications. Qualify with uneven reporting.
**Back:** *"176 reporters, 65 down, twice as many touched, a trillion lost, 151 strategies, 128 warnings."*

### Flashcard DM-025 — India's ten-point DRR agenda (AMCDRR, November 2016)
**Front:** What was the PM's ten-point DRR agenda, and where was it announced?
**Definition (learn):** A ten-point agenda on DRR announced at the Asian Ministerial Conference on Disaster Risk Reduction (AMCDRR) in New Delhi, November 2016. Each point aligns with one or more Sendai priorities (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Venue: AMCDRR, New Delhi
- Date: November 2016
- Link to Sendai: each point maps onto Sendai priorities
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** AMCDRR, Nov 2016 (✅ owner 01, PDF p. 8).
**Links:** DM-013, DM-055, DM-056
**Trap:** A policy agenda, not legislation. Do not present the ten points as a statutory list.
**Mains hook:** Use the agenda as the bridge between domestic DM cycle and Sendai. Qualify that the agenda's outcomes need separate evidence.
**Back:** *"Ten points in Delhi, 2016: India's bridge from the cycle to Sendai."*

### Flashcard DM-026 — Risk-creation feedback loop (advanced)
**Front:** How does development create risk, and why must resilience answer this loop?
**Definition (learn):** Development choices (land use, construction, drainage, agriculture, industrial siting made without risk information) increase exposure and vulnerability even where hazard frequency is unchanged. The disaster then interacts with the accumulated risk, and reconstruction either recreates or reduces it (⚠️ owner 01 Advanced Section 2 analytical model).
**Breakdown:**
- Stages: development choice → risk accumulation → disaster → response and recovery choice → feedback
- Owner's example: urban flooding and landslides, both produced by development decisions
- Feedback: reconstruction can recreate the same risk or reduce it
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF pp. 29–32 (owner 01 advanced cites); owner 08 cross-link.
**Links:** DM-004, DM-006, DM-009, DM-010
**Trap:** Resilience is not only about response capacity. Ignoring the risk-creation loop makes an answer incomplete.
**Mains hook:** Argue that resilience must be built into land-use decisions before the disaster. Qualify: not all development is risk-creating, and some risk is inherited.
**Back:** *"Build first, flood later: development can manufacture the disaster it then has to answer."*

### Flashcard DM-027 — PYQ 2024 Q17 (resilience and Sendai)
**Front:** What does the 2024 GS-III Q17 demand, and how should it be routed?
**Definition (learn):** "What is disaster resilience? How is it determined? Describe various elements of a resilience framework. Also mention the global targets of Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (2015-2030)" (250 words, 15 marks; verbatim with the source's typo "targests") (✅ owner 01 and owner 00 PYQ ledger).
**Breakdown:**
- Definition → determination through hazard, exposure, vulnerability and capacity → framework elements → all seven Sendai targets (A–G, compact) → voluntary-framework and outcome caveat
- "Mention" allows compact naming of all seven
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-III 2024 Q17 (✅ owner 01; ✅ owner 00).
**Links:** DM-009, DM-006, DM-013, DM-015–DM-021, DM-022
**Trap:** Do not describe three targets at length and drop the other four. Do not omit the Target E 2020 detail.
**Mains hook:** Use the answer route above. Qualify with the 2023 political milestone and the 2026 SFM status.
**Back:** *"Define, Determine, Describe, Name all seven, Caveat."*

### Flashcard DM-028 — PYQ 2018 GS-III Q18 (DRR measures and Sendai versus Hyogo)
**Front:** What does the 2018 GS-III Q18 demand, and what is its answer route?
**Definition (learn):** Routed by owner 01 as a demand to describe DRR measures and compare the Sendai Framework with the Hyogo Framework (15 marks, 250 words) (✅ owner 01 Historical PYQ block, 2018–2023 ledger).
**Breakdown:**
- Compare Hyogo's five priorities with Sendai's four priorities plus seven targets
- Use DM Act 2005 and NPDM 2009 as pre-Sendai Indian measures
- Use NDMP 2016/2019 and the 2016 ten-point agenda as post-Sendai measures
- Do not invent numerical performance comparisons
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-III 2018 Q18 (✅ owner 01 ledger); Hyogo years (✅ Wiki).
**Links:** DM-012, DM-013, DM-030, DM-055, DM-057, DM-025
**Trap:** Presenting a before/after claim with no dated anchor. Use the dated policy anchors above.
**Mains hook:** A before/after comparison with dated anchors. Qualify that policy adoption does not prove outcomes.
**Back:** *"Hyogo's five, Sendai's four and seven; India's Act 2005 before, NDMP and Delhi 2016 after."*

### Flashcard DM-029 — PYQ 2019 GS-III Q8 (vulnerability concept)
**Front:** What does the 2019 GS-III Q8 demand?
**Definition (learn):** Routed by owner 01 as "vulnerability concept for defining disaster impacts and its types" (discuss, 10 marks, 150 words) (✅ owner 01 Historical PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Define vulnerability
- Name types: economic, physical, social, environmental, attitudinal
- Link vulnerability to who is harmed most
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-III 2019 Q8 (✅ owner 01 ledger).
**Links:** DM-004, DM-069, DM-082
**Trap:** Naming only economic or social vulnerability is an incomplete answer. Attitudinal vulnerability is a distinct category.
**Mains hook:** Use the typology to explain differential harm. Qualify: vulnerability is dynamic.
**Back:** *"Five kinds of weakness: Economic, Physical, Social, Environmental, Attitudinal."*

### GROUP — 02 Indian Legal and Institutional Architecture

### Flashcard DM-030 — Disaster Management Act, 2005 (three-tier architecture)
**Front:** What does the Disaster Management Act, 2005 create, and at what three tiers?
**Definition (learn):** The statute that institutionalised India's shift from relief-centred administration to a whole-cycle DM framework, creating the NDMA (national), SDMA (State) and DDMA (district) tiers, plus NEC, NIDM and NDRF (✅ owner 02, VisionIAS PDF p. 11).
**Breakdown:**
- NDMA: apex, Prime Minister-headed (s. 3)
- SDMA: State, Chief Minister-headed (s. 14)
- DDMA: district, Collector-headed with elected co-chair (s. 25)
- NEC (s. 8), NIDM (s. 42), NDRF (ss. 44–45)
- NDMA constituted 27 September 2006 under s. 3(1) (✅ Wiki: Disaster Management Act, 2005)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** DM Act 2005 (✅ owner 02); Act 10 of 2025 amends it (DM-045).
**Links:** DM-031, DM-033 to DM-044, DM-045, DM-053, DM-062
**Trap:** The Act is not the only current law. Read it with the 2025 and 2026 amendments (DM-053).
**Mains hook:** Show the cascade from NDMA policy to DDMA operations. Qualify that the cascade is only as strong as its district tier (DM-062).
**Back:** *"Three tiers, one cascade: N-S-D, Prime Minister, Chief Minister, Collector."*

### Flashcard DM-031 — Constitutional basis of disaster management
**Front:** Which constitutional entries underpin the DM Act, and is "disaster" in any list?
**Definition (learn):** The Constitution does not list "disaster" as a subject. The Act draws its legal basis from Concurrent List Entry 23 ("Social security and social insurance") and Entry 29 ("Prevention of the extension from one State to another of infectious or contagious diseases... affecting men, animals or plants") (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 52).
**Breakdown:**
- Derivative authority, not a dedicated entry
- Entry 29 was used during the COVID-19 lockdown discussion (owner 02)
- Concurrent List means Union and States both legislate
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Concurrent List Entries 23 and 29 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-044, DM-053
**Trap:** "Disaster management is a State subject" or "a dedicated Union List entry" are both wrong. The Constitution is silent on "disaster."
**Mains hook:** Use the concurrent basis to argue for Centre-State coordination. Qualify that the derivative basis limits how far the Union can direct States without the cooperation mechanisms (NPDRR, DM-040).
**Back:** *"No 'disaster' in the Constitution; borrow Entries 23 and 29 from the Concurrent List."*

### Flashcard DM-032 — Evolution of India's DM institutions (1990s to 2005)
**Front:** How did Indian DM move from activity-based reaction to institutionalised structure?
**Definition (learn):** A 1990s "Disaster Management Cell" under the Ministry of Agriculture, set up during the UN's International Decade for Natural Disaster Reduction, was followed by the DM Act 2005 and the three-tier NDMA-SDMA-DDMA structure (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: activity-based, reactive setup
- Stage 2: 1990s DM Cell under Ministry of Agriculture (IDNDR period, 1990–1999; ⚠️ decade span from memory, not in owner)
- Stage 3: DM Act 2005 creates the institutional cascade
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** DM Cell 1990s; DM Act 2005 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-060, DM-061, DM-066
**Trap:** The DM Cell is not the NDMA. The NDMA dates from the Act and its 27 September 2006 constitution (DM-030).
**Mains hook:** Trace the reactive-to-proactive shift. Qualify with the observation that the shift is in statute more than in district practice.
**Back:** *"From Agriculture's cell in the nineties to the Act of 2005: reactive, then institutional."*

### Flashcard DM-033 — National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA)
**Front:** What is the NDMA's composition and mandate?
**Definition (learn):** The apex body under the DM Act 2005, headed by the Prime Minister. It lays down policies, plans and guidelines, approves the National DM Plan and central ministry plans, and exercises general superintendence, direction and control over the NDRF (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 13).
**Breakdown:**
- Chair: Prime Minister (s. 3)
- Membership: not more than nine members including a Vice-Chairperson (✅ Wiki)
- Constituted 27 September 2006 under s. 3(1) (✅ Wiki)
- Guidelines "assist" ministries and States in plan-making (owner 02)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDMA under DM Act s. 3 (✅ owner 02; ✅ Wiki).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-034, DM-039, DM-056, DM-064
**Trap:** NDMA is a policy and guidance body, not the field implementer. Field action rests with the State and district tiers.
**Mains hook:** Name NDMA when the question is about policy or plan approval. Qualify that guidelines do not enforce themselves (DM-064).
**Back:** *"Nine members, one Prime Minister, one policy voice, no field boots."*

### Flashcard DM-034 — National Executive Committee (NEC)
**Front:** What is the NEC, who chairs it, and what can it do?
**Definition (learn):** A committee constituted under s. 8 of the DM Act 2005 to assist NDMA. It is chaired by the Union Home Secretary, with Secretary-level members from key ministries, and acts as the coordinating and monitoring body (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Chair: Union Home Secretary (ex officio)
- Role: coordinate and monitor
- May direct Ministries and States on measures for a specific disaster (owner 02)
- Under the 2025 amendment, retains implementation and monitoring role (ss. 10, 22; DM-050)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NEC under s. 8 (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-033, DM-035, DM-046, DM-050
**Trap:** NEC is not the same as NCMC. NCMC is chaired by the Cabinet Secretary and handles major disasters with national ramifications (DM-046).
**Mains hook:** Use NEC to show horizontal coordination across ministries. Qualify that it coordinates rather than commands.
**Back:** *"NEC = the Home Secretary's coordination table, not a command post."*

### Flashcard DM-035 — State Disaster Management Authority (SDMA)
**Front:** What is the SDMA's composition and role?
**Definition (learn):** The State-level DM authority under s. 14 of the DM Act 2005, chaired by the Chief Minister, which lays down State DM policy and approves the State DM Plan (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 14; ✅ Wiki).
**Breakdown:**
- Chair: Chief Minister
- Members: up to eight, appointed by the Chief Minister (✅ Wiki)
- Approves the State DM Plan; adapts NDMA guidelines to the State
- Maintains State database feeding national database under s. 18(2)(p) (DM-049)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** SDMA under s. 14 (✅ owner 02; ✅ Wiki).
**Links:** DM-036, DM-037, DM-033, DM-049, DM-062
**Trap:** The SDMA is a policy body; the SEC coordinates operations. Do not give the SDMA the Chief Secretary role.
**Mains hook:** Use SDMA as the State adaptation layer. Qualify with the State capacity gap (DM-062).
**Back:** *"Chief Minister chairs, the State plans, the SEC acts."*

### Flashcard DM-036 — State Executive Committee (SEC)
**Front:** Who chairs the State Executive Committee, and what does it do?
**Definition (learn):** The State-level coordinating and monitoring body, chaired by the Chief Secretary, which coordinates and monitors DM activities in the State (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 14).
**Breakdown:**
- Chair: Chief Secretary
- Role: coordinate and monitor DM activity
- Under the 2025 amendment, retains implementation/monitoring role (s. 22; DM-050)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** SEC (✅ owner 02); s. 22 (✅ owner 02 Section 9).
**Links:** DM-034, DM-035, DM-037
**Trap:** SEC is not the SDMA. The SDMA is CM-chaired policy body; the SEC is the Chief Secretary's operational coordinator.
**Mains hook:** Use SEC to show State-level coordination. Qualify with its dependence on the Chief Secretary's bandwidth.
**Back:** *"Chief Secretary runs the State table; the Chief Minister writes the plan."*

### Flashcard DM-037 — District Disaster Management Authority (DDMA)
**Front:** Who heads the DDMA, and what does it do?
**Definition (learn):** The district-level authority under s. 25 of the DM Act 2005, headed by the District Collector/Magistrate, with an elected local-authority representative as ex officio Co-Chairperson. It prepares the District DM Plan and ensures NDMA and SDMA guidelines are followed locally (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 14; ✅ Wiki).
**Breakdown:**
- Chair: District Collector/DM/Deputy Commissioner
- Co-chair: elected representative of the area (ex officio)
- Prepares the district plan; operational nodal point near the affected population
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** DDMA under s. 25 (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-035, DM-037, DM-062, DM-065, DM-003
**Trap:** The elected co-chair is a formal co-chair, not the head. The Collector remains the principal operator (owner 02 Advanced Section 6).
**Mains hook:** Name DDMA as the operational nodal point. Qualify with the district capacity gap and the district-fund exception (DM-065).
**Back:** *"Collector heads, elected co-chair shares, district plan delivers."*

### Flashcard DM-038 — National Institute of Disaster Management (NIDM)
**Front:** What is NIDM, under which section, and does the 2025 amendment abolish it?
**Definition (learn):** The nodal agency for human-resource development, capacity building, training, research, documentation and policy advocacy, working within NDMA's policy framework and supporting States through DM Centres (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 13; s. 42 per ✅ Wiki).
**Breakdown:**
- Established under s. 42 of the DM Act 2005
- Role: training, research, capacity building
- Survives the 2025 amendment: s. 42 is retained; s. 43 is substituted to allow NIDM to recruit experts (⚠️ owner 02)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NIDM under s. 42; s. 43 substituted in 2025 (✅ owner 02; ✅ Wiki).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-033, DM-049
**Trap:** NIDM is not abolished by the 2025 amendment, despite some commentary. Do not state otherwise.
**Mains hook:** Use NIDM as the capacity-development pillar. Qualify with the caution that training capacity needs funding and local uptake.
**Back:** *"NIDM teaches and researches; it survived 2025 with its s. 42 intact."*

### Flashcard DM-039 — National Disaster Response Force (NDRF, the force)
**Front:** What is the NDRF force, who controls it, and what is its strength?
**Definition (learn):** The specialist response force under ss. 44–45 of the DM Act 2005, for specialist response to a threatening disaster situation or disaster. It is headed by a Director General appointed by the Central Government, and NDMA exercises general superintendence, direction and control (✅ Wiki; ✅ owner 02, PDF p. 13).
**Breakdown:**
- Legal basis: ss. 44–45
- Head: Director General (Central Government appointee)
- Parent ministry: MHA (✅ Wiki)
- Strength: MHA reported 16 operational battalions and sanctioned strength 18,581 on 22 July 2025 (⚠️ owner 02; Lok Sabha reply not re-fetched)
- Personnel from central armed police forces on deputation; the battalion breakdown varies between sources (⚠️ Wiki list vs. table)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDRF formed 2006 per Wiki infobox (⚠️ one passage says 2005); MHA 22 July 2025 (⚠️ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-033, DM-041, DM-044, DM-048
**Trap:** The VisionIAS figure of "12 battalions" is document-period. Cite 16 battalions and 18,581 sanctioned (DM-039, owner 02). Do not confuse the force with the NDRF fund (DM-041).
**Mains hook:** Argue that NDRF is centrally raised and deployed, so the State tier also needs its own professional response force (DM-048). Qualify that size alone is not capacity.
**Back:** *"Sixteen battalions, one Director General, one name shared with a fund — keep the force and the fund apart."*

### Flashcard DM-040 — National Platform for DRR (NPDRR)
**Front:** What is the NPDRR, who chairs it, and what is its power?
**Definition (learn):** A multi-stakeholder body chaired by the Union Home Minister that reviews implementation of DRR policy and advises on Centre-State coordination. It is advisory, not directive (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 12).
**Breakdown:**
- Chair: Union Home Minister
- Members: State ministers, select mayors and MPs (owner 02 advanced)
- Role: review progress and advise on coordination
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NPDRR (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 12).
**Links:** DM-031, DM-033, DM-064
**Trap:** NPDRR does not bind States. Advisory coordination is different from the NDMA's statutory powers.
**Mains hook:** Use NPDRR to show convening power without compliance power. Qualify that federal coordination depends on political will.
**Back:** *"The Home Minister convenes; he cannot command."*

### Flashcard DM-041 — National Disaster Response Fund (s. 46)
**Front:** What is the National Disaster Response Fund, and under which section?
**Definition (learn):** The Central fund under s. 46 of the DM Act 2005, managed by the Centre to meet emergency response, relief and rehabilitation expenses beyond State Disaster Response Fund capacity. The earlier National Calamity Contingency Fund was merged into it (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 15; s. 46 ✅ owner 02 Section 5).
**Breakdown:**
- Section: 46 (owner 02)
- Purpose: response, relief and rehabilitation (post-event)
- Merged the National Calamity Contingency Fund
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** s. 46 (✅ owner 02); NCCF merger (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-039, DM-042, DM-043, DM-044, DM-046
**Trap:** The NDRF fund is post-event relief finance. It is not mitigation finance (DM-042). It is also not the NDRF force.
**Mains hook:** Route relief finance to the correct fund. Qualify with the revision chart point: relief compensation is not ex-ante risk finance.
**Back:** *"Section 46 pays after the disaster; NDRF-the-fund is relief money, not the force."*

### Flashcard DM-042 — National Disaster Mitigation Fund (s. 47)
**Front:** What is the National Disaster Mitigation Fund, and when did its operative provision commence?
**Definition (learn):** The Central mitigation fund under s. 47 of the DM Act 2005 for ex-ante risk reduction (✅ owner 02). Section 47(1) commenced on 5 February 2021 (⚠️ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Section: 47; approval routed through the High Level Committee (s. 8B; ✅ owner 02)
- Purpose: prospective risk reduction
- Commenced later than the relief fund (s. 46)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** s. 47; commencement 5 February 2021 (⚠️ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-041, DM-043, DM-046, DM-058, DM-059
**Trap:** The mitigation fund is not a relief fund. Relief/response finances immediate needs; mitigation reduces prospective risk (✅ owner 00).
**Mains hook:** Argue that mitigation finance is the ex-ante instrument needed to break the risk-creation loop (DM-026). Qualify that its use needs its own status source.
**Back:** *"Section 47 saves before the storm; Section 46 pays after it."*

### Flashcard DM-043 — State and District Disaster Response and Mitigation Funds (s. 48)
**Front:** Which funds does s. 48 create, and when did it commence?
**Definition (learn):** Section 48 of the DM Act 2005 provides for the State Disaster Response Fund (SDRF), State Disaster Mitigation Fund, District Disaster Response Fund and District Disaster Mitigation Fund (✅ owner 02). Section 48 commenced on 1 August 2007 (⚠️ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Four instruments: State response, State mitigation, District response, District mitigation
- SDRF finances immediate relief in the State (distinct from SDRF force in s. 44A, DM-044)
- Fifteenth/Sixteenth FC disaster awards fund the SDRF (DM-059)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** s. 48, 1 August 2007 (⚠️ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-041, DM-042, DM-044, DM-059, DM-065
**Trap:** SDRF is a fund; the State Disaster Response Force (s. 44A) is a force. Two instruments share an acronym (DM-044).
**Mains hook:** Use s. 48 to show the district tier has a statutory fund basis. Qualify with the district-level fund gap (DM-065).
**Back:** *"Section 48: State and District, response and mitigation — four funds, one section."*

### Flashcard DM-044 — Trap: SDRF fund versus State Disaster Response Force
**Front:** Does "SDRF" in the DM Act refer to a fund or a force?
**Definition (learn):** Both exist. The SDRF under s. 48 is the State Disaster Response Fund (money). The State Disaster Response Force under s. 44A is a specialist State force (personnel) (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- SDRF (fund): s. 48; finances relief
- State Disaster Response Force: s. 44A; enabling power for a specialist force
- Two different instruments sharing an acronym
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** ss. 44A and 48 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-043, DM-048, DM-039
**Trap:** Saying "the SDRF is a force" or "the State Disaster Response Force is the SDRF fund" is a standing examiner trap.
**Mains hook:** Correct the naming first, then argue. Qualify that the two instruments serve different functions.
**Back:** *"SDRF pays for relief; the State Disaster Response Force responds. Fund, then force."*

### Flashcard DM-045 — Disaster Management (Amendment) Act, 2025: timeline
**Front:** What is the 2025 amendment's Act number and timeline?
**Definition (learn):** The Disaster Management (Amendment) Act, 2025 (Act 10 of 2025), amended the DM Act 2005. Introduced in the Lok Sabha on 1 August 2024 by MoS Home Nityanand Rai; passed by Lok Sabha on 12 December 2024 and Rajya Sabha on 25 March 2025; published in the Gazette on 29 March 2025 (✅ Wiki: Disaster Management Act, 2005).
**Breakdown:**
- Commencement: in force from 9 April 2025 under S.O. 1648(E), 8 April 2025 (✅ owner 02, cites official commencement notification)
- Key inserted provisions: ss. 8A, 8B, 41A, 44A, 60A, 76A (DM-046 to DM-048, DM-050)
- Definition of disaster now excludes law-and-order situations (owner 02)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Bill 1 Aug 2024; LS 12 Dec 2024; RS 25 Mar 2025; Gazette 29 Mar 2025; in force 9 Apr 2025 (✅ Wiki; S.O. 1648(E) ✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-046, DM-047, DM-048, DM-049, DM-050, DM-051, DM-052, DM-053
**Trap:** Gazetted (29 March) is not the same as in force (9 April). Use the commencement date for operative law.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish statute from implementation. Qualify that commencement is settled, but local operationalisation is not (DM-063).
**Back:** *"Bill in August, Parliament in March, Gazette the 29th, in force 9 April — the 2025 timeline."*

### Flashcard DM-046 — National Crisis Management Committee (s. 8A) and High Level Committee (s. 8B)
**Front:** What are the NCMC and HLC under the 2025 amendment?
**Definition (learn):** The NCMC under s. 8A is the nodal body, chaired by the Cabinet Secretary, to deal with major disasters with serious or national ramifications. The HLC under s. 8B provides financial assistance to States under s. 46 and approves mitigation assistance under s. 47 (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 13).
**Breakdown:**
- NCMC: Cabinet Secretary chair; disaster coordination not law-and-order
- HLC: financial assistance (s. 46) and mitigation assistance (s. 47)
- Both existed administratively before 2025; the Act converts practice into statute (✅ owner 02 Advanced)
- Parallel escalation channel: CCS engaged where security implications exist (✅ owner 02)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** ss. 8A, 8B (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-034, DM-041, DM-042, DM-045, DM-063
**Trap:** NCMC is not a security body. The CCS handles disasters with security implications; NCMC coordinates major disasters.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the 2025 law strengthens accountability at the top of the cascade. Qualify that the lower tiers remain discretionary (DM-062).
**Back:** *"8A Cabinet Secretary coordinates the storm; 8B High Level Committee signs the cheque."*

### Flashcard DM-047 — Urban Disaster Management Authority (s. 41A)
**Front:** What does s. 41A allow, and who chairs a UDMA?
**Definition (learn):** A State Government may, by Gazette notification, constitute a UDMA for its State capital and every city with a Municipal Corporation, except the NCT of Delhi and UT of Chandigarh. It prepares the Urban Plan, approved by the SDMA (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 13).
**Breakdown:**
- Enabling power: "may", not "shall"
- Chair: Municipal Commissioner (ex officio); Vice-Chair: District Collector (ex officio)
- Excludes Delhi and Chandigarh
- MHA reported Karnataka's BBMP UDMA on 11 February 2026 (⚠️ owner 02; parliamentary reply not re-fetched)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** s. 41A (✅ owner 02); Karnataka UDMA 11 Feb 2026 (⚠️ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-045, DM-048, DM-063, DM-062
**Trap:** Every State capital has a UDMA because s. 41A is in force. Wrong. It is enabling; each UDMA needs a dated State notification.
**Mains hook:** Use s. 41A to show urban DRR governance is still discretionary. Qualify with the Karnataka example as a beginning, not a national roll-out.
**Back:** *"Section 41A says may: the Municipal Commissioner chairs, the Collector co-chairs, Delhi and Chandigarh excluded."*

### Flashcard DM-048 — State Disaster Response Force (s. 44A)
**Front:** What does s. 44A allow?
**Definition (learn):** A State Government may, by Gazette notification, constitute a specialist State Disaster Response Force and prescribe its functions and terms of service (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 13 on NDRF context).
**Breakdown:**
- Enabling power, not a mandate
- A specialist force, not a fund
- Owner 02: the absence of an equally professional State tier beneath NDRF is the real constraint
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** s. 44A (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-039, DM-044, DM-063
**Trap:** Do not confuse with the SDRF fund (DM-044). Do not assume every State has a constituted State force.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the State force gap, not the NDRF size, limits response. Qualify that a State may legitimately choose its own model.
**Back:** *"A State may raise a force of its own — the power is enabling, not automatic."*

### Flashcard DM-049 — National disaster database
**Front:** What is the disaster database, and which sections create the duty?
**Definition (learn):** A new definition of "disaster database" in s. 2(da) covers disaster assessment, fund allocation and expenditure, preparedness and mitigation plans, and a risk register. NDMA must create the national database (s. 6(2)(v)) and SDMAs must maintain State databases that feed into it (s. 18(2)(p)) (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Definition: s. 2(da)
- National duty: s. 6(2)(v) (NDMA "create")
- State duty: s. 18(2)(p)
- Statutory verb is "create", not "publish", so public disclosure is not guaranteed
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** ss. 2(da), 6(2)(v), 18(2)(p) (✅ owner 02); MHA restated it 11 February 2026 (⚠️ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-035, DM-045, DM-050, DM-065
**Trap:** Do not confuse with the NDMA's 2012 National Disaster Management Information and Communication System guideline (✅ owner 02 Advanced).
**Mains hook:** Argue that plans cannot set measurable targets without standard loss and expenditure data. Qualify that the rollout status needs verification.
**Back:** *"Two-dot-da defines it, six-two-v makes NDMA create it, eighteen-two-p makes the State feed it."*

### Flashcard DM-050 — Other 2025 amendment provisions (plans, nodal departments, penalties)
**Front:** Which other provisions did the 2025 amendment add or change?
**Definition (learn):** Plan preparation moved to NDMA and SDMA, with the National and State Plans to be reviewed every three years and updated at least every five years (ss. 11, 23). NEC and SEC keep implementation and monitoring roles (ss. 10, 22). Hazard-wise nodal departments (ss. 35, 38). NDMA regulation power (s. 76A). Hazard-action orders with a penalty up to ₹10,000 (s. 60A). Substituted ss. 5 and 43 (✅ owner 02, Section 9).
**Breakdown:**
- Plans: ss. 11, 23 (3-year review, 5-year update)
- Monitoring: ss. 10, 22
- Nodal departments: ss. 35, 38
- Penalty: s. 60A (up to ₹10,000)
- Regulations: s. 76A
- Staffing: substituted s. 5
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** ss. 5, 10, 11, 22, 23, 35, 38, 43, 60A, 76A (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-034, DM-036, DM-038, DM-045, DM-049
**Trap:** The 2025 amendment did not remove NIDM (DM-038). It did not create a national penalty regime beyond the enacted sections.
**Mains hook:** Use the plan-review cycle to argue for measurable accountability. Qualify that the statute mandates review but not public reporting.
**Back:** *"Plans every three, updated every five; nodal departments per hazard; ten thousand for hazard-action defiance."*

### Flashcard DM-051 — Trap: "Section 9B" does not exist
**Front:** Is there a Section 9B in the DM Act 2005 as amended?
**Definition (learn):** No. The provisions inserted by the 2025 amendment are ss. 8A, 8B, 41A, 44A, 60A and 76A, plus substituted ss. 5 and 43 and the new definition s. 2(da) (✅ owner 02, Section 8).
**Breakdown:**
- No s. 9B in the Act
- Correct citations: ss. 8A, 8B, 41A, 44A, 60A, 76A
- Database duties: ss. 2(da), 6(2)(v), 18(2)(p)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 02 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-045, DM-046, DM-047, DM-048, DM-050
**Trap:** Citing a non-existent section is a credibility loss in both Prelims and Mains.
**Mains hook:** Cite the correct sections with dates, as a discipline check. Qualify that statutory citations are the first check, not the whole answer.
**Back:** *"No 9B: eight-A, eight-B, forty-one-A, forty-four-A, sixty-A, seventy-six-A."*

### Flashcard DM-052 — Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Act, 2026
**Front:** What did the Jan Vishwas Act 2026 change in the DM Act?
**Definition (learn):** The Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Act, 2026 (Act 8 of 2026), Schedule serial 60, reduced only the maximum imprisonment terms in DM Act ss. 51–53 (✅ owner 02, Gazette p. 126). S.O. 2181(E), 30 April 2026, commenced the changes on 15 May 2026 (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- s. 51: one year becomes six months; aggravated two-year term becomes one year
- s. 52: two years becomes six months
- s. 53: two years becomes one year
- No change to NDMA, NCMC, HLC, UDMA, NDRF or plan/database architecture
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Act 8 of 2026; Schedule serial 60; S.O. 2181(E) of 30 April 2026; in force 15 May 2026 (✅ owner 02; ⚠️ official PDFs not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-045, DM-053, DM-050
**Trap:** Do not describe this as a major 2026 overhaul or as creating an administrative penalty system. Its change is limited to imprisonment terms.
**Mains hook:** Separate the 2025 institutional change from the 2026 penalty change. Qualify that the amendments do not prove enforcement.
**Back:** *"Jan Vishwas trimmed the jail terms in sections 51 to 53, nothing else — 15 May 2026."*

### Flashcard DM-053 — Current legal stack (law as at 5 October 2026)
**Front:** What is the correct current legal position of the DM framework?
**Definition (learn):** The operative law is the DM Act 2005 as amended by the DM (Amendment) Act 2025 (Act 10 of 2025, in force 9 April 2025) and by the Jan Vishwas Act 2026 (Act 8 of 2026; ss. 51–53 penalties, in force 15 May 2026). Owner 02 treats 5 October 2026 as its current-law recheck date (✅ owner 02; ✅ owner 00).
**Breakdown:**
- Original 2005 text: base
- 2025 amendment: institutional architecture
- 2026 amendment: penalty terms only
- Implementation actions still need their own notifications
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** DM Act 2005; Act 10 of 2025; Act 8 of 2026 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-045, DM-052, DM-063
**Trap:** Citing either the 2005 text or the 2025 amendment alone as the complete current law is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Begin an answer with the correct legal stack. Qualify with the distinction between statutory commencement and operational rollout.
**Back:** *"Base 2005, institutions 2025, jail terms 2026 — read all three layers."*

### Flashcard DM-054 — Incident Response System (IRS)
**Front:** What is the Incident Response System, and what roles does it define?
**Definition (learn):** NDMA's National Disaster Management Guidelines on the Incident Response System, providing a standard operational organisation for an incident. The Responsible Officer activates and oversees the arrangement; the Incident Commander directs field operations, supported by Operations, Planning, Logistics and, where required, Finance/Administration sections (✅ owner 02; official IRS guideline cited by owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Responsible Officer: activates and oversees
- Incident Commander: field direction
- Sections: Operations, Planning, Logistics, Finance/Administration
- IRS is operational doctrine, not a new statutory tier (✅ owner 02)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDMA IRS guideline (✅ owner 02; not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-010, DM-033, DM-046
**Trap:** IRS is not another NDMA-SDMA-DDMA tier. It does not displace statutory powers. Do not call it a statute.
**Mains hook:** Use IRS for response coordination. Qualify that pre-designation must be exercised in drills to be real.
**Back:** *"Responsible Officer sets the system, Incident Commander runs the field: O-P-L-F."*

### Flashcard DM-055 — National Disaster Management Plan 2016 (first edition)
**Front:** What is NDMP 2016, what are its five thematic areas, and what are its self-acknowledged gaps?
**Definition (learn):** India's first National Disaster Management Plan (2016), aligned with Sendai, the SDGs (2015–2030) and the Paris Agreement (COP-21). It organises action into five Thematic Areas: Understanding Risk; Inter-Agency Coordination; Investing in DRR (structural); Investing in DRR (non-structural); Capacity Development (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 14).
**Breakdown:**
- Response section: 18 broad activities in a role matrix down to Panchayat/ULB level (✅ owner 02)
- Gaps: too generic; no precise time frame; no fund projection; no goals or targets (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 18)
- Contrast: unlike Sendai and SDGs, no own targets
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDMP 2016 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-056, DM-013, DM-025, DM-062
**Trap:** NDMP 2016 is not the current plan. The revised NDMP 2019 is the latest edition (DM-056).
**Mains hook:** Use the NDMP 2016 gaps to argue for measurable, time-bound, financed targets. Qualify with the need to check whether the 2019 revision addressed them.
**Back:** *"Five thematic areas, eighteen response activities, no targets — the first plan lacked a scoreboard."*

### Flashcard DM-056 — Revised National Disaster Management Plan, 2019
**Front:** Which National Disaster Management Plan is the latest published?
**Definition (learn):** The revised NDMP 2019 is the latest published National Disaster Management Plan and supersedes the 2016 first edition (✅ owner 02, owner 01 and owner 00).
**Breakdown:**
- Latest published edition: 2019 revision
- Cite 2019 as operative NDMP content
- Whether 2019 addressed 2016 gaps needs verification (⚠️ owner 02)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDMP 2019 (✅ owner 02; ✅ owner 00 Section 8).
**Links:** DM-055, DM-057, DM-013
**Trap:** Citing NDMP 2016 as current is one of the most repeated stale claims in the folder.
**Mains hook:** Use NDMP 2019 as the operative plan and test it for the same accountability gaps. Qualify that the 2019 text, not the 2016 critique, must be checked.
**Back:** *"Two thousand nineteen is the latest; sixteen was the first draft."*

### Flashcard DM-057 — National Policy on Disaster Management, 2009 (NPDM)
**Front:** What is the NPDM 2009's vision?
**Definition (learn):** The 2009 national policy whose vision is "to build a safe and disaster resilient India... through a culture of prevention, mitigation, preparedness and response" (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 15).
**Breakdown:**
- Pre-Sendai national policy
- Vision: safe, disaster-resilient India
- Culture of prevention, mitigation, preparedness and response
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NPDM 2009 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-055, DM-028
**Trap:** NPDM is a policy, not a statute. The DM Act is the statute.
**Mains hook:** Use NPDM as the pre-Sendai Indian anchor. Qualify that a policy vision does not itself reduce risk (DM-064).
**Back:** *"2009: a safe, resilient India through a culture of prevention, not a culture of relief."*

### Flashcard DM-058 — Standing Committee on Finance report, 2019
**Front:** What did the 2019 Standing Committee on Finance recommend on disaster finance?
**Definition (learn):** The 2019 report on "Central Assistance for Disaster Management and Relief," chaired by Dr. M. Veerappa Moily, recommended enhanced SDRF/NDRF assistance scales; a separate Disaster Mitigation Fund; earmarking 10% of centrally sponsored scheme allocations for permanent restoration; and a 15% annual SDRF corpus increase for 2020–25 (✅ owner 02, PDF p. 15).
**Breakdown:**
- Recommendation 1: enhanced assistance scales
- Recommendation 2: separate mitigation fund
- Recommendation 3: 10% earmark for permanent restoration
- Recommendation 4: 15% annual SDRF corpus increase (2020–25)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Standing Committee on Finance, 2019 (✅ owner 02). Document-period recommendations.
**Links:** DM-042, DM-043, DM-059
**Trap:** The 2019 recommendations are document-period. Cite the Finance Commission awards for current fund levels, not this report.
**Mains hook:** Use the 2019 report as evidence of a known funding gap. Qualify that the core recommendation was later acted on through FC awards (DM-059).
**Back:** *"Ten percent for restoration, fifteen percent for the corpus, a mitigation fund — 2019's wish list, since partly adopted."*

### Flashcard DM-059 — Fifteenth and Sixteenth Finance Commission disaster awards
**Front:** What do the 15th and 16th Finance Commissions provide for disaster funds?
**Definition (learn):** The Fifteenth FC (2021–26) created National and State Disaster Risk Management Funds, each split 80:20 between response and mitigation. The Sixteenth FC (report submitted 17 November 2025, award period 2026–31) kept the 80:20 State split and introduced a Disaster Risk Index weighting (✅ owner 02, Section 9). The Sixteenth FC reported on 1 February 2026 that Union and State funds are constituted, with district-level funds the exception (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- 15th FC (2021–26): 80:20 response:mitigation
- 16th FC (2026–31): 80:20 State split; Disaster Risk Index
- District-level funds: the exception (owner 02)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 15th FC (2021–26); 16th FC report 17 November 2025; award 2026–31 (✅ owner 02; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-043, DM-058, DM-065
**Trap:** The 16th FC award is a fiscal transfer, not an NDMA policy. Do not cite the 2019 report for current fund levels.
**Mains hook:** Use the 80:20 split to argue for the mitigation shift. Qualify with the district gap and the need for fund-use verification.
**Back:** *"Fifteenth split it eighty-twenty; Sixteenth kept it and added an index; districts are still the exception."*

### Flashcard DM-060 — J. C. Pant High-Powered Committee (1999–2001)
**Front:** What did the J. C. Pant High-Powered Committee do?
**Definition (learn):** The High-Powered Committee on Disaster Management, chaired by J. C. Pant (1999–2001), tasked with developing national, State and district plans covering natural and human-induced hazards. It built the all-hazard classification and multi-level planning framework before the DM Act (⚠️ repo `_revision/Major-Committees-Commissions-and-Reports.md`; not web-verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Period: 1999–2001
- Mandate: national, State and district plans
- Contribution: all-hazard classification and multi-level planning
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** HPC on DM (⚠️ revision file).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-061, DM-032
**Trap:** The HPC is pre-Act; the Second ARC is post-Act (DM-061).
**Mains hook:** Trace the institutional lineage from the HPC to the Act. Qualify that its recommendations need separate verification.
**Back:** *"Pant's panel drew the all-hazard map before the Act."*

### Flashcard DM-061 — Second ARC, "Crisis Management: From Despair to Hope" (2006)
**Front:** What did the Second Administrative Reforms Commission report on crisis management recommend?
**Definition (learn):** A 2006 report, titled "Crisis Management: From Despair to Hope," reviewing prevention, mitigation, response, recovery and governmental coordination. It recommended local plans, incident-command systems, professional response, community capacity and development-linked mitigation (⚠️ repo `_revision` file; report number not verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 2006
- Themes: local plans, incident command, professional response, community capacity
- Links development to mitigation
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2006 (⚠️ revision file).
**Links:** DM-054, DM-060, DM-032
**Trap:** The Second ARC evaluated governance after the DM Act; it did not create the Act.
**Mains hook:** Use the ARC to show that governance reform preceded the 2025 statute. Qualify that the report's recommendations need dated verification.
**Back:** *"Despair in 2006, hope through local plans and incident command."*

### Flashcard DM-062 — Advanced: district-tier implementation as the weakest link
**Front:** Where does India's DM cascade most often fail, according to the advanced analysis?
**Definition (learn):** India's DM cascade (NDMA to SDMA to DDMA) is effective only as strong as its weakest tier, usually the district. A generic national plan can become operationally non-binding at the district tier where implementation must occur (⚠️ owner 02 Advanced Sections 1–2 analytical thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: national policy and plans
- Stage 2: State adaptation
- Stage 3: district implementation (DDMA, municipal bodies, PRIs)
- Outcome depends on the weakest tier
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 02 Advanced (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-037, DM-047, DM-065, DM-055
**Trap:** Do not present the NDMA-SDMA-DDMA organogram as proof of coordination.
**Mains hook:** Diagnose the district capacity gap with evidence. Qualify with the observation that the district-tier evidence is thinner in the source material.
**Back:** *"The cascade is only as strong as the district link."*

### Flashcard DM-063 — Advanced: enabling power versus existing body
**Front:** How should a candidate treat "may constitute" provisions (ss. 41A and 44A)?
**Definition (learn):** These are enabling provisions. Their existence in law does not mean a body exists. A named authority needs a dated State notification or an official Parliament reply (✅ owner 02 Sections 3 and 10; ✅ owner 00 Section 8 gap).
**Breakdown:**
- Statutory mandate: law permits
- Operational fact: a notification and an operating body exist
- Commencement of the Act is settled; implementation of its permissions is not
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** ss. 41A, 44A (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-047, DM-048, DM-053
**Trap:** Presenting "may constitute" as "has constituted" is the enabling-power gap.
**Mains hook:** Separate law from implementation. Qualify with the Karnataka example.
**Back:** *"May is not is: enabling powers need notifications before they become institutions."*

### Flashcard DM-064 — Advanced: NDMA guidance versus enforcement power
**Front:** Does NDMA enforce its guidelines?
**Definition (learn):** NDMA lays down policies, plans and guidelines, which "assist" ministries and States in making their own plans. Implementation power rests with State and district tiers. This advisory-plus-approval role explains uneven outcomes across States (✅ owner 02 Advanced Section 7).
**Breakdown:**
- NDMA: guidance and approval
- State/district: implementation
- Same guideline, different ground outcomes
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 02 Advanced (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-033, DM-040, DM-062
**Trap:** A guideline is not compliance. Do not equate an NDMA guideline with binding action.
**Mains hook:** Use the guidance-versus-enforcement distinction to explain uneven State outcomes. Qualify with the need for State-specific evidence.
**Back:** *"NDMA writes the guide; the States drive the car."*

### Flashcard DM-065 — Advanced: district fund gap
**Front:** Is the district tier unfunded under the DM Act?
**Definition (learn):** No. Section 48 expressly contemplates District Disaster Response and Mitigation Funds. The gap is implementation: the Sixteenth FC reported that district-level funds are the exception (✅ owner 02 Advanced Section 10).
**Breakdown:**
- Drafting gap: none (s. 48 exists)
- Implementation gap: district funds not widely constituted (owner 02)
- Fiscal-institutional mismatch: district bears operational role without a dedicated corpus (owner 02 Advanced Section 8)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** s. 48 (✅ owner 02); Sixteenth FC (1 February 2026, ✅ owner 02).
**Links:** DM-043, DM-059, DM-062
**Trap:** Do not say the district is "fiscally unprovided for in law." That is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Argue that funding the district tier is an implementation problem. Qualify that the status should be checked against a dated FC or MHA source.
**Back:** *"The law provides the district fund; the ground has not yet filled it."*

### Flashcard DM-066 — PYQ 2020 GS-III Q18 (proactive disaster management)
**Front:** What does the 2020 GS-III Q18 demand?
**Definition (learn):** Routed by owner 02 as "government proactive disaster management approach replacing earlier reactive strategy" (discuss, 15 marks, 250 words) (✅ owner 02 Historical PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: India has moved beyond a relief-only model
- Evidence sequence: DM Act tiers → NDMP 2019/Sendai → NDRF pre-positioning → mitigation funds and BBB
- Counterweight: a plan or force is not district execution (owner 02, Section 13.2)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-III 2020 Q18 (✅ owner 02 ledger).
**Links:** DM-030, DM-032, DM-055, DM-056, DM-041
**Trap:** Describing reform as achieved outcome rather than institutional design.
**Mains hook:** Make the answer a verdict on credibility. Qualify that proactivity is credible only when local plans, finance and drills convert the framework into outcomes.
**Back:** *"Relief-only was the old script; the new script needs districts that act."*

### Flashcard DM-067 — PYQ 2020 GS-II Q11 (centralisation and emergency legislation)
**Front:** What does the 2020 GS-II Q11 demand?
**Definition (learn):** Routed by owner 02 as a cross-cutting demand on "Centralising tendencies and emergency legislation and the Farm Acts" (elucidate, 15 marks, 250 words); federal balance and disaster law both named in the stem (✅ owner 02 Historical PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Cross-cutting: federalism and disaster law
- Use DM Act's Concurrent-List basis (DM-031)
- Compare the Centre's emergency powers with State autonomy
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-II 2020 Q11 (✅ owner 02 ledger).
**Links:** DM-031, DM-040, DM-044
**Trap:** Do not treat disaster law as only a GS-III topic. This is a federal-balance question.
**Mains hook:** Use the Concurrent-List basis to discuss emergency legislation and federal balance. Qualify with the need for State consent in coordination.
**Back:** *"Centralising tendencies meet disaster law at the Concurrent List."*

### GROUP — 03 Community-Based DRR and Inclusive Protection

### Flashcard DM-068 — Community as first responder (before, during, after roles)
**Front:** What roles does the community play before, during and after a disaster?
**Definition (learn):** Communities are the "repository of knowledge and skills" and "the first line of responders." Disaster management works only if communities participate and are given specific roles (✅ owner 03, VisionIAS PDF p. 65).
**Breakdown:**
- Before: awareness, training, mock drills, community safety plans
- During: search and rescue, warning relay, mutual aid
- After: damage assessment, relief distribution, psycho-social support, recovery
- Inclusive lens across all three stages (owner 03)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 65 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-069, DM-070, DM-078, DM-081, DM-086
**Trap:** "Community participation" is not a generic good unless specific roles are named (DM-081).
