**Mains hook:** Name the group, the role and the stage. Qualify that communities need training, equipment and institutional backing.
**Back:** *"Before, During, After — three named roles; never a vague 'participation'."*

### Flashcard DM-069 — Social vulnerability groups
**Front:** Which groups does the source name as socially vulnerable?
**Definition (learn):** The potential impact of events on groups such as the poor, pregnant or lactating women, the disabled, children and the elderly (✅ owner 03, PDF pp. 4–5).
**Breakdown:**
- Poor households
- Pregnant or lactating women
- Persons with disabilities
- Children
- Elderly persons (may be unable to self-evacuate in floods)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF pp. 4–5 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-004, DM-068, DM-082, DM-074
**Trap:** A "vulnerable section" phrase is too generic. Name the group and the mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Disaggregate the vulnerable population for evacuation and relief. Qualify that other groups (migrants, tribal, homeless) are present in the revision chart.
**Back:** *"P-P-D-C-E: poor, pregnant or lactating, disabled, children, elderly."*

### Flashcard DM-070 — Aapda Mitra pilot (2016)
**Front:** What was the pilot phase of Aapda Mitra?
**Definition (learn):** An NDMA-approved Centrally Sponsored Scheme that trains community volunteers for basic relief and rescue in floods, flash floods and urban flooding in the 30 most flood-prone districts of 25 States (✅ owner 03, PDF p. 29). Launched in May 2016 with a 6,000-volunteer target; NDMA records 5,186 volunteers trained across 23 project States (📰 owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Coverage: 30 flood-prone districts, 25 States
- Target: 6,000 volunteers
- Achieved (NDMA): 5,186 across 23 project States
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Launched May 2016 (📰 owner 03; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-071, DM-072, DM-073, DM-083, DM-085
**Trap:** Do not cite the 30-district figure as current scope. It is the 2016 pilot.
**Mains hook:** Use the pilot as a capacity investment, not an outcome proof. Qualify with the flood-only scope limitation (DM-085).
**Back:** *"Pilot 2016: thirty districts, twenty-five States, six thousand target, five thousand one hundred and eighty-six trained."*

### Flashcard DM-071 — Aapda Mitra expanded scheme (1,00,000 volunteers in 350 districts)
**Front:** What is the expanded Aapda Mitra target, and what did MHA report?
**Definition (learn):** The expanded scheme targets 1,00,000 volunteers across 350 vulnerable districts. MHA reported around one lakh volunteers trained in 350 districts on 25 July 2024 (📰 owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: 1,00,000 volunteers
- Districts: 350 vulnerable districts
- Reported: about one lakh trained (MHA, 25 July 2024)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MHA statement 25 July 2024 (📰 owner 03; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-070, DM-072, DM-083
**Trap:** Cite the pilot and the expanded scheme separately. Conflating them gives a wrong district count.
**Mains hook:** Argue that headcount is an input, not capability (DM-083). Qualify that trained volunteers still need refresher training and equipment.
**Back:** *"Expanded: a lakh of volunteers across three hundred and fifty districts."*

### Flashcard DM-072 — Yuva Aapda Mitra (youth volunteer programme)
**Front:** What is Yuva Aapda Mitra, and how is it different from Aapda Mitra?
**Definition (learn):** A distinct youth-volunteer programme covering 315 districts, with about 1,300 trainers and master trainers and roughly 2.37 lakh youth volunteers (MHA/PIB, 29 October 2024; 📰 owner 03). It is not a rebranding of Aapda Mitra.
**Breakdown:**
- Districts: 315
- Trainers: about 1,300
- Volunteers: about 2.37 lakh
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MHA/PIB 29 October 2024 (📰 owner 03; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-070, DM-071
**Trap:** Yuva Aapda Mitra and Aapda Mitra are separate programmes, with different district counts.
**Mains hook:** Use youth volunteering as a complement to Aapda Mitra. Qualify with the same input-versus-capability caution.
**Back:** *"Youth three-fifteen districts, two-point-three-seven lakh volunteers — a separate programme."*

### Flashcard DM-073 — NDMA National Guidelines on Community-Based DRR (CBDRR, October 2024)
**Front:** Which NDMA document governs community DRR now?
**Definition (learn):** The National Guidelines on Community-Based Disaster Risk Reduction, issued October 2024, are the current governing document for community DRR (📰 owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: October 2024
- Scope: community-based DRR
- Use as current institutional reference
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDMA CBDRR Guidelines (Oct 2024) (📰 owner 03; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-068, DM-070, DM-074, DM-075, DM-076, DM-077
**Trap:** Cite the document by name, not "NDMA guidelines" generically.
**Mains hook:** Use CBDRR as the benchmark against which community capacity is assessed. Qualify with the need to verify adoption locally.
**Back:** *"Community guidelines, October 2024: the current governing document."*

### Flashcard DM-074 — NDMA Guidelines on Disability Inclusive DRR (September 2019)
**Front:** What is the NDMA guideline on disability-inclusive DRR, and when was it issued?
**Definition (learn):** The Guidelines on Disability Inclusive Disaster Risk Reduction, issued September 2019, are the NDMA anchor for inclusive protection of persons with disabilities (📰 owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: September 2019
- Scope: disability inclusion in DRR
- Needs: accessible alerts, transport, shelter
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** September 2019 (📰 owner 03; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-069, DM-075, DM-077, DM-104
**Trap:** Do not cite the guideline as proof that accessible alerts were delivered.
**Mains hook:** Name the group and the need. Qualify that adoption and outcome require local verification.
**Back:** *"Disability inclusion, September 2019: accessible alert, accessible shelter, accessible evacuation."*

### Flashcard DM-075 — NDMA Guidelines on Temporary Shelters (September 2019)
**Front:** What do the 2019 temporary-shelter guidelines cover?
**Definition (learn):** NDMA guidelines issued in September 2019 for temporary shelters for disaster-affected families (📰 owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: September 2019
- Scope: shelter for displaced families
- Relevant to gender-segregated facilities (owner 03 Advanced)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** September 2019 (📰 owner 03).
**Links:** DM-074, DM-079, DM-080
**Trap:** A guideline on shelters is not evidence that shelters were built to standard.
**Mains hook:** Use shelter design as one of the named protections for displaced women and children. Qualify with the need for verification.
**Back:** *"Temporary shelter, September 2019: a roof that respects dignity."*

### Flashcard DM-076 — Updated National Guidelines on MHPSS in Disasters (December 2023)
**Front:** What do the 2023 MHPSS guidelines cover?
**Definition (learn):** The Updated National Guidelines for Mental Health and Psychosocial Support Services in Disasters, issued December 2023 (📰 owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: December 2023
- Scope: mental health and psychosocial support
- Relevant to recovery and rehabilitation (owner 03, PDF p. 65)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** December 2023 (📰 owner 03).
**Links:** DM-068, DM-069
**Trap:** Psychosocial support is part of recovery, not only emergency response.
**Mains hook:** Include psychosocial support in recovery answers. Qualify that services need funding and trained staff.
**Back:** *"MHPSS, December 2023: the mind is part of the recovery."*

### Flashcard DM-077 — National Guidelines on Animal-inclusive DRR (AiDRR, April 2026)
**Front:** What are the AiDRR guidelines, and when were they issued?
**Definition (learn):** The National Guidelines on Animal-inclusive Disaster Risk Reduction, issued April 2026 (📰 owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: April 2026
- Scope: livestock and animal welfare in DRR
- Recent addition to NDMA guidance
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** April 2026 (📰 owner 03; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-073, DM-074, DM-076
**Trap:** The AiDRR guideline is recent; do not describe it as a long-standing framework.
**Mains hook:** Use animal inclusion as an example of expanded inclusive DRR. Qualify that its implementation status needs verification.
**Back:** *"Animals count too: April 2026 AiDRR."*

### Flashcard DM-078 — Cascading community training through panchayats
**Front:** Why does the source recommend cascading training through panchayats?
**Definition (learn):** Location-specific training is entrusted to local bodies such as village panchayats because of the very large number of people who need training. This is a train-the-trainer design (✅ owner 03, PDF p. 65).
**Breakdown:**
- Institution: PRIs (village panchayats)
- Method: train-the-trainer
- Advantage: reach at scale
- Risk: diluted training quality (owner 03 Advanced)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 65 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-068, DM-083, DM-070
**Trap:** Cascading training does not transfer the State's duty to train. Local training still needs State resources.
**Mains hook:** Use cascading training as a capacity-building model. Qualify with the scale-quality trade-off.
**Back:** *"Train the trainers at the village level: scale first, depth second."*

### Flashcard DM-079 — Disaster displacement: scale and harms
**Front:** What harms does the source name for disaster-displaced persons?
**Definition (learn):** Displacement is linked to humanitarian discrimination in aid distribution, gender-based violence, susceptibility to extremist recruitment, and disproportionate casualties in poorer countries for equal-magnitude disasters. The source gives an average of 13.9 million people displaced each year by natural disasters (✅ owner 03, PDF p. 63; figure document-period, ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Harm 1: discrimination in aid
- Harm 2: gender-based violence
- Harm 3: extremism recruitment risk
- Harm 4: disproportionate losses in poorer countries
- Scale: 13.9 million a year (document-period)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 63 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-016, DM-080, DM-075
**Trap:** The 13.9 million figure is not current. Cite a dated IDMC or UNDRR figure if precision is needed.
**Mains hook:** Argue that displacement needs distinct policy responses for each harm. Qualify with the need for a current displacement statistic.
**Back:** *"Displaced people face four harms: unequal aid, violence, recruitment, and poverty's extra cost."*

### Flashcard DM-080 — Trap: disaster-displaced persons and the 1951 Refugee Convention
**Front:** Are disaster-displaced persons refugees under the 1951 Convention?
**Definition (learn):** Not automatically. The 1951 Refugee Convention uses a persecution-based definition. Disaster-displaced persons' protection depends on domestic law, general human-rights obligations and non-binding international displacement guidance, not a dedicated binding treaty (✅ owner 03, PDF p. 63).
**Breakdown:**
- 1951 test: persecution-based
- Disaster displacement: no universal binding treaty
- Protection exists through domestic law and human-rights obligations
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 1951 Refugee Convention (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-079, DM-075
**Trap:** "No protection at all" is wrong. The gap is a protection gap, not an absence of rights.
**Mains hook:** Argue for a domestic status and entitlement regime. Qualify that international guidance is non-binding.
**Back:** *"Persecution opens the 1951 door; disaster displacement knocks on domestic law instead."*

### Flashcard DM-081 — Trap: community first responders cannot shift State responsibility
**Front:** Can calling communities "first responders" justify less State investment?
**Definition (learn):** No. Community capacity needs State training, equipment, transport and accountable relief. Calling communities first responders is not a reason to withdraw State support (✅ owner 03, revision chart).
**Breakdown:**
- Community: first contact
- State: training, equipment, transport, accountability
- Localisation improves reach but cannot replace public capacity (owner 03 Advanced)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Revision chart "Trap" (✅ revision chart).
**Links:** DM-068, DM-078, DM-083
**Trap:** Localisation rhetoric used to excuse under-resourced States.
**Mains hook:** Argue for co-production: DDMA, ULB, PRI and trained volunteers. Qualify that co-production needs funding.
**Back:** *"First responders are not first-and-only responsibility."*

### Flashcard DM-082 — Advanced: disaggregated vulnerability assessment
**Front:** Why must community plans disaggregate vulnerable groups?
**Definition (learn):** Social vulnerability is a prioritisation tool. Plans should separate evacuation assistance for disabled and elderly persons from relief for pregnant or lactating women, rather than one "vulnerable population" plan (⚠️ owner 03 Advanced Section 2).
**Breakdown:**
- Disaggregate by named group
- Separate evacuation, relief and shelter arrangements
- Compounding, not merely additive, risk (owner 03)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 03 Advanced (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-004, DM-069, DM-079
**Trap:** A single "vulnerable population" plan hides differential need.
**Mains hook:** Argue for disaggregated plans. Qualify that data must exist to disaggregate.
**Back:** *"Name the group, name the need, plan the evacuation for each."*

### Flashcard DM-083 — Advanced: volunteer scale versus quality trade-off
**Front:** What trade-off do volunteer systems face?
**Definition (learn):** Cascading, panchayat-level training reaches many quickly but may dilute quality per volunteer. A smaller, intensively trained corps (closer to Aapda Mitra's model) gives depth but less reach (⚠️ owner 03 Advanced Section 7).
**Breakdown:**
- Option 1: wide reach, shallow training
- Option 2: narrow reach, deep training
- Trade-off is a design choice, not solved by the source
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 03 Advanced (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-070, DM-071, DM-078
**Trap:** Do not assume cascading training is costlessly scalable.
**Mains hook:** Name the trade-off and recommend a hybrid. Qualify with the need for refresher and retention data.
**Back:** *"Wide and thin, or narrow and deep — name the trade-off."*

### Flashcard DM-084 — Advanced: relief distribution accountability gap
**Front:** What accountability gap exists in community-based relief distribution?
**Definition (learn):** The source says community and SHG involvement makes damage assessment and relief distribution proceed smoothly, but it does not detail a grievance-redress mechanism for exclusion errors, such as a household wrongly left off a list (✅ owner 03 Advanced Section 8).
**Breakdown:**
- Process gain: smoother distribution
- Gap: exclusion error redress
- Need: transparent, appealable local process
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 65 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-068, DM-081, DM-062
**Trap:** Community involvement does not automatically make relief accountable.
**Mains hook:** Argue for an appealable relief list. Qualify that the design must avoid elite capture.
**Back:** *"A missing name on the relief list is a disaster too."*

### Flashcard DM-085 — Advanced: flood-only targeting of Aapda Mitra
**Front:** What scope limitation does the flood-district focus create?
**Definition (learn):** The pilot targets the 30 most flood-prone districts. Owner 03 notes the source does not address whether this covers other hazards, such as earthquake, cyclone or heat-wave prone districts (⚠️ owner 03 Advanced Section 3).
**Breakdown:**
- Pilot scope: flood-prone districts
- Gap: other hazard-specific volunteer needs
- Question: whether scope should extend
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 29 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** DM-070, DM-071, DM-072
**Trap:** Do not infer that Aapda Mitra covers all hazards.
**Mains hook:** Recommend hazard-specific volunteer coverage. Qualify with the need for data on hazard-specific gaps.
**Back:** *"Flood-only scope is a pilot limit, not a national policy."*

### GROUP — 04 Multi-Hazard Early Warning and Disaster Technology

### Flashcard DM-086 — End-to-end early warning chain
**Front:** What is the complete early-warning chain, and why must it be read end to end?
**Definition (learn):** The chain runs: risk knowledge → detection/forecast → authoritative warning → dissemination → comprehension → early action → feedback to risk knowledge (✅ owner 04 Section 1; ✅ revision chart Section 8).
**Breakdown:**
- Risk knowledge: hazard and vulnerability data, mapping
- Detection and forecast: sensors, satellites, models
- Authoritative warning: official issuance
- Dissemination: multi-channel
- Comprehension and action: the recipient understands and can act
- Feedback: improves the next cycle
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 Section 1 (✅ owner 04); revision chart (✅ revision chart).
**Links:** DM-011, DM-087, DM-101, DM-103
**Trap:** A sensor is not a warning system. A warning succeeds only if the last person acts.
**Mains hook:** Trace the chain for a named hazard and find the weakest stage. Qualify that the weakest stage differs by hazard and context.
**Back:** *"Know, Detect, Authorise, Send, Understand, Act, Feed back."*

### Flashcard DM-087 — Prediction versus forecast versus early warning
**Front:** How do prediction, forecast, early warning and nowcasting differ?
**Definition (learn):** Prediction is a precise claim about the occurrence, time and place of an event (often impossible for earthquakes). Forecast is a probabilistic estimate over time or area. Early warning is timely, actionable information before impact. Nowcasting is very short-range forecasting (✅ revision chart Section 8; ✅ owner 04).
**Breakdown:**
- Prediction: precise occurrence claim
- Forecast: probabilistic estimate
- Early warning: actionable alert
- Nowcasting: 5–30 minutes ahead (DM-088)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 04 Section 8; revision chart (✅ owner 04; ✅ revision chart).
**Links:** DM-086, DM-088, DM-102, DM-125
**Trap:** Earthquake "prediction" is not possible in magnitude, place and time (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 20). Do not conflate it with forecasts or early warnings.
**Mains hook:** Define the three terms precisely before any hazard answer. Qualify that forecasting tools can be strong for some hazards and weak for others.
**Back:** *"Prediction says when and where for sure; forecast says how likely; warning says act now."*

### Flashcard DM-088 — Nowcasting
**Front:** What is nowcasting, and what lead time does it give?
**Definition (learn):** A technique for very short-range forecasting that maps the current weather and estimates its speed and direction to forecast a short period ahead. It generates forecasts 5–30 minutes ahead (✅ owner 04, PDF p. 30).
**Breakdown:**
- Method: map current weather, then extrapolate movement
- Lead time: 5–30 minutes
- Use: very short-range warnings
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 30 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-087, DM-089
**Trap:** Nowcasting is not a long-range forecast.
**Mains hook:** Use nowcasting for flash-flood and cloudburst alerts. Qualify with the short window it offers.
**Back:** *"Nowcast: now-mapped, five to thirty minutes ahead."*

### Flashcard DM-089 — Doppler weather radar and hydro-meteorological networks (urban flood lead time)
**Front:** What lead time do Doppler radar and hydro-meteorological networks give for urban floods?
**Definition (learn):** Integrated with urban area planning, these systems provide a lead time of 3 to 6 hours for monitoring rainfall ahead of urban flood events (✅ owner 04, PDF p. 30).
**Breakdown:**
- Tool: Doppler radar plus national hydro-meteorological network
- Lead time: 3–6 hours
- Use: urban rainfall monitoring
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 30 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-088, DM-090, DM-102
**Trap:** Radar lead time for rainfall is not evacuation lead time. Action still needs drainage and warnings.
**Mains hook:** Use the lead time to argue for pre-emptive evacuation. Qualify with urban drainage capacity.
**Back:** *"Radar gives three to six hours for urban rain; the drains must still carry it."*

### Flashcard DM-090 — Decision Support System and official-issuance rule
**Front:** What is the Decision Support System for floods, and who may issue public warnings?
**Definition (learn):** A DSS, typically set up by urban local bodies, generates flood warnings and maps hazards. Official warnings must be issued to the public only through government officials (✅ owner 04, PDF p. 30; per NDMA guidelines).
**Breakdown:**
- Function: generate warnings and hazard maps
- Typical operator: ULBs
- Issuance: government officials only
- Flood severity drives evacuation decisions (owner 04)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 30 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-089, DM-104, DM-047
**Trap:** A DSS is not a public broadcast system. Public issuance is authority-controlled.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the official-issuance rule protects against false alarms but creates a bottleneck. Qualify with the need for a decision rule for speed.
**Back:** *"The system thinks; only officials speak to the public."*

### Flashcard DM-091 — Impact-based forecasting
**Front:** What is impact-based forecasting, and how does it differ from weather-based forecasting?
**Definition (learn):** A forecast that communicates likely consequences, such as roof damage, road flooding or crop loss, rather than only meteorological parameters such as wind speed or rainfall. Owner 04 describes it as an internationally recognised shift referenced generically in VisionIAS's material (⚠️ requires current IMD verification).
**Breakdown:**
- Weather-based: wind speed, rainfall amount
- Impact-based: roof damage, roads flooded, crops lost
- Partially realised in India (owner 04)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 Section 2 and Section 5 (✅ owner 04; ⚠️ rollout status not verified).
**Links:** DM-087, DM-090, DM-104
**Trap:** Do not claim comprehensive India-wide impact-based coverage without an IMD service source.
**Mains hook:** Argue for impact-based warnings to link forecasts to actions. Qualify that rollout is incomplete.
**Back:** *"Not how hard the wind blows, but what it will break."*

### Flashcard DM-092 — Geographic Information System (GIS) in three phases
**Front:** What is GIS in disaster management, and which Indian applications does the source give?
**Definition (learn):** An effective, economic and efficient tool for storing, manipulating and presenting spatial and geographic data, used before, during and after disasters (✅ owner 04, PDF pp. 70–71).
**Breakdown:**
- Pre-disaster: vulnerability mapping (Indian Ocean earthquake and tsunami zones), alternate shelter routes, cyclone-shelter siting in Odisha, no-construction and relocation zones
- During: rescue and evacuation planning (Sikkim landslide search and rescue), spread direction as internal warning
- Post-disaster: identifying most damaged areas (Gujarat earthquake region), siting reconstruction in safer places
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDFs pp. 70–71 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-086, DM-101, DM-102
**Trap:** GIS does not substitute for the planning process. It assists only when integrated into planning.
**Mains hook:** Cite the three phases with the Indian examples. Qualify that GIS is an analytical tool, not a decision-maker.
**Back:** *"Map before, rescue during, rebuild after — Odisha, Sikkim, Gujarat."*

### Flashcard DM-093 — Cyclone Warning Dissemination System (IMD)
**Front:** What is the Cyclone Warning Dissemination System, and who runs it?
**Definition (learn):** IMD's satellite-based direct-dissemination system that sends cyclone warnings to cyclone-prone coastal areas via Area Cyclone Warning Centres and Cyclone Warning Centres under a single satellite-based system (✅ owner 04, PDF p. 36; owner 07 develops fully).
**Breakdown:**
- Operator: IMD
- Mode: satellite-based direct dissemination
- Chain: Area Cyclone Warning Centres and Cyclone Warning Centres
- Owner 04: hub-and-spoke model (distributed sensing, centralised issuance)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 36 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-103, DM-086, DM-100
**Trap:** The cyclone system is a dissemination system, not a forecasting model.
**Mains hook:** Use the hub-and-spoke structure to explain coastal alerts. Qualify with the need for last-mile reach.
**Back:** *"Satellite spokes from IMD's cyclone hubs to the coast."*

### Flashcard DM-094 — India Quake app and BhooKamp
**Front:** Which app does NCS currently identify as its earthquake-information app?
**Definition (learn):** VisionIAS's document-period "India Quake" app, developed by the National Centre for Seismology for automatic dissemination of earthquake parameters. NCS and MoES now identify BhooKamp as the official earthquake-information app (📰 PIB/MoES, 1 April 2026; ✅ owner 04).
**Breakdown:**
- Document-period name: India Quake
- Current name: BhooKamp (NCS/MoES)
- Content: location, time, magnitude after an event
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PIB/MoES 1 April 2026 (📰 owner 04; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-115, DM-095, DM-098
**Trap:** Do not use "India Quake" as the current app name without noting BhooKamp.
**Mains hook:** Cite the current app and the parameter it gives. Qualify that a post-event parameter is not a prediction.
**Back:** *"Old name India Quake, new name BhooKamp; neither predicts the next shake."*

### Flashcard DM-095 — SACHET: NDMA's CAP-based Integrated Alert System
**Front:** What is SACHET, and what is its architecture?
**Definition (learn):** NDMA's SACHET portal is the official national source for Common Alerting Protocol (CAP) warnings and multi-channel dissemination (📰 owner 04).
**Breakdown:**
- Standard: CAP
- Channels: SMS, cell broadcast, radio, TV, sirens, web, apps
- Value: interoperability; authored once, rendered many times
- Coverage statistics: use the live portal or a dated official release
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDMA SACHET (📰 owner 04; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-096, DM-097, DM-105
**Trap:** SACHET's existence does not prove receipt, comprehension or action.
**Mains hook:** Use SACHET as the dissemination pillar of EW4All. Qualify with the reach gap for vulnerable groups.
**Back:** *"SACHET sends one alert through many channels."*

### Flashcard DM-096 — Common Alerting Protocol (CAP)
**Front:** What is CAP, and what problem does it solve?
**Definition (learn):** A structured alert standard that lets a hazard agency author one alert and render it across channels such as SMS, cell broadcast, radio, TV, sirens and apps (📰 owner 04).
**Breakdown:**
- Purpose: format interoperability
- Benefit: no need to redraft for each channel
- Used by: SACHET (DM-095)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 (📰 owner 04).
**Links:** DM-095, DM-097, DM-105
**Trap:** CAP solves format, not mandate. It does not decide who authorises an alert (DM-105).
**Mains hook:** Use CAP to show technical interoperability. Qualify that institutional authority is a separate question.
**Back:** *"CAP writes once, sends everywhere; it cannot decide who may press send."*

### Flashcard DM-097 — Cell Broadcast System
**Front:** What does cell broadcast do, and what is the status trap?
**Definition (learn):** A channel that delivers an area-targeted alert to compatible devices in a selected cell, rather than addressing subscribers one by one as SMS does (📰 owner 04).
**Breakdown:**
- Mode: area-targeted, not per-subscriber
- Advantage: avoids SMS queuing and roaming exclusion (owner 04 Advanced)
- Status: nationwide launch, reach and handset coverage need a dated DoT or NDMA source
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 (📰 owner 04; ⚠️ status not verified).
**Links:** DM-095, DM-096, DM-104
**Trap:** A launch date or coverage claim is not established by SACHET's existence.
**Mains hook:** Argue cell broadcast is the strongest technical answer to the bottleneck, but not a repeal of institutional authority. Qualify with status verification.
**Back:** *"Cell broadcast reaches a place, not a list."*

### Flashcard DM-098 — Hazard public apps and their owners
**Front:** Which agency owns each hazard app?
**Definition (learn):** Mausam (IMD, forecasts and warnings); Meghdoot (IMD, agro-met advisories); Damini (IITM Pune, lightning warning); BhooKamp (NCS/MoES, earthquakes); UMANG (MeitY, carrying IMD content) (📰 PIB, 22 July 2026; ✅ owner 04).
**Breakdown:**
- Mausam: IMD
- Meghdoot: IMD, agro-met
- Damini: IITM Pune, lightning
- BhooKamp: NCS/MoES, earthquake
- UMANG: MeitY platform
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PIB 22 July 2026 (📰 owner 04; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-094, DM-095, DM-115
**Trap:** Ownership confusion is frequent. Mausam is not NCS. Damini is not IMD.
**Mains hook:** Use ownership to show institutional division. Qualify that apps reach only smartphone users.
**Back:** *"Mausam, Meghdoot (IMD); Damini (IITM); BhooKamp (NCS); UMANG (MeitY)."*

### Flashcard DM-099 — Early Warnings for All (EW4All)
**Front:** What is EW4All, and what are its four pillars?
**Definition (learn):** The UN Secretary-General's March 2022 call, launched at COP27 in November 2022, to protect everyone on earth with an early warning system by 2027. It is the operational vehicle for Sendai Target G (✅ owner 01; ⚠️ Wikipedia page 404, not fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Pillar 1: disaster-risk knowledge (UNDRR)
- Pillar 2: detection, observation, forecasting (WMO)
- Pillar 3: dissemination and communication (ITU)
- Pillar 4: preparedness and response (IFRC)
- WMO and UNDRR co-lead overall (owner 01)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Launched COP27, November 2022; target 2027 (✅ owner 01; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-021, DM-086, DM-095
**Trap:** EW4All is a global initiative, not an Indian statute. Do not cite it as an Indian programme.
**Mains hook:** Map India's institutions onto the four pillars. Qualify that the mapping is an analytical structure, not a statistical scorecard.
**Back:** *"Knowledge by UNDRR, Forecast by WMO, Dispatch by ITU, Respond by IFRC — all by 2027."*

### Flashcard DM-100 — Phailin "exact prediction" (track forecasting, not prediction)
**Front:** What does the source claim about Cyclone Phailin, and how should it be framed?
**Definition (learn):** VisionIAS credits the "exact prediction of Phailin cyclone" with considerably reducing loss of life and property (✅ owner 04, PDF p. 70). The correct framing is accurate track and intensity forecasting, not deterministic prediction (⚠️ owner 04 analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Source claim: exact prediction reduced loss
- Correct term: forecasting and tracking
- Caution: do not extend to earthquake prediction (DM-108)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 70 (✅ owner 04). Year of Phailin not stated in owner (⚠️).
**Links:** DM-087, DM-093, DM-108
**Trap:** "Prediction" used loosely across hazards is itself an examiner trap.
**Mains hook:** Use Phailin as evidence that forecasting plus evacuation saved lives. Qualify that the success depends on the evacuation chain.
**Back:** *"Phailin was a forecast well acted on, not a prediction."*

### Flashcard DM-101 — Trap: technology deployed does not equal warning success
**Front:** Does deploying satellites, radar and apps prove that warning works?
**Definition (learn):** No. Owner 04 shows technology (detection) and governance (issuance authority and last-mile reach) are separate layers. A warning succeeds only when the last person understands, trusts and can act (✅ owner 04 Section 8; ✅ revision chart).
**Breakdown:**
- Layer 1: detection
- Layer 2: governance and issuance
- Layer 3: last-mile comprehension and action
- Result test: avoided loss, not alerts issued
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 Section 8; revision chart (✅ owner 04; ✅ revision chart).
**Links:** DM-086, DM-103, DM-029
**Trap:** Counting alerts sent as the measure of success.
**Mains hook:** Measure early action, not message transmission. Qualify that outcome data is often not published.
**Back:** *"Sent is not heard; heard is not acted on; acted on is the test."*

### Flashcard DM-102 — Trap: lead times differ by hazard
**Front:** Do all hazards give comparable warning lead times?
**Definition (learn):** No. Lead times range from near zero for earthquakes, to minutes for cloudburst and flash floods, to hours for urban floods and cyclones, and to days for some cyclone tracks (✅ owner 04, Section 8).
**Breakdown:**
- Earthquake: essentially none for prediction; tsunami detection <10 minutes (DM-132)
- Flash flood and cloudburst: minutes
- Urban flood: 3–6 hours radar lead (DM-089)
- Cyclone: hours to days
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-089, DM-106, DM-132
**Trap:** Generalising a single lead time across hazards.
**Mains hook:** Tailor the chain to each hazard. Qualify with the lead-time figure's source date.
**Back:** *"Earthquake: none; flash flood: minutes; urban flood: hours; cyclone: hours to days."*

### Flashcard DM-103 — Advanced: four-stage warning chain (detection, validation, dissemination, last mile)
**Front:** How should a warning chain be analysed, according to the advanced analysis?
**Definition (learn):** Examine four stages: detection, centralised processing and official validation, dissemination channel, and last-mile comprehension and action. Strength in one stage does not guarantee strength in another (⚠️ owner 04 Advanced Section 2).
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: detection (seismic network, Doppler radar, satellite, buoy)
- Stage 2: validation and issuance (IMD, INCOIS, GSI)
- Stage 3: dissemination (bulletins, apps, sirens, community relay)
- Stage 4: last-mile comprehension and action
- Tsunami detection in under 10 minutes; last mile still fails
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 Advanced (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-086, DM-104, DM-101, DM-118
**Trap:** Analysing detection alone and calling the system strong.
**Mains hook:** Name the weakest stage for the case in question. Qualify with evidence needs.
**Back:** *"Detect, Validate, Disseminate, Last Mile — check all four."*

### Flashcard DM-104 — Advanced: centralised-issuance trade-off (speed versus accuracy)
**Front:** Why does the rule that only officials issue warnings create a trade-off?
**Definition (learn):** Central issuance guards against panic from unverified alerts but assumes officials are reachable and fast. Faster preliminary release risks false alarms and warning fatigue; full validation risks lost lead time (✅ owner 04 Advanced Sections 3 and 7).
**Breakdown:**
- Accuracy and control: central issuance
- Speed and reach: distributed or pre-authorised alerts
- Cell broadcast reduces technical bottlenecks but not the decision bottleneck (owner 04)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 Advanced (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-090, DM-097, DM-103
**Trap:** Treating faster dissemination as costless.
**Mains hook:** Propose a decision rule with thresholds and liability for false alarms. Qualify with the need for legal clarity.
**Back:** *"Fast can be false; careful can be late — set the threshold in advance."*

### Flashcard DM-105 — Advanced: CAP solves format, not mandate, interoperability
**Front:** What can CAP solve, and what can it not?
**Definition (learn):** CAP standardises alert format across channels. It does not decide which agency owns a compound event, such as a cyclone that becomes an urban flood and then a dam-safety question (✅ owner 04 Advanced Section 8).
**Breakdown:**
- CAP solves: format interoperability
- CAP does not solve: mandate interoperability
- Compound events need institutional lead designation
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 04 Advanced (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** DM-095, DM-096, DM-054
**Trap:** Believing a standard solves a governance question.
**Mains hook:** Argue for a designated lead agency for compound events. Qualify that the designation needs verification.
**Back:** *"CAP speaks one language; it does not say who is in charge."*

### GROUP — 05 Earthquake Risk and Resilient Construction

### Flashcard DM-106 — Earthquake causation: plate boundaries, faults and stress release
**Front:** What causes earthquakes, and how fast does the Indian plate move?
**Definition (learn):** Earthquakes arise from movements along plate boundaries (divergent, convergent, transform). Stress accumulates at faults and is released by slipping or rupturing, generating seismic waves (✅ owner 05, PDF pp. 19–20). The Indian plate thrusts beneath the Eurasian plate at about 5 cm per year (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Boundary types: divergent, convergent, transform
- Fault: zone of stress at plate boundaries (DM-107)
- Convergent Indian–Eurasian boundary drives the Himalayan belt
- Other active zones: Gulf of Khambhat/Rann of Kutch, peninsular India, Lakshadweep, Andaman and Nicobar (owner 05)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDFs pp. 19–20 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-107, DM-108, DM-110, DM-112
**Trap:** Himalayan belt is not the only seismic area. Owner 05 names peninsular and island exceptions.
**Mains hook:** Use plate mechanics to explain why risk is spatially varied. Qualify that the rate is a broad average.
**Back:** *"Plates push, faults hold, stress slips: the Indian plate's five centimetres a year."*

### Flashcard DM-107 — Earthquake anatomy: focus, epicentre and liquefaction
**Front:** What are the focus, epicentre and liquefaction in earthquake terms?
**Definition (learn):** The focus (hypocentre) is the point of rupture within the Earth. The epicentre is the surface point above the focus. Liquefaction is when saturated, loose soil loses strength during shaking (✅ revision chart Section 9).
**Breakdown:**
- Focus or hypocentre: rupture point underground
- Epicentre: surface point above focus
- Liquefaction: saturated soil loses strength
- Retrofitting: strengthening existing structure (revision chart)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** revision chart Section 9 (✅ revision chart).
**Links:** DM-106, DM-109, DM-124
**Trap:** Epicentre is not the cause. Damage depends on soil, construction and intensity at the site.
**Mains hook:** Use liquefaction to show site-specific risk. Qualify with the need for microzonation data.
**Back:** *"Focus is underground, epicentre above it, liquefaction turns soil to slurry."*

### Flashcard DM-108 — Earthquake prediction is not possible
**Front:** Can earthquakes be predicted in magnitude, place and time?
**Definition (learn):** No. Earthquakes "can neither be prevented nor predicted in terms of their magnitude, or place and time of occurrence." The most effective measures are pre-disaster mitigation, preparedness and preventive measures for reducing vulnerability (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 20).
**Breakdown:**
- Cannot be prevented
- Cannot be predicted in magnitude, place or time
- Therefore risk reduction substitutes for prevention
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 20 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-087, DM-125, DM-106, DM-123
**Trap:** Confusing prediction with early warning or forecast. EEW (DM-125) is not prediction.
**Mains hook:** Build the answer on vulnerability reduction. Qualify that post-event alerts and seconds-scale warning are different.
**Back:** *"Earthquakes come unannounced; build so they cannot hurt."*

### Flashcard DM-109 — Magnitude versus intensity
**Front:** What is the difference between magnitude and intensity, and which scales measure each?
**Definition (learn):** Magnitude measures energy released, commonly on the moment magnitude scale (Mw) for large events. Intensity describes observed effects and may be expressed on the Modified Mercalli Intensity (MMI) or MSK scale (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 19, updated per current practice).
**Breakdown:**
- Magnitude: energy released (Mw; Richter is an older, magnitude-limited local scale)
- Intensity: observed shaking and damage (MMI, MSK)
- One event: one magnitude, many intensities by location
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 05 (✅ owner 05; ⚠️ scale update per current practice).
**Links:** DM-106, DM-107, DM-110
**Trap:** Richter and Mercalli measure the same thing. They do not; they measure different things.
**Mains hook:** Use magnitude and intensity together to explain why damage varies. Qualify that intensity depends on distance and soil.
**Back:** *"Magnitude is energy released; intensity is damage felt. Mw for size, MMI or MSK for effects."*

### Flashcard DM-110 — IS 1893 (Part 1): 2016 seismic zones II–V
**Front:** How many seismic zones does India have under IS 1893 (Part 1): 2016, and where is Zone V?
**Definition (learn):** IS 1893 (Part 1): 2016 defines four seismic zones (II–V) by expected seismic intensity or hazard. Zone V is the highest hazard, comprising all of North-East India and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, plus parts of northern Bihar, Jammu and Kashmir/Ladakh, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand and Gujarat. Most of India lies in Zones II and III (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 20; ⚠️ Wikipedia page for seismic zoning not fetched; the IS standard is not re-fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Zone II: lower hazard
- Zone III: moderate
- Zone IV: high
- Zone V: highest (NE, A&N, parts of Bihar, J&K/Ladakh, HP, Uttarakhand, Gujarat)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** IS 1893 (Part 1): 2016 (✅ owner 05; ⚠️ not web-verified).
**Links:** DM-111, DM-120, DM-106, DM-112
**Trap:** A five-zone revised map is not in force. IS 1893:2016 remains operative (DM-120).
**Mains hook:** Name the zone and the States for the region under question. Qualify that zones describe hazard, not building fate.
**Back:** *"Two to five, five is the worst: North-East, Andamans and parts of the north and west."*

### Flashcard DM-111 — Land-area shares of India's seismic zones
**Front:** What share of India's land area falls in each seismic zone?
**Definition (learn):** Approximate shares are Zone V about 11%, Zone IV about 18%, Zone III about 30%. About 59% of India's landmass is prone to moderate-to-severe earthquakes (Zones III–V), with the balance in Zone II (✅ owner 05, citing PIB/MoES, 30 July 2021; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Zone V: about 11%
- Zone IV: about 18%
- Zone III: about 30%
- Zones III–V combined: about 59%
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PIB/MoES 30 July 2021 (📰 owner 05; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-110, DM-112
**Trap:** Do not quote the percentages as exact; they are approximations from an official release.
**Mains hook:** Use the 59% figure to frame national exposure. Qualify that the share does not mean all buildings are unsafe.
**Back:** *"Eleven, eighteen, thirty: fifty-nine percent of India shakes at moderate or worse."*

### Flashcard DM-112 — Alpine-Himalayan Belt and fourteen States
**Front:** What is the Alpine-Himalayan Belt, and how many States does it expose?
**Definition (learn):** The convergent boundary where the Indian plate meets the Eurasian plate, thrusting at about 5 cm per year. It makes fourteen States across the western and central Himalayas, the North-East and the Indo-Gangetic basin highly earthquake-prone (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 20).
**Breakdown:**
- Process: convergent thrust
- Rate: about 5 cm per year
- Exposure: fourteen States
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 20 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-106, DM-110, DM-123, DM-126
**Trap:** The belt is one hazard zone among several, not the entire seismic map.
**Mains hook:** Link Himalayan seismicity with land-use pressure. Qualify with the caution that Himalayan risk is also landslide and GLOF risk (topic 10).
**Back:** *"Fourteen States sit on the Himalayan thrust."*

### Flashcard DM-113 — NDMA's six pillars of seismic safety
**Front:** What are NDMA's six pillars of seismic safety?
**Definition (learn):** NDMA's earthquake-management guidelines rest on six pillars: (1) earthquake-resistant construction of new structures; (2) selective seismic strengthening and retrofitting of priority and lifeline structures; (3) regulation and enforcement; (4) awareness and preparedness; (5) capacity development; (6) emergency response (✅ owner 05, PDF pp. 21–22; 2007 earthquake guidelines per owner).
**Breakdown:**
- P1 new construction
- P2 retrofit priority structures
- P3 regulate and enforce
- P4 awareness and preparedness
- P5 capacity development
- P6 emergency response
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDMA 2007 earthquake guidelines (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-114, DM-118, DM-119, DM-123
**Trap:** The six pillars are a framework, not a list of completed measures.
**Mains hook:** Cite the pillars by number in a framework answer. Qualify that compliance is the constraint (DM-123).
**Back:** *"New build, retrofit, enforce, aware, capacity, respond: six pillars."*

### Flashcard DM-114 — National Building Code and NDMA's earthquake building guidelines
**Front:** What is the National Building Code, and which NDMA guidelines build on it?
**Definition (learn):** The NBC gives comprehensive guidance on building materials, planning, design and construction practice, protecting structural sufficiency, fire safety and health (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 21). NDMA issued a Simplified Guideline for Earthquake Safety of Buildings from NBC 2016 (May 2021) and Guidelines on Seismic Retrofitting of Deficient Buildings and Structures (June 2014) (📰 owner 05; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- NBC: comprehensive construction code
- NBC 2016 simplified earthquake guideline: May 2021
- Seismic retrofitting guidelines: June 2014
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NBC (✅ owner 05); guidelines dated per owner (📰 owner 05).
**Links:** DM-113, DM-123, DM-124
**Trap:** A code's existence does not mean buildings comply (DM-123).
**Mains hook:** Use the NBC as the design layer and enforcement as the binding constraint. Qualify with compliance evidence.
**Back:** *"The code writes the rule; compliance builds the wall."*

### Flashcard DM-115 — National Centre for Seismology (NCS)
**Front:** What is the NCS, which ministry houses it, and what does it run?
**Definition (learn):** An office of the Ministry of Earth Sciences that conducts earthquake surveillance and submits hazard reports to government agencies. It developed the earthquake-parameter app recorded by VisionIAS as "India Quake" (✅ owner 05, PDF pp. 21–22).
**Breakdown:**
- Parent: Ministry of Earth Sciences
- Network: 170 observatories (📰 PIB/MoES, 1 April 2026)
- App: BhooKamp (current; DM-094)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NCS (✅ owner 05); PIB 1 April 2026 (📰 owner 05; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-094, DM-098, DM-125
**Trap:** NCS reports after events. It does not predict earthquakes.
**Mains hook:** Use NCS as the monitoring anchor. Qualify that monitoring is not prediction.
**Back:** *"NCS watches the ground; it reports the shake, not the next one."*

### Flashcard DM-116 — BMTPC and the Vulnerability Atlas of India
**Front:** What does BMTPC do, and what is the Vulnerability Atlas of India?
**Definition (learn):** The Building Materials and Technology Promotion Council promotes resource-efficient, disaster-resistant construction and lifeline-structure retrofitting. It produces the Vulnerability Atlas of India, third edition 2019, covering earthquake, wind, flood, landslide, cyclone and thunderstorm hazards State-wise, plus housing-stock vulnerability (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 18).
**Breakdown:**
- Acronym: BMTPC (not "BMPTC" as the source renders it)
- Atlas: third edition 2019
- Hazards: six hazard types, State and UT-wise
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** BMTPC; Vulnerability Atlas third edition 2019 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-113, DM-114, DM-117
**Trap:** The 2019 edition may not be the latest. Verify before calling it current (owner 05 Section 8).
**Mains hook:** Use the Atlas for State-wise vulnerability planning. Qualify with the need for the current edition.
**Back:** *"BMTPC builds better and maps the weakness in the Atlas."*

### Flashcard DM-117 — National Earthquake Risk Mitigation Project
**Front:** What does the National Earthquake Risk Mitigation Project do?
**Definition (learn):** A project that strengthens structural and non-structural dimensions and reduces vulnerability in high-risk districts (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 21).
**Breakdown:**
- Scope: structural and non-structural
- Target: high-risk districts
- Budget and status: not detailed in owner 05 (⚠️)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 21 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-116, DM-118
**Trap:** A project name does not prove completed works. Verify status with a dated source.
**Mains hook:** Use the project as a targeted mitigation tool. Qualify with funding and status verification.
**Back:** *"Mitigate the high-risk districts, both bricks and non-bricks."*

### Flashcard DM-118 — State responsibility for priority structures
**Front:** Which structures must States identify for retrofitting?
**Definition (learn):** The responsibility to identify priority structures for retrofitting rests with State Governments through ULBs and PRIs. Examples: Raj Bhavans, legislatures, courts, academic institutions, reservoirs, dams and multi-storeyed buildings of five or more floors (✅ owner 05, PDF p. 21).
**Breakdown:**
- Responsible: State Governments
- Delivery: ULBs and PRIs
- Priority list: government and public buildings, reservoirs, dams, tall buildings
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 21 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-113, DM-119, DM-120
**Trap:** Retrofit identification is a State duty; the Centre sets codes and guidelines. Do not write "the government" as the actor.
**Mains hook:** Name the State and the structure class. Qualify that the pace varies with State capacity.
**Back:** *"States find, ULBs and PRIs fix: five floors and up, dams, courts, legislatures."*

### Flashcard DM-119 — Centre and State division of seismic responsibility
**Front:** How are seismic-safety duties divided between the Centre and States?
**Definition (learn):** Central ministries and State governments must facilitate seismically safe design and construction of structures under their administrative control, such as bridges, flyovers, ports and harbours. States must establish mechanisms to ensure builders, architects, engineers and departments comply. A Home Ministry-constituted national expert group recommended changes to town and country planning Acts, zoning, DCRs and building bye-laws (✅ owner 05 Advanced Section 3, PDF pp. 21–22).
**Breakdown:**
- Centre: codes and guidelines; structures under central control
- States: compliance, retrofit identification, enforcement
- National expert group: recommended planning-law reform
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDFs pp. 21–22 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-118, DM-113, DM-114
**Trap:** Seismic safety is not a single government duty. Name the tier.
**Mains hook:** Use the division to show accountability gaps. Qualify with state capacity variation.
**Back:** *"Centre sets, State enforces: ministries own their bridges, States own the rest."*

### Flashcard DM-120 — Trap: seismic zonation revision withdrawn (March 2026)
**Front:** Has India adopted a revised seismic zone map?
**Definition (learn):** No. A proposed revised zonation was withdrawn in March 2026 (📰 PIB/MoES, 1 April 2026), so IS 1893 (Part 1): 2016 with four zones remains operative (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Status: withdrawn in March 2026
- Operative standard: IS 1893 (Part 1): 2016
- Do not describe a five-zone map as in force
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PIB/MoES 1 April 2026 (📰 owner 05; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-110, DM-111, DM-124
**Trap:** Announced revision is not implemented revision.
**Mains hook:** Use the withdrawal to argue that hazard maps have distributional consequences. Qualify with the need for a re-verified source.
**Back:** *"Proposed in, withdrawn in March 2026: the four-zone map still rules."*

### Flashcard DM-121 — Bhuj (Gujarat) 2001
**Front:** Why is the Bhuj earthquake of 2001 a key case in the owner's earthquake analysis?
**Definition (learn):** The owner uses Bhuj (Gujarat) 2001 as the local-source historical case showing damaged built environments and post-event GIS damage identification (✅ owner 05, Section 13.1; GIS use per ✅ owner 04, PDF p. 71).
**Breakdown:**
- Case: Bhuj, Gujarat, 2001
- Lesson: construction and siting matter, not only tectonic location
- Use: GIS post-event damage mapping
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2001 (✅ owner 05); exact day not stated in owner (⚠️).
**Links:** DM-092, DM-110, DM-122
**Trap:** Use no casualty or magnitude figure without a case-specific source (owner 05).
**Mains hook:** Use Bhuj to argue for compliance and siting. Qualify with the need for an exact event source.
**Back:** *"Bhuj 2001: built environment failed; GIS mapped the damage."*

### Flashcard DM-122 — Latur 1993 and Koyna: the peninsula is not aseismic
**Front:** Which cases refute the idea that the peninsula is aseismic?
**Definition (learn):** Latur (1993) and Koyna are used by owner 05 to prevent the error that "the Peninsula is aseismic" (✅ owner 05, Section 13.1). Dates for Koyna are not stated in the owner file (⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Latur 1993: peninsular damaging earthquake (owner)
- Koyna: reservoir-linked seismicity (⚠️ memory; owner names Koyna without this detail)
- Lesson: vulnerability and siting matter outside the Himalaya
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Latur 1993 (✅ owner 05); Koyna (no date in owner; ⚠️).
**Links:** DM-110, DM-121
**Trap:** Do not claim the peninsula is free of seismic risk.
**Mains hook:** Use peninsular cases to argue for national, not Himalayan-only, preparedness. Qualify with case-specific data.
**Back:** *"Latur and Koyna: the peninsula shakes too."*

### Flashcard DM-123 — Advanced: earthquake risk as a compliance chain
**Front:** Why is earthquake mitigation a compliance chain rather than a list of gaps?
**Definition (learn):** Since earthquakes cannot be predicted, safety depends on code design, licensing, enforcement, retrofitting funding and land-use control. The owner's "existing challenges" list (code enforcement, absence of seismic features, lack of professional training, low awareness, no engineer or mason licensing) is a single chain (✅ owner 05 Advanced Section 2).
**Breakdown:**
- Design: code
- Professional: training and licensing
- Enforcement: builders and officials
- Retrofitting and funding
- Failure at any link is loss
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDFs pp. 20–21 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-113, DM-114, DM-108, DM-124
**Trap:** A National Building Code does not mean safe buildings.
**Mains hook:** Trace the chain and name the weakest link. Qualify that the weakest link differs by district.
**Back:** *"Code, licence, enforce, retrofit: break one link and the chain fails."*

### Flashcard DM-124 — Advanced: new-construction codes versus existing-stock retrofitting
**Front:** Why is retrofitting existing buildings the harder task?
**Definition (learn):** NDMA's six pillars separate new-construction codes from retrofitting existing priority structures. Enforcing new-build codes is easier than retrofitting the much larger, non-compliant stock (✅ owner 05 Advanced Section 7).
**Breakdown:**
- New build: enforce at approval
- Existing stock: costly, slow, often owner-resisted
- Owner 05 argues existing-stock retrofitting is the harder and arguably higher-priority task
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 05 Advanced (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-113, DM-123, DM-120
**Trap:** Treating a new-build code as proof that the stock is safe.
**Mains hook:** Argue for retrofit financing as a priority. Qualify that the financing route is not identified in the source (owner 05 Section 9).
**Back:** *"New buildings follow the code; old ones need the retrofit."*

### Flashcard DM-125 — Advanced: earthquake early warning is not prediction
**Front:** How does earthquake early warning differ from prediction?
**Definition (learn):** Earthquake early warning detects P-waves seconds before more destructive S-waves arrive. It is a narrower technology distinct from prediction, and from the earthquake-to-tsunami detection chain (⚠️ owner 05 Advanced Section 5; not web-verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Prediction: precise occurrence claim (not possible)
- EEW: post-detection alert, seconds
- Do not claim an operational Indian EEW without a dated official source
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 05 Advanced (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-108, DM-087, DM-115
**Trap:** Presenting EEW as prediction.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish the three terms in every seismic answer. Qualify with the need for status verification.
**Back:** *"P-waves arrive first; EEW uses them; prediction never existed."*

### Flashcard DM-126 — Advanced: informal construction and equity
**Front:** Why does seismic risk fall disproportionately on informal and rural housing?
**Definition (learn):** The absence of engineer and mason licensing implies informal, owner-built rural and peri-urban housing, often used by lower-income households, is less likely to be code-compliant even where the NBC is adequate (⚠️ owner 05 Advanced Section 6).
**Breakdown:**
- Informal construction: owner-built, unlicensed
- Lower-income households more exposed
- Compliance failure is not uniformly distributed
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 05 Advanced (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** DM-123, DM-114, DM-069
**Trap:** Assuming compliance failure is evenly spread.
**Mains hook:** Argue for affordable compliance and mason training. Qualify with household-level evidence.
**Back:** *"Unlicensed hands build the poorest homes, where the code rarely reaches."*

### Flashcard DM-127 — PYQ 2021 GS-III Q8 (earthquake vulnerability and historical examples)
**Front:** What does the 2021 GS-III Q8 demand?
**Definition (learn):** Routed by owner 05 as "India's vulnerability to earthquake hazards and historical disaster examples" (discuss, 10 marks, 150 words) (✅ owner 05 Historical PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Vulnerability as hazard plus built environment
- Use Bhuj 2001 and one peninsular counterexample (DM-121, DM-122)
- Conclude with codes and retrofit priorities
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-III 2021 Q8 (✅ owner 05 ledger).
**Links:** DM-110, DM-121, DM-122, DM-123
**Trap:** Do not argue only from the Himalayan belt.
**Mains hook:** Link vulnerability to compliance. Qualify with local evidence.
**Back:** *"Bhuj plus one peninsular case, then codes and retrofits."*

### GROUP — 06 Tsunami and Coastal Hazard Management

### Flashcard DM-128 — Tsunami: definition and etymology
**Front:** What is a tsunami?
**Definition (learn):** A series of large waves of extremely long wavelength and period, usually generated by an undersea disturbance. The word is Japanese for "harbour wave" (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 23; etymology per owner 06 Section 2).
**Breakdown:**
- Waves of long wavelength and period
- Generated by undersea disturbance near the coast or in the ocean
- Not a storm wave; a displacement wave (owner 06 revision chart)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 23 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-129, DM-130, DM-131
**Trap:** A tsunami is not a large surf wave from a storm.
**Mains hook:** Define the tsunami as a displacement wave to explain its consequences. Qualify that not every undersea earthquake triggers a destructive tsunami.
**Back:** *"Harbour wave: a long-period wave from the seabed, not a storm's surf."*

### Flashcard DM-129 — Tsunami triggers
**Front:** What triggers tsunamis, besides earthquakes?
**Definition (learn):** Most tsunamis are earthquake-driven through vertical seabed movement. Landslides (into or under water), volcanic activity and meteorite impacts are alternative triggers (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Primary: earthquake (vertical seabed movement)
- Alternatives: landslide, volcanic activity, meteorite impact
- Bay of Bengal and Arabian Sea sediment from Ganges and Indus makes landslide tsunamis a plausible regional scenario (owner 06)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 23 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-128, DM-106, DM-143
**Trap:** "Tsunamis are caused only by earthquakes" is a standard trap (owner 06).
**Mains hook:** Use the trigger list for scenario planning. Qualify that non-earthquake triggers are less frequent.
**Back:** *"Quake, slide, volcano, meteor: earthquake leads, the other three are the alternatives."*

### Flashcard DM-130 — Tsunami wave mechanics: deep water to shallow water
**Front:** How do tsunami waves behave in deep and shallow water?
**Definition (learn):** In deep ocean, wave height may be low and speed high. As the wave reaches shallow water it slows, its height grows (shoaling), and inundation risk rises. This shoaling enables run-up, inundation and damaging return flow (✅ revision chart Section 10; ✅ owner 06 Section 13.1).
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: major undersea displacement
- Stage 2: long ocean waves
- Stage 3: shoaling near coast
- Stage 4: inundation and return flow
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** revision chart; owner 06 (✅ revision chart; ✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-128, DM-131, DM-132
**Trap:** A tsunami is not dangerous only at the coast; deep-water speed is high.
**Mains hook:** Use the mechanism to explain formation to consequence. Qualify that no unverified speed or height should be quoted.
**Back:** *"Deep and fast, shallow and tall: shoaling turns a ripple into a wall."*

### Flashcard DM-131 — Effect-determining factors
**Front:** What determines the effect of a tsunami?
**Definition (learn):** The characteristics of the generating seismic event, the distance from the origin or magnitude, and bathymetry (the ocean-depth configuration) (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: event characteristics
- Distance from origin and magnitude
- Bathymetry: depth configuration
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 23 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-130, DM-146, DM-140
**Trap:** Magnitude alone does not determine impact; bathymetry and distance matter.
**Mains hook:** Use the three factors to explain why nearby and far impacts differ. Qualify that the factors interact.
**Back:** *"Source, distance, depth: three factors decide the tsunami's bite."*

### Flashcard DM-132 — Indian Tsunami Early Warning System
**Front:** What is the Indian Tsunami Early Warning System, and how fast does it detect?
**Definition (learn):** Established with the Department of Space, Department of Science and Technology and CSIR. It is a real-time network of seismic stations, Bottom Pressure Recorders and tide gauges that detects tsunamigenic earthquakes in the Indian Ocean in under 10 minutes (✅ owner 06, PDF pp. 24–25; ✅ owner 04). The seismic subsystem draws on IMD's national seismic network and international networks (✅ owner 06 Section 3).
**Breakdown:**
- Partners: Department of Space, DST, CSIR
- Seismic network: IMD national plus international
- BPRs detect open-ocean wave propagation
- Tide gauges: five-minute interval readings at ITEWC (✅ Wiki: ITEWC)
- RTSMN: 17-station network envisaged by IMD (owner 06)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDFs pp. 24–25 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-133, DM-135, DM-102, DM-103
**Trap:** The under-10-minute figure is detection, not warning delivery (DM-135).
**Mains hook:** Use the detection layer as the strongest link in the chain. Qualify with the inundation-data gap (DM-146).
**Back:** *"Seismic, pressure, tide: three sensors, ten minutes to detect."*

### Flashcard DM-133 — ITEWC at INCOIS, Hyderabad
**Front:** When did the Indian Tsunami Early Warning Centre start, and what are its timings?
**Definition (learn):** The ITEWC at INCOIS, Hyderabad, set up by the Ministry of Earth Sciences, became operational on 15 October 2007 (✅ Wiki: ITEWC). It detects tsunamigenic earthquakes in about 10 minutes and disseminates advisories in about 20 minutes (📰 PIB, 4 February 2026, per owner 06; ⚠️ not re-fetched). By 2012 it provided round-the-clock alerts to all Indian Ocean Rim countries (✅ Wiki).
**Breakdown:**
- Start: 15 October 2007
- Parent: Ministry of Earth Sciences; INCOIS is part of ESSO (✅ Wiki)
- Detection: about 10 minutes
- Advisory: about 20 minutes
- Coverage: India and the Indian Ocean Rim (IOTWMS)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 15 October 2007 (✅ Wiki); PIB 4 February 2026 (📰 owner 06).
**Links:** DM-132, DM-134, DM-135
**Trap:** The recipient-country count changes; cite INCOIS's current roster rather than a memorised number (owner 06).
**Mains hook:** Use ITEWC as the regional-service example. Qualify with the dissemination gap (DM-135).
**Back:** *"Fifteen October 2007, Hyderabad: ten minutes to detect, twenty to advise."*

### Flashcard DM-134 — UNESCO-IOC Tsunami Service Provider (IOTWMS)
**Front:** What is ITEWC's regional role under UNESCO-IOC?
**Definition (learn):** ITEWC operates as an approved Tsunami Service Provider under the UNESCO Intergovernmental Oceanographic Commission's Indian Ocean Tsunami Warning and Mitigation System (IOTWMS), issuing advisories for India and the wider region (📰 owner 06).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: regional and global tsunami service provider
- Framework: IOTWMS
- Exact designation date: not established by a dated first-party source (⚠️ owner 06)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNESCO-IOC IOTWMS (📰 owner 06; ⚠️ not re-fetched).
**Links:** DM-133, DM-136
**Trap:** Do not invent a designation year. State the role.
**Mains hook:** Use the service-provider role to show India's regional leadership. Qualify with the need for dated designation evidence.
**Back:** *"ITEWC serves the ocean, not just India."*

### Flashcard DM-135 — Trap: detection time is not warning time
**Front:** Why are detection time and warning time different numbers?
**Definition (learn):** ITEWC detects a tsunamigenic earthquake in roughly 10 minutes but disseminates the advisory in roughly 20 minutes. The gap is confirmation and authorisation (✅ owner 06, Section 8).
**Breakdown:**
- Detection: about 10 minutes
- Advisory: about 20 minutes
- Gap: confirmation and authorisation
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 06 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-132, DM-133, DM-104
**Trap:** Quoting one figure for both collapses the distinction.
**Mains hook:** Separate detection speed from dissemination speed. Qualify that the gap is institutional as well as technical.
**Back:** *"Ten to detect, twenty to advise: the gap is confirmation."*

### Flashcard DM-136 — UNESCO-IOC "Tsunami Ready" and Odisha's first communities
**Front:** What is Tsunami Ready, and which Indian communities were first recognised?
**Definition (learn):** A UNESCO-IOC community-level, indicator-based recognition programme (not a national certification). India's first recognised communities were Venkatraipur (Ganjam) and Noliasahi (Jagatsinghpur) in Odisha, recognised on 7 August 2020 (📰 owner 06; ⚠️ Wikipedia page 404; not fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Level: community
- Basis: defined preparedness indicators
- Odisha communities: Venkatraipur (Ganjam), Noliasahi (Jagatsinghpur)
- Date: 7 August 2020
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNESCO-IOC; 7 August 2020 (📰 owner 06).
**Links:** DM-103, DM-148, DM-134
**Trap:** Do not describe a State or the country as "fully Tsunami Ready."
**Mains hook:** Use Tsunami Ready as a community readiness indicator. Qualify with the need for periodic renewal.
**Back:** *"Two Odisha villages, 7 August 2020: community-level, indicator-based, not national."*

### Flashcard DM-137 — Tsunami N2 model
**Front:** What is the Tsunami N2 model, and who customised it?
**Definition (learn):** A model customised by ICMAM and INCOIS to predict surges for different earthquake scenarios and inundation extent, informing evacuation, settlement avoidance and structural design (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 25).
**Breakdown:**
- Developers: ICMAM and INCOIS
- Output: surge and inundation scenarios
- Use: evacuation, siting, design
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 25 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-132, DM-146, DM-092
**Trap:** A model scenario is not a forecast of a specific event.
**Mains hook:** Use the model for scenario planning. Qualify with the near-shore bathymetry gap (DM-146).
**Back:** *"N2 imagines the wave before it comes."*

### Flashcard DM-138 — Bio-shield
**Front:** What is a bio-shield?
**Definition (learn):** A narrow, thickly planted strip of land along the coastline, developed as a coastal-zone disaster-management sanctuary with public-awareness spaces (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 24).
**Breakdown:**
- Form: thick planting along coast
- Function: buffer and awareness
- Ecosystem-based measure
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 24 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-141, DM-144, DM-149
**Trap:** A bio-shield is not a sea wall. It is an ecosystem-based buffer.
**Mains hook:** Argue for ecosystem measures alongside structural ones. Qualify that they take years to mature (DM-149).
**Back:** *"Bio-shield: a planted coast that breathes and buffers."*

### Flashcard DM-139 — CRZ Notification 2019 and the MHA model techno-legal framework
**Front:** Which instruments regulate tsunami-safe coastal development?
**Definition (learn):** The CRZ Notification 2019 sets category-specific controls (CRZ-I to CRZ-IV), each with distinct no-development and regulated-development zones. These are combined with tsunami inundation and hazard mapping, and with MHA's model techno-legal framework for tsunami-safe zoning, planning, design and construction (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 24; updated per CRZ Notification 2019).
**Breakdown:**
- CRZ categories: I, II, III, IV
- Combined with tsunami inundation mapping
- MHA model techno-legal framework
- No single universal distance or elevation rule
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** CRZ Notification 2019 (✅ owner 06; ⚠️ not re-fetched); MHA model framework (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-138, DM-149, DM-143
**Trap:** Do not present a single universal setback distance as the CRZ rule.
**Mains hook:** Name the category and the instrument. Qualify with enforcement gaps (owner 06 Advanced).
**Back:** *"CRZ one to four, plus the inundation map: no single distance fits all."*

### Flashcard DM-140 — 26 December 2004 Indian Ocean Tsunami
**Front:** What lessons does the 2004 Indian Ocean tsunami give for India?
**Definition (learn):** The most destructive tsunami to hit India in the source's record, on 26 December 2004 (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 23). Maximum damage occurred in low-lying, densely populated coastal areas, and heavy damage occurred where sand dunes were mined (✅ owner 06). The Indian Naval Hydrographic Department deployed seven survey ships to open sea lines of communication and re-chart bathymetry (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 24).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 26 December 2004
- Exposure: low-lying, dense coast
- Natural barrier loss: dune mining
- Response: seven NHD survey ships
- Institutional result: ITEWC (DM-133)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDFs pp. 23–24 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-141, DM-142, DM-132, DM-145
**Trap:** Use the 2004 case for a specified lesson, not a casualty recital (owner 06).
**Mains hook:** Use 2004 to show how institutions follow disasters. Qualify that a single event does not prove recurrence rates.
**Back:** *"Boxing Day 2004: dunes lost, ships launched, warning built after the wave."*

### Flashcard DM-141 — Natural barriers versus structural defences
**Front:** Which natural barriers does the source name, and how does it weigh them against sea walls?
**Definition (learn):** Mangroves, forests, sand dunes and coastal cliffs provided the best natural barriers in 2004. Owner 06 says ecosystem-based measures are not a secondary option to structural ones (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: mangroves, forests, sand dunes, coastal cliffs
- Structural: sea walls, embankments, breakwaters
- Combined strategy is advisable (owner 06 Advanced Section 7)
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 23 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-138, DM-140, DM-144, DM-149
**Trap:** Sea walls are not always the preferred choice; natural barriers can be better.
**Mains hook:** Argue for a combined natural and engineered strategy. Qualify that natural barriers need decades to mature.
**Back:** *"Mangroves, forests, dunes, cliffs — nature's first line."*

### Flashcard DM-142 — Indian Naval Hydrographic Department (INHD)
**Front:** What role does the INHD play in tsunami preparedness?
**Definition (learn):** The INHD regularly supplies bathymetry information for inundation maps, and in 2004 deployed seven survey ships for sea-lane clearance, medical aid and bathymetry re-charting (✅ owner 06, PDF pp. 23–24; Advanced Section 4).
**Breakdown:**
- Bathymetry for inundation mapping
- 2004: seven survey ships
- Civil-military role in civilian disaster response
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDFs pp. 23–24 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-140, DM-146, DM-132
**Trap:** The NHD's civilian role is within DM scope, not Internal Security operations.
**Mains hook:** Use the NHD as an example of dual-use capacity. Qualify that its role is civilian support.
**Back:** *"Hydrographers chart the sea and clear the lanes."*

### Flashcard DM-143 — Exposure and historical record (document-period)
**Front:** What exposure and historical figures does the source give for India's coast?
**Definition (learn):** India's East and West coasts are vulnerable, with more than 2,200 km of heavily populated shoreline. In the last 300 years the Indian Ocean region recorded 13 tsunamis, three in the Andaman and Nicobar region (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 23; document-period figures, ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Shoreline: more than 2,200 km (document-period)
- Historical record: 13 tsunamis in 300 years (Indian Ocean)
- Andaman and Nicobar: three of the 13
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 23 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-129, DM-140, DM-145
**Trap:** Do not treat document-period figures as current statistics (owner 06).
**Mains hook:** Use the record to justify preparedness for rare events. Qualify with a current INCOIS or GSI source.
**Back:** *"Two thousand two hundred kilometres, thirteen tsunamis in three centuries."*

### Flashcard DM-144 — Structural mitigation toolkit for coastal tsunami risk
**Front:** Which structural and coastal measures does NDMA recommend?
**Definition (learn):** Cyclone shelters, submerged sand barriers or dykes, sand dunes with sea-weed planting, mangrove and coastal-forest plantation, local knowledge centres, location-specific sea walls or coral reefs, breakwaters, bio-shields, and identification of vulnerable structures for retrofitting (✅ owner 06, PDF p. 24).
**Breakdown:**
- Engineered: cyclone shelters, dykes, sea walls, breakwaters
- Ecological: dunes, mangroves, coral reefs, bio-shields
- Institutional: knowledge centres, retrofit identification
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 24 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-138, DM-141, DM-149
**Trap:** A list is not a plan. Combine with location-specific siting.
**Mains hook:** Use the toolkit to argue for a mixed strategy. Qualify with cost and maintenance.
**Back:** *"Shelters, dykes, dunes, mangroves, reefs, walls, bio-shields — a mix, not a single fix."*

### Flashcard DM-145 — Advanced: low-frequency, high-consequence readiness decay
**Front:** What is readiness decay in tsunami governance?
**Definition (learn):** Tsunamis are rare, so monitoring, drills and structural upkeep lose priority between events. Institutions are built after catastrophe (for example ITEWC after 2004), then readiness erodes until the next event (⚠️ owner 06 Advanced Section 2 analytical model).
**Breakdown:**
- Low frequency
- High consequence
- Post-event institution-building spike
- Gradual readiness decay
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 06 Advanced (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-140, DM-143, DM-148, DM-136
**Trap:** Treating tsunami risk as a budget priority that is automatically sustained.
**Mains hook:** Argue for institutionalised drills and recognition cycles. Qualify that the argument rests on analysis, not on measured decay.
**Back:** *"Rare waves, forgotten drills: build the routine, not just the monument."*

### Flashcard DM-146 — Advanced: bathymetry data gap
**Front:** What limits the accuracy of tsunami inundation modelling?
**Definition (learn):** The source lists lack of high-resolution near-shore bathymetric and topographic data as a limiting factor for inundation models (✅ owner 06 Advanced Section 5, PDF p. 25).
**Breakdown:**
- Detection speed: strong (under 10 minutes)
- Inundation precision: data-limited
- Two different strengths and weaknesses
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 25 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-132, DM-137, DM-142
**Trap:** Treating detection speed as proof of inundation accuracy.
**Mains hook:** Separate detection from modelling precision. Qualify with the need for bathymetric survey.
**Back:** *"Fast detection, fuzzy flood map: the bathymetry is missing."*

### Flashcard DM-147 — Advanced: vulnerability of the sensing network
**Front:** What physical threat does the source name to the tsunami detection network?
**Definition (learn):** Unattended ocean observation platforms are "being vandalized" (✅ owner 06 Advanced Section 5, PDF p. 24). The advanced response is redundant, tamper-resistant sensing.
**Breakdown:**
- Threat: vandalism of unattended platforms
- Remedy: redundancy and tamper resistance
- Single-point detection is fragile
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 24 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-132, DM-133, DM-103
**Trap:** Assuming the network is secure by design.
**Mains hook:** Argue for redundant sensing. Qualify with cost and maintenance.
**Back:** *"Unattended buoys get vandalised: build redundancy into the eyes."*

### Flashcard DM-148 — Advanced: community awareness and traditional-knowledge gaps
**Front:** Which community gaps does the source name for tsunami preparedness?
**Definition (learn):** Inadequate community awareness of tsunami risk and vulnerability; lack of people's participation in strengthening preparedness; and lack of documentation of traditional knowledge for tsunami risk management (✅ owner 06 Advanced Sections 6 and 8, PDF p. 25).
**Breakdown:**
- Awareness gap
- Participation gap
- Traditional-knowledge documentation gap
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 25 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-068, DM-136, DM-145
**Trap:** A technologically capable system still fails if communities do not understand.
**Mains hook:** Argue for community-based drills and documented local knowledge. Qualify with local data.
**Back:** *"Aware people, present people, and written memories of the sea."*

### Flashcard DM-149 — Advanced: structural versus ecosystem-based coastal defence
**Front:** How should structural and ecosystem-based coastal defences be compared?
