**Definition (learn):** Sea walls and embankments give predictable, engineered protection at higher cost and possible ecological disruption. Bio-shields, mangroves and dunes offer lower cost and co-benefits such as fisheries and carbon storage, but take decades to mature and face the same mining and encroachment pressures (⚠️ owner 06 Advanced Section 7).
**Breakdown:**
- Structural: predictable, costly, ecological cost
- Ecosystem: cheap, co-benefits, slow, vulnerable
- Recommendation: combined strategy
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** owner 06 Advanced (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** DM-141, DM-144, DM-138
**Trap:** Choosing either one as a complete solution.
**Mains hook:** Recommend a combined strategy. Qualify with the site-specific trade-off.
**Back:** *"Engineer for certainty, plant for longevity, combine for resilience."*

### Flashcard DM-150 — PYQ 2025 GS-I Q7 (tsunamis: formation and consequences)
**Front:** What does the 2025 GS-I Q7 demand?
**Definition (learn):** Routed as "tsunamis: formation and consequences" (explain, 10 marks, 150 words). The primary ownership is Geography, and this owner supplies the disaster-management application (✅ owner 06 Recent PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Formation: undersea displacement, wave travel
- Consequences: shoaling, inundation, return flow
- DM application: warning and coastal management
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-I 2025 Q7 (✅ owner 06 ledger).
**Links:** DM-128, DM-130, DM-132, DM-135
**Trap:** Do not relabel it a direct DM question. It is cross-paper.
**Mains hook:** Give formation to consequence, then one warning line. Qualify that deep-water and shoaling behaviour depend on local bathymetry.
**Back:** *"Displace, travel, shoal, inundate, warn: the 2025 chain."*

### Flashcard DM-151 — PYQ 2023 GS-I Q14 (coastline resources and hazard preparedness)
**Front:** What does the 2023 GS-I Q14 demand?
**Definition (learn):** Routed by owner 06 as "resource potential of India's coastline and hazard preparedness" (comment and highlight, 15 marks, 250 words) (✅ owner 06 Historical PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Coastal resources and hazard preparedness linked
- Use CRZ, bio-shields, warning chain
- Give a counterpoint on resource use versus protection
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** GS-I 2023 Q14 (✅ owner 06 ledger).
**Links:** DM-139, DM-138, DM-132, DM-149
**Trap:** Describing resources without linking them to preparedness.
**Mains hook:** Argue that coastal resource use must be regulated for hazard safety. Qualify with the need for CRZ compliance.
**Back:** *"Resources and readiness must share the same coast."*

### Flashcard DM-152 — Trap: the 2025 seawater-intrusion question is adjacent only
**Front:** Does the 2025 seawater-intrusion question test tsunami management?
**Definition (learn):** No. It is an adjacent-only cross-link to Geography and coastal-water management. It shares a coastal-vulnerability context but is not a direct tsunami question (✅ owner 06 Section 6; ✅ owner 00 Section 7).
**Breakdown:**
- Question: seawater intrusion in coastal aquifers
- Owner: Geography or Environment
- Shared theme: coastal vulnerability only
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2025 PYQ (✅ owner 06; ✅ owner 00).
**Links:** DM-150, DM-143
**Trap:** Force-fitting it as a tsunami PYQ.
**Mains hook:** Cite it only for shared coastal context. Qualify that its direct answer lies outside this folder.
**Back:** *"Seawater intrudes below ground; the tsunami comes from the sea floor."*

## DISASTER MANAGEMENT — Epidemics, Urban Resilience, Climate Risk, Disaster Finance, Humanitarian Logistics, Governance

### GROUP — 13 Epidemics and Public Health Emergencies (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard DM-201 — Biological disaster: definition and origin
**Front:** How does the DM framework define a biological disaster, and what are its two origins?
**Definition (learn):** Scenario of disease, disability or death on a large scale among humans, animals and plants caused by toxins or by live organisms or their products. Natural form = epidemic/pandemic; man-made form = biological warfare/bioterrorism.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: epidemics, pandemics (disease outbreak with natural origin).
- Man-made: biological warfare (use of biological agents as an act of war) and bioterrorism.
- Epidemic causes named in source: poor sanitation contaminating food/water, inadequate carcass disposal after disasters, risk spikes after floods/earthquakes, poor solid-waste management (plague example).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13, basic §2; VisionIAS PDF p.48)
**Links:** DM-202, DM-209, DM-215 (concept chain); topic 12 CBRN (DM-xxx of 12 owner, not in this fragment).
**Trap:** A "biological disaster" is not only infectious disease; toxins also count. Bioterrorism is a man-made hazard, not a natural epidemic, even though both are handled by the health system.
**Mains hook:** A GS-III answer on biological hazards must name both origins and then the health-system response. Qualified conclusion: the hazard is natural or deliberate, but the dominant determinant of harm is surveillance and health-system capacity, not the origin label.
**Back:** *"Nature or nurture of the bug: both are biological disasters, one is an accident of nature, one is an act of man."*

### Flashcard DM-202 — Four transmission categories of Indian epidemics
**Front:** Classify the four transmission routes of epidemics in India with one disease each.
**Definition (learn):** Epidemic diseases classified by their route of transmission, which determines the prevention lever.
**Breakdown:**
- Water-borne: cholera, typhoid, hepatitis A/B.
- Vector-borne: dengue, chikungunya, Japanese encephalitis, malaria, kala-azar.
- Person-to-person: AIDS, venereal diseases.
- Air-borne: influenza, measles (fomite-transmissible).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §2; PDF p.48)
**Links:** DM-203 (sanitation cause), DM-201, DM-216 (endemic vs epidemic).
**Trap:** Dengue is vector-borne, not water-borne, even though breeding in stagnant water links them; the prevention lever for vector-borne disease is vector control, not water treatment alone.
**Mains hook:** Use the transmission category to pick the lever: water-borne -> sanitation/treated water; vector-borne -> vector control and ecology; person-to-person -> contact and treatment; air-borne -> isolation, vaccination. Qualified conclusion: one generic "hygiene" message fails across categories.
**Back:** *"W-V-P-A: Water needs sanitation, Vectors need control, People need care, Air needs isolation."*

### Flashcard DM-203 — Why biological-weapon trends raise the threat
**Front:** Which factors make biological disasters/bioterrorism attractive as a threat?
**Definition (learn):** Features of biological agents that make them a distinct hazard from chemical or conventional attacks.
**Breakdown:**
- Low cost and wide availability.
- High efficiency per kilogram of payload.
- Biotechnology-enabled easy production.
- Use of natural pathogens that simulate existing disease (hard to attribute).
- Unmatched destructive potential.
- Long infection-to-symptom lag compared with chemical exposure (delays detection).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §2; PDF p.49)
**Links:** DM-201, DM-209 (MHA nodal for BW), topic 12 CBRN.
**Trap:** The incubation lag is a feature of detection, not of harm; a delayed symptom onset means surveillance must be early and syndromic, not symptom-triggered after mass onset.
**Mains hook:** Explain why bio-hazards need surveillance before symptoms cluster. Qualified conclusion: the threat is asymmetric and invisible for days, so preparedness is primarily about detection speed and laboratory capacity.
**Back:** *"Cheap, scalable, silent for days: the bio-threat is an invisible sprint."*

### Flashcard DM-204 — Epidemic Diseases Act, 1897: enacted, in force, amended
**Front:** What is the Epidemic Diseases Act, 1897, and what is its current legal status?
**Definition (learn):** Colonial-era sector law "to provide for the better prevention of the spread of Dangerous Epidemic Diseases," passed first for bubonic plague in Bombay.
**Breakdown:**
- Enacted: assent and commencement 4 February 1897 (Act No. 3 of 1897).
- Gives authorities special powers to contain epidemics.
- Amended by the Repealing and Amending Act 1914, the Devolution Act 1920 and the Epidemic Diseases (Amendment) Act 2020 (penalties for attacks on health workers).
- Owner-source (VisionIAS) says it "does not provide any power to the centre to intervene in biological emergencies" and "needs to be repealed".
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia "Epidemic Diseases Act, 1897", fetched: "In force (amended)"; ✅ owner 13 basic §4 and advanced §3 for the reform recommendation.)
**Links:** DM-205 (Concurrent List basis), DM-213 (COVID invoked DM Act instead), DM-212 (international layer).
**Trap:** Do not state that the 1897 Act has been repealed or replaced. The owner's "needs to be repealed" is a recommendation, and the fetched source shows it still in force. Any successor law needs a dated official source.
**Mains hook:** Use the 1897 Act to show a legal-design gap: a sector-specific colonial law without Centre-intervention power, so the DM Act was used instead. Qualified conclusion: the reform recommendation is valid, but the current statute is amended, not abolished.
**Back:** *"Ninety-seven, still in force, amended not abolished: check status before you say repealed."*

### Flashcard DM-205 — Legal basis for pandemic action: Concurrent List entries 23 and 29
**Front:** Which constitutional entries support central and State action on epidemic and disaster response?
**Definition (learn):** The Constitution does not use the word "disaster"; the DM Act 2005 draws on Concurrent List entries.
**Breakdown:**
- Entry 23 (Concurrent List): "Social security and social insurance" — the DM Act's stated basis.
- Entry 29 (Concurrent List): prevention of extension of infectious or contagious diseases or pests from one State to another, affecting men, animals or plants — "can also be used for specific law making".
- Health is a State subject under Schedule VII (State List), so primary responsibility lies with States.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §2, advanced §2; PDF p.52)
**Links:** DM-204, DM-206, DM-213.
**Trap:** Health being a State subject does not exclude central action: Entry 29 and the DM Act both enable it. Also, Entry 23 is not a health entry; relying on it for pandemic law was a legal choice, not an obvious one.
**Mains hook:** Argue that health is a State subject yet central action is constitutionally available through the concurrent list. Qualified conclusion: the legal basis for central pandemic action exists, but the question is proportionality and calibration, not existence of power.
**Back:** *"Twenty-three for the Act, twenty-nine for the spread: Concurrent List does the central lifting."*

### Flashcard DM-206 — Surat plague 1994 and avian influenza 2006: coordination lessons
**Front:** Which two Indian outbreaks are the owner's historical cases of coordination failure?
**Definition (learn):** Named precedents used to show that outbreak response needs continuous cross-sector coordination.
**Breakdown:**
- 1994 Surat plague outbreak.
- 2006 avian-influenza outbreak.
- Lesson: coordinate with animal health, home department, communication and media.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §3, §6; PDF p.51)
**Links:** DM-207 (structural gaps), DM-220 (One Health), DM-219 (advanced thesis).
**Trap:** Surat (1994) is a human plague case; avian influenza (2006) is a zoonotic case. Do not swap them as examples of the same type of spread.
**Mains hook:** Use the two cases to argue that coordination was identified as a lesson before COVID-19 and that the test is whether it was institutionalised. Qualified conclusion: repeated lessons without a standing mechanism mean the problem is recurrent rather than solved.
**Back:** *"Surat for the sewer-rat plague, 2006 for the bird flu: two warnings, one coordination lesson."*

### Flashcard DM-207 — Structural health-system gaps named for epidemics
**Front:** What structural vulnerabilities does the owner name for epidemic response in India?
**Definition (learn):** Standing gaps that predate any single outbreak and set the baseline for surge capacity.
**Breakdown:**
- Shortage of medical and paramedical staff at district and sub-district levels.
- Acute shortage of public-health specialists, epidemiologists, clinical microbiologists and virologists.
- Limited teaching and training institutions.
- Infrastructure gaps: biosafety laboratories, sub-centre/PHC/CHC networks, essential-medicine and vaccine stockpiles.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §3; PDF p.51. The "10-year-old" MoHFW contingency plan claim is document-period ⚠️.)
**Links:** DM-206, DM-209, DM-215.
**Trap:** Human-resource shortage is structural, not an outbreak surprise; a surge plan must cover the chronic baseline deficit, not only the emergency spike.
**Mains hook:** Argue that epidemic preparedness is a health-system capacity question first. Qualified conclusion: lockdowns do not substitute for trained district staff, laboratories and stockpiles.
**Back:** *"Staff, specialists, labs, stockpiles: the four pillars the outbreak finds missing."*

### Flashcard DM-208 — NDMA biological-disaster and medical-preparedness guidelines
**Front:** Name the two NDMA guidelines an answer should cite for biological hazards.
**Definition (learn):** National guidelines issued under the DM Act for biological and mass-casualty hazards.
**Breakdown:**
- Guidelines on Management of Biological Disasters — July 2008.
- Guidelines on Medical Preparedness and Mass Casualty Management — October 2007.
- Both are advisory guidelines under the DM Act, not statutory rules.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 13 basic §5, PDF p.51; NDMA guideline dates ⚠️ owner-cited, not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-205, DM-209, DM-214.
**Trap:** Name the specific document, not "NDMA guidelines". Guidelines are not enforceable regulations and do not prove district adoption.
**Mains hook:** Name the guideline when asserting preparedness. Qualified conclusion: guidance exists, but its implementation at district level needs separate evidence.
**Back:** *"Biology in July 2008, medical mass-casualty in October 2007: name both, claim neither as law."*

### Flashcard DM-209 — Nodal institutions for epidemics and biological warfare
**Front:** Which ministry and agency are nodal for epidemics, and which ministry is nodal for biological warfare?
**Definition (learn):** Institutional allocation of nodal responsibility in biological hazards.
**Breakdown:**
- Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW): nodal for epidemics; chief advisory/decision body.
- National Centre for Disease Control (NCDC): current nodal outbreak-investigation institution; owner names its predecessor NICD, which has been subsumed.
- Ministry of Home Affairs: nodal ministry for biological warfare.
- Health is a State subject (Schedule VII): States hold primary response responsibility.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §5; NICD-to-NCDC subsumption ⚠️ owner-stated, not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-205, DM-207, DM-210.
**Trap:** Use "NCDC", not "NICD", for a current reference. MoHFW is nodal for epidemics; MHA is nodal for biological warfare. Do not give MHA the epidemic role.
**Mains hook:** Map the nodal split precisely: MoHFW for disease, MHA for warfare, States for delivery. Qualified conclusion: nodal clarity helps, but cross-sector coordination still depends on a standing mechanism.
**Back:** *"Health handles the disease, Home handles the warfare, States deliver the care."*

### Flashcard DM-210 — Integrated Disease Surveillance Programme (IDSP) as current surveillance anchor
**Front:** What is the current anchor for India's outbreak-surveillance architecture?
**Definition (learn):** NCDC's Integrated Disease Surveillance Programme and its technical guidelines are the current official reference for outbreak surveillance.
**Breakdown:**
- Continuous collection, analysis and action on health data.
- Owner names IDSP and technical-guideline resources as the correct current anchor.
- Owner's own checking date: 18 July 2026.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 13 basic §9, advanced §10; IDSP details ⚠️ not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-209, DM-211, DM-212.
**Trap:** VisionIAS's "no policy on biological disasters" claim is document-period. Do not repeat it as current without NCDC or MoHFW verification.
**Mains hook:** Cite the IDSP for surveillance architecture, not the 2010-era VisionIAS description. Qualified conclusion: surveillance data is valuable only if it triggers timely testing and action.
**Back:** *"Surveillance is data, not action: IDSP reports, the clinic must respond."*

### Flashcard DM-211 — Amended International Health Regulations (2005): "pandemic emergency" tier
**Front:** What did the 2024 IHR amendments add, and when did they enter into force?
**Definition (learn):** Amendments to the International Health Regulations (2005) adopted to create a higher alert category above a PHEIC.
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted at WHA77 on 1 June 2024.
- Entered into force 19 September 2025 (owner-stated).
- New "pandemic emergency" category: communicable-disease event with, or at high risk of, wide international spread requiring coordinated international response.
- Sits above, not as a synonym for, a Public Health Emergency of International Concern (PHEIC).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 13 basic §5, §9; ⚠️ not fetched — verify with WHO IHR page before citing as current.)
**Links:** DM-212, DM-210, DM-215.
**Trap:** "Pandemic emergency" is not a PHEIC by another name; it is a higher tier. Do not claim India's acceptance status without a dated official source.
**Mains hook:** Use the IHR amendment as the international benchmark for alert thresholds. Qualified conclusion: the IHR changes adjusted an existing binding regime; they do not themselves guarantee national implementation.
**Back:** *"Pandemic emergency sits above PHEIC: a rung higher, not a new name."*

### Flashcard DM-212 — WHO Pandemic Agreement: adopted, not in force
**Front:** What is the status of the WHO Pandemic Agreement, and what does each stage require?
**Definition (learn):** A WHO instrument on pandemic prevention, preparedness and response, adopted by consensus but not yet binding.
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted at WHA78 on 20 May 2025 (owner-stated).
- Opens for signature only after the Pathogen Access and Benefit-Sharing (PABS) annex is adopted.
- Entry into force requires 60 ratifications.
- Owner says PABS negotiations were still continuing on 20 July 2026.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 13 basic §5, §9; ⚠️ WHO page fetch returned 404 — verify before asserting India's status.)
**Links:** DM-211, DM-218 (HEPR), DM-213.
**Trap:** Adoption, signature, ratification and entry into force are four distinct stages. Do not call the agreement binding or say India is bound.
**Mains hook:** Name PABS as the unresolved pathogen-access and benefit-sharing issue, which bites on sovereignty and vaccine equity. Qualified conclusion: the agreement is a framework in progress; its legal force depends on the annex and ratifications.
**Back:** *"Adopted, not in force: sign after the annex, ratify sixty times, then bind."*

### Flashcard DM-213 — COVID-19 as first pan-India test of the DM Act
**Front:** What did COVID-19 show about the DM Act's use for pandemics?
**Definition (learn):** The DM Act 2005, a generic disaster statute, was invoked for the first time for a nationwide lockdown in pandemic response.
**Breakdown:**
- NDMA directed Ministries, States and SDMAs to ensure social distancing to prevent the spread of COVID-19.
- Owner calls COVID-19 the first pan-India biological disaster handled by the legal and constitutional institutions of the country.
- The legal route used Entry 23 (DM Act basis), not a purpose-built public-health statute.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §3; PDF p.52.)
**Links:** DM-204, DM-205, DM-214 (2020 PYQ).
**Trap:** Do not say the DM Act was designed for epidemics; it was designed for disasters generically. "First time" refers to this invocation for nationwide lockdown.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the DM Act was a legal improvisation for pandemic response. Qualified conclusion: the improvisation worked for coordination, but a generic disaster law applies uniform measures to a health crisis that needs localised, graded calibration.
**Back:** *"A disaster Act for a pandemic: improvised, invoked, and uniform where calibration was needed."*

### Flashcard DM-214 — 2020 PYQ: technology in COVID-19 management
**Front:** How should a 15-mark answer on technology in COVID-19 management be structured?
**Definition (learn):** Technology is useful only when embedded in test-trace-treat-vaccinate and health-system capacity.
**Breakdown:**
- Diagnosis: RT-PCR confirms RNA-virus infection; laboratories.
- Surveillance, contact and risk communication: IDSP and Aarogya Setu (self-assessment and contact-tracing functions).
- Delivery: CoWIN supported vaccination registration, scheduling and certificates (vaccine phase only).
- Limits: digital divide, privacy and data quality, staff and bed/oxygen continuity.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §13; 2020 GS-III Q15 "Technology in COVID-19 pandemic management and advancements", 15 marks, 250 words, PDF p.51-52.)
**Links:** DM-210, DM-213, DM-207.
**Trap:** Do not write an app-list answer. An app is not a substitute for public-health workers or care. CoWIN is a vaccine-phase example, not evidence that all 2020 control problems were solved.
**Mains hook:** Structure as diagnosis, surveillance, delivery and limits, ending with a public-health verdict. Qualified conclusion: technology helps only where health staff, laboratories and essential care continue to function.
**Back:** *"Test, trace, deliver, limit: four stations, one public-health verdict, no app-list."*

### Flashcard DM-215 — Concept: epidemic chain and public-health response sequence
**Front:** What is the causal chain for an epidemic and the standard response sequence?
**Definition (learn):** An epidemic results from a pathogen meeting a susceptible population through a transmission route, amplified by mobility and weak health systems.
**Breakdown:**
- Chain: pathogen + susceptible population + transmission route + mobility/contact + health-system vulnerability.
- Response: surveillance -> testing/diagnosis -> risk communication -> isolation/treatment -> vaccination or medical countermeasure where available -> surge capacity -> continuity of essential care.
- Prevention/mitigation: cooked food, boiled/chlorinated water, vector and rodent control, isolation, lab networks, mass immunisation (owner PDF p.49).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §1, §4; REVISION-CHART §17.)
**Links:** DM-202, DM-210, DM-216, DM-218.
**Trap:** Response is not only case treatment; continuity of essential non-COVID care is a measured outcome.
**Mains hook:** Trace the chain to show where intervention is cheapest. Qualified conclusion: early surveillance and continuity of care matter more than late, uniform lockdown.
**Back:** *"Pathogen, host, route, movement, weakness: break one link early, not the whole economy late."*

### Flashcard DM-216 — Endemic, epidemic and pandemic; quarantine and isolation
**Front:** Distinguish endemic, epidemic and pandemic, and quarantine from isolation.
**Definition (learn):** Standard epidemiological terms used to describe disease spread and public-health measures.
**Breakdown:**
- Endemic: expected persistent presence in an area or population.
- Epidemic: occurrence above the expected level.
- Pandemic: epidemic spreading across countries and continents.
- Quarantine: separation of people who may have been exposed.
- Isolation: separation of people already infected.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ REVISION-CHART §17 and owner 13 basic §4.)
**Links:** DM-202, DM-215, DM-211 (pandemic emergency tier).
**Trap:** Quarantine applies to exposed, possibly uninfected persons; isolation applies to infected cases. Calling every restriction "quarantine" is the common error.
**Mains hook:** Specify which measure applies to whom; blanket lockdown conflates the two. Qualified conclusion: targeted quarantine and isolation are more proportionate than uniform movement bans.
**Back:** *"Quarantine for the might-have, isolation for the has."*

### Flashcard DM-217 — COVID-19 technology: diagnosis, tracing and vaccination functions
**Front:** Which technology supported which COVID-19 function?
**Definition (learn):** A function-by-function mapping of technology to public-health tasks, used to avoid app-centric answers.
**Breakdown:**
- Diagnosis: RT-PCR (molecular confirmation).
- Contact tracing and self-assessment: Aarogya Setu.
- Vaccination registration, scheduling, certificates: CoWIN.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 13 basic §13.1; app functions as stated by owner.)
**Links:** DM-214, DM-210.
**Trap:** Test, trace and deliver are different functions; one app cannot replace them. Digital access and data privacy limit reach.
**Mains hook:** Map each tool to its function, then state equity limits. Qualified conclusion: digital tools amplify public-health workers; they do not replace them.
**Back:** *"Test with RT-PCR, trace with Setu, deliver with CoWIN: one tool, one job."*

### Flashcard DM-218 — WHO HEPR framework: international benchmark
**Front:** What is the WHO Health Emergency Prevention, Preparedness, Response and Resilience (HEPR) framework, and why cite it?
**Definition (learn):** WHO's framework for health-emergency preparedness, reported to WHA78 in May 2025, used to benchmark national preparedness.
**Breakdown:**
- Reported to WHA78 (May 2025) per owner.
- Use as international benchmark for India's post-COVID surveillance reforms.
- Owner says older VisionIAS material predates it.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 13 advanced §5; ⚠️ not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-211, DM-212, DM-210.
**Trap:** Do not present VisionIAS's material as reflecting HEPR; it does not.
**Mains hook:** Use HEPR to ask whether India's surveillance meets a global standard. Qualified conclusion: a framework is a benchmark, not evidence of national implementation.
**Back:** *"HEPR is the yardstick, not the scorecard: benchmark, don't boast."*

### Flashcard DM-219 — Advanced: legal-improvisation thesis for pandemic governance
**Front:** What is the advanced thesis on India's pandemic legal architecture?
**Definition (learn):** The central analytical claim that India used a generic disaster law because the sector-specific law was obsolete, and the mismatch matters.
**Breakdown:**
- Sector-specific law (Epidemic Diseases Act 1897) lacked Centre-intervention power (owner's view).
- Generic DM Act 2005 invoked via Entry 23 (Entry 29 available but not the basis).
- Result: uniform national measures for a health crisis that needs graded, local calibration.
- Analytical lesson: the legal design choice itself, not only the lockdown, is the finding.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ advanced analysis of owner 13 advanced §1-2; sources as DM-204, DM-205, DM-213.)
**Links:** DM-204, DM-205, DM-213, DM-220.
**Trap:** Do not argue that the EDA 1897 was repealed; it is in force but amended. Do not claim the DM Act was a failure without outcome evidence.
**Mains hook:** Argue the legal-improvisation pattern and the calibration trade-off. Qualified conclusion: a generic disaster Act can coordinate, but a purpose-built, graded public-health law is the institutional gap to close.
**Back:** *"Borrowed law, blunt measures: the Act fitted the disaster, not the disease."*

### Flashcard DM-220 — Advanced: One Health and the zoonotic-spillover interface
**Front:** What does One Health add to the avian-influenza example?
**Definition (learn):** One Health is the integrated approach to human, animal and environmental health; the owner's 2006 avian-influenza case is a zoonotic-spillover example.
**Breakdown:**
- Human-animal-environment interface.
- Owner's 2006 avian-flu lesson required coordination with animal health.
- Advanced answer names it One Health, using current terminology.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ analysis of owner 13 advanced §4; terminology from REVISION-CHART §17.)
**Links:** DM-206, DM-215, DM-209.
**Trap:** One Health is an approach, not a single agency; do not treat it as a named ministry.
**Mains hook:** Use One Health to link zoonoses to animal and environmental health. Qualified conclusion: coordination must be institutionalised across three health domains, not merely across sectors.
**Back:** *"One health, three domains: human, animal, environment, one coordinated line."*

### Flashcard DM-221 — Advanced: public-health calibration versus enforcement
**Front:** What trade-off does the advanced file name between disease control and enforcement?
**Definition (learn):** Generic enforcement (lockdown, movement restriction) versus epidemiologically graded measures (targeted testing, contact tracing, localised containment).
**Breakdown:**
- Uniform enforcement is simple to command but less proportionate.
- Graded measures are more precise but demand data and local capacity.
- Aggressive containment can disrupt essential non-outbreak health services.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ analysis of owner 13 advanced §7.)
**Links:** DM-213, DM-216, DM-219.
**Trap:** Do not conclude that lockdowns were wrong; state the trade-off and the evidence test.
**Mains hook:** Compare uniform and graded measures on proportionality, cost and service continuity. Qualified conclusion: proportionality requires surveillance data; without it, uniform measures are the default, not the optimum.
**Back:** *"Uniform is easy, graded is precise: pick the measure the data can defend."*

### Flashcard DM-222 — Advanced: PABS as the sovereignty and equity bind
**Front:** Why is pathogen access and benefit-sharing (PABS) the stalled issue in the Pandemic Agreement?
**Definition (learn):** The unresolved annex governing who supplies pathogen samples and who receives vaccines or products made from them.
**Breakdown:**
- IHR amendments succeeded because they adjust an existing regime's thresholds.
- The Pandemic Agreement stalls where sovereignty and equity meet: sample access and benefit-sharing.
- India is both a large vaccine manufacturer and a sample-supplying country.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ analysis of owner 13 advanced §10; PABS status per DM-212.)
**Links:** DM-211, DM-212, DM-218.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the stall to vague "geopolitical differences"; name PABS precisely.
**Mains hook:** Name PABS and explain India's dual interest. Qualified conclusion: the agreement will bind only when benefit-sharing is credible to supplier and recipient countries alike.
**Back:** *"Samples in, vaccines out: the bargain that stalls is who gets what."*

### GROUP — 14 Urban and Critical Infrastructure Resilience (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard DM-223 — Disaster-resilient infrastructure: structural and non-structural measures
**Front:** Define disaster-resilient infrastructure and list its two measure types.
**Definition (learn):** Infrastructure designed to withstand significant damage from natural disasters, using both engineering and governance measures.
**Breakdown:**
- Structural: flood control systems, protective embankments, seawall rehabilitation, building retrofitting.
- Non-structural: risk-sensitive planning, institutional frameworks, hazard mapping, ecosystem-based management, disaster-risk financing.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §1-2; PDF p.64.)
**Links:** DM-224, DM-225, DM-228 (UFC), topic 08 flood tools.
**Trap:** "Resilient" does not mean only physical hardening; planning, mapping and finance are part of the definition.
**Mains hook:** Argue that resilience is structural plus non-structural. Qualified conclusion: physical strength without planning and finance will fail under the next fragmented event.
**Back:** *"Build strong, plan long: structure plus non-structure, both count."*

### Flashcard DM-224 — CDRI: launch and purpose
**Front:** What is the Coalition for Disaster Resilient Infrastructure (CDRI), when and where was it launched, and what does it do?
**Definition (learn):** An international coalition launched by India to build common infrastructure-resilience standards, financing and compliance mechanisms, and R&D that shapes multilateral-bank investment.
**Breakdown:**
- Launched by India at the UN Climate Action Summit, New York, 23 September 2019 (owner-stated; the fetched CDRI About page confirms the 2019 Summit link but not the day).
- Purpose: common standards in infrastructure building; financial and compliance mechanisms; R&D influencing funding from multilateral banks.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §2; PDF p.64. ⚠️ launch date and venue not confirmed by cdri.world fetch.)
**Links:** DM-225, DM-226, DM-227, DM-310.
**Trap:** CDRI is an international coalition, not a domestic urban-planning body. Its membership is an influence metric, not an outcome.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish CDRI's international standard-setting role from domestic city-level coordination. Qualified conclusion: CDRI shapes investment criteria, but city resilience depends on domestic authorities.
**Back:** *"Two-three-nineteen in New York: India's coalition sets standards for the world's investors."*

### Flashcard DM-225 — CDRI membership and secretariat status
**Front:** What are CDRI's current membership, partner and secretariat facts?
**Definition (learn):** The official CDRI About page reports membership and partner counts as separate categories.
**Breakdown:**
- 59 member countries (fetched cdri.world/about).
- 13 partner organisations (fetched).
- Secretariat headquartered in New Delhi, holding International Organization status (fetched).
- Do not add the two categories into a single total.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ cdri.world/about, fetched; owner 14 basic §2, §7.)
**Links:** DM-224, DM-226.
**Trap:** Partner organisations are not members, and no combined total is valid. Do not state membership as evidence of resilient infrastructure.
**Mains hook:** Use the membership count only as evidence of convening power. Qualified conclusion: membership demonstrates influence on standards, not that standards are implemented.
**Back:** *"Fifty-nine members, thirteen partners, one Delhi secretariat: never add them into one number."*

### Flashcard DM-226 — CDRI's four thematic areas
**Front:** List CDRI's four thematic areas.
**Definition (learn):** The four workstreams through which CDRI operates.
**Breakdown:**
- Disaster risk assessment: time-series hazard data and probabilistic risk models.
- Standards of design and implementation: codes reflecting evolving hazard understanding.
- Financing new infrastructure and risk-covering mechanisms: budget reserve funds and catastrophe bonds.
- Reconstruction and recovery: Build Back Better for structural design and management systems.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §2; PDF p.64.)
**Links:** DM-224, DM-238, DM-269 (BBB), DM-258 (finance), DM-223.
**Trap:** Catastrophe bonds and reserve funds are named tools, not evidence of Indian deployment. Do not claim wide domestic use without a dated source.
**Mains hook:** Cite the four areas precisely, then test which one an Indian project actually uses. Qualified conclusion: risk assessment must precede finance; finance without data is blind.
**Back:** *"Assess, standardise, finance, rebuild: the four CDRI rungs in order."*

### Flashcard DM-227 — CDRI flagship initiatives (IRIS, global report, DRI Connect)
**Front:** What flagship initiatives does CDRI run?
**Definition (learn):** Named CDRI programmes beyond the four thematic areas.
**Breakdown:**
- DRI Connect: knowledge-exchange platform for resilient-infrastructure professionals (confirmed as a CDRI link on fetched page).
- Global infrastructure resilience report: biennial report, first edition October 2023 (owner-stated; fetched page lists GIRI, not the full title).
- IRIS (Infrastructure for Resilient Island States): owner-stated; not confirmed on the fetched page.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §2; ⚠️ IRIS and the report title not confirmed by cdri.world fetch.)
**Links:** DM-224, DM-225.
**Trap:** The report name appears as GIRI on the fetched page; use the owner's exact name only with a source check. Do not claim IRIS scope without confirmation.
**Mains hook:** Name flagship programmes only when verified. Qualified conclusion: knowledge platforms support standards but do not guarantee adoption.
**Back:** *"DRI Connect knows, the report counts, IRIS helps islands: verify the names before you cite them."*

### Flashcard DM-228 — Urban Flooding Cell: recommended, not self-executing
**Front:** What is the Urban Flooding Cell (UFC), and what is its legal status?
**Definition (learn):** A national coordination cell recommended in NDMA's 2010 Guidelines on Management of Urban Flooding, to sit within the urban-development ministry, with ULBs managing locally.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: NDMA Guidelines on Management of Urban Flooding (2010).
- National coordination at the urban-development ministry level.
- Local implementation by urban local bodies.
- Existence and operation need a current MoHUA or State notification.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §2, §5; PDF p.31.)
**Links:** DM-229 (UFRMP), DM-230 (UDMA), DM-224.
**Trap:** A 2010 guideline recommendation is not an operating body. Do not claim UFC exists in every city.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish recommended coordination from constituted coordination. Qualified conclusion: the cell is necessary but the test is whether it has notified powers and funds.
**Back:** *"Recommended in 2010, constituted by notification: the cell is a promise until you see the order."*

### Flashcard DM-229 — Urban Flood Risk Management Programme (UFRMP)
**Front:** What is the Urban Flood Risk Management Programme, and what sanctions has it had?
**Definition (learn):** A sanctioned mitigation programme financed from the National Disaster Mitigation Fund for urban flood risk in named cities.
**Breakdown:**
- Chennai: sanctioned 7 December 2023.
- Six more cities: 25 July 2024.
- Phase 2: 11 cities, Rs 2,444.42 crore, 90:10 sharing, approved 1 October 2025 (owner-stated; owner also cites MHA parliamentary replies February 2026).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 14 basic §7, §9; owner 16 advanced §2. ⚠️ figures not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-228, DM-230, DM-255 (NDMF mitigation).
**Trap:** Sanction is not completion or avoided loss. Do not confuse UFRMP (programme, NDMF-funded) with UFC (recommended coordination cell) or UDMA (enabling authority).
**Mains hook:** Use UFRMP as an example of ex-ante finance, then test whether works were completed. Qualified conclusion: sanctions show intent; resilience needs delivery and maintenance evidence.
**Back:** *"Sanctioned is not built: UFRMP funds intent, and only completed drains reduce loss."*

### Flashcard DM-230 — Urban Disaster Management Authority (s. 41A, DM Amendment Act 2025)
**Front:** What does s. 41A of the Disaster Management Act provide for urban areas, and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** The 2025 amendment enables an Urban Disaster Management Authority (UDMA) for urban areas, chaired by the Municipal Commissioner with the District Collector as Vice-Chairperson.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner-stated: UDMA prepares an Urban Plan, approved by the SDMA.
- Delhi and Chandigarh are excluded.
- It is enabling: a UDMA exists only where a State has notified one.
- Owner-stated: MHA cited Karnataka's BBMP UDMA on 11 February 2026.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 14 basic §7, §9; ⚠️ s. 41A not confirmed by the fetched Wikipedia summary of the Act.)
**Links:** DM-231 (Amendment Act 2025), DM-228, DM-234.
**Trap:** Statutory enabling power is not an operating authority. Do not say every city now has a UDMA.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish enabling power, notification and operation. Qualified conclusion: the provision creates the route to a single urban authority, but its value depends on notified, funded UDMAs.
**Back:** *"Enabled is not established: a UDMA exists only where a State has notified one."*

### Flashcard DM-231 — Disaster Management (Amendment) Act, 2025: dates and status
**Front:** What are the commencement facts of the Disaster Management (Amendment) Act, 2025?
**Definition (learn):** The 2025 amendment to the DM Act 2005 that adds urban authority, database and State force provisions.
**Breakdown:**
- Act No. 10 of 2025.
- Gazetted 29 March 2025 (confirmed by Wikipedia, Act page).
- Lok Sabha passed 12 December 2024; Rajya Sabha 25 March 2025 (Wikipedia).
- Owner says in force from 9 April 2025.
- Stated aims: create a disaster database and look at urban disaster and development (Wikipedia).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia "Disaster Management Act, 2005" fetched; 9 April 2025 commencement and section numbers ⚠️ owner-stated.)
**Links:** DM-230, DM-266, DM-262.
**Trap:** Owner says there is no s. 9B. Do not cite the amendment's section numbers without the official text; Wikipedia does not confirm s. 41A or s. 44A.
**Mains hook:** Use the Act as the current legal anchor, then check operational rollout. Qualified conclusion: the amendment strengthens the architecture, but notified implementation is the test.
**Back:** *"Ten of twenty-five, gazetted in March: the Act is law, the rollout is the question."*

### Flashcard DM-232 — NDMA crowd-management guide (mass gatherings), May 2014
**Front:** What is NDMA's governing document for crowd disasters at mass gatherings?
**Definition (learn):** An NDMA guide on managing crowds at events and venues of mass gathering, addressed to State governments, local authorities and organisers.
**Breakdown:**
- Title: Managing Crowd at Events and Venues of Mass Gathering — A Guide for State Governments, Local Authorities, Administrators and Organizers.
- Date: May 2014.
- Covers causes and triggers, advance planning, safety standards and SOPs, technology, media communication.
- Not one of the numbered National Disaster Management Guidelines; it is advisory in form.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §2, §9.)
**Links:** DM-233 (flow-capacity logic), DM-234.
**Trap:** Guide is not an enforceable venue regime. Do not call it a numbered national guideline.
**Mains hook:** Argue that crowd safety is a flow-design problem, not only weather or hazard forecasting. Qualified conclusion: guidance without enforceable capacity limits leaves safety to operators after incidents.
**Back:** *"Crowd guide, May 2014: advice for organisers, not a venue law."*

### Flashcard DM-233 — New Delhi railway station crowd disaster: response measures
**Front:** What measures did the Ministry of Railways report after the New Delhi Railway Station crowd disaster?
**Definition (learn):** Operator-led flow-design measures reported in response to a stampede at a lifeline station.
**Breakdown:**
- Reported deaths 18 and injuries 15 (owner-stated, PIB 19 March 2025).
- Measures: holding areas, controlled access, CCTV and war-room monitoring, wider foot-over-bridges, capacity-linked ticketing, strengthened station-director powers.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 14 basic §2, §3; PIB 19 March 2025 ⚠️ not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-232, DM-234.
**Trap:** Do not quote casualty figures for other crowd incidents without a dated government source. Operator measures are incident-triggered, not a standing enforceable regime.
**Mains hook:** Use the measures as evidence of flow-design logic. Qualified conclusion: the lasting fix is a standing capacity regime, not post-incident hardening.
**Back:** *"Holding, control, watch, widen, cap: five flow fixes after the crush."*

### Flashcard DM-234 — Five-city urban flood evidence base
**Front:** Which Indian cities form the owner's evidence set for urban-flood infrastructure failure, with years?
**Definition (learn):** Named urban flood events where lifeline services (transport, power, water) failed.
**Breakdown:**
- Mumbai: 2005 and 2017.
- Surat: 2006.
- Kashmir: 2014.
- Chennai: 2015.
- Delhi: 2023.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §6; PDF p.30.)
**Links:** DM-235 (cascade), DM-233, topic 08 cross-link.
**Trap:** Kashmir (2014) is a riverine-regional event, not a Mumbai-type urban pluvial case; keep the category when citing.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the cascade recurs across distinct Indian cities. Qualified conclusion: recurrence shows a governance pattern, not isolated bad luck.
**Back:** *"Mumbai twice, Surat, Kashmir, Chennai, Delhi: five cities, one recurring cascade."*

### Flashcard DM-235 — Cascading lifeline failure in cities
**Front:** How does a single hazard turn into cross-system urban failure?
**Definition (learn):** Interdependent lifelines fail in sequence when one node is disrupted, and each agency restores its system separately.
**Breakdown:**
- Chain: power loss -> water pumping and hospital equipment fail -> transport blocks emergency response -> telecom failure blocks warning.
- Recovery is fragmented when no single authority maps criticality.
- Lifelines: electricity, telecom, water and sanitation, transport, health facilities.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 advanced §2; REVISION-CHART §18.)
**Links:** DM-236, DM-234, DM-241.
**Trap:** Cascades are not only physical: information and investment fragmentation cause them too.
**Mains hook:** Argue for systemic protection over facility-by-facility hardening. Qualified conclusion: criticality mapping and a coordinating authority matter more than any single asset's strength.
**Back:** *"Power falls, water stops, roads jam, signals die: one fall, five failures."*

### Flashcard DM-236 — Urban governance: fragmented investment and coordination gaps
**Front:** What urban-flood challenges does the owner list?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's own list of structural urban-governance failures.
**Breakdown:**
- Inadequate comprehensive risk assessment.
- Ignored hazard-factor mapping in development planning.
- Unsatisfactory coordination among institutions.
- Lack of information sharing.
- Disintegrated investment decisions.
- Lack of stakeholder consultation.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §3; PDF p.31.)
**Links:** DM-235, DM-237, DM-223.
**Trap:** Institutional existence does not fix these gaps; a cell or authority on paper does not resolve information sharing.
**Mains hook:** Use these six gaps as the diagnostic list in any urban-resilience answer. Qualified conclusion: the central failure is governance and investment fragmentation more than engineering knowledge.
**Back:** *"Assess, map, coordinate, share, invest, consult: six gaps, one fragmented city."*

### Flashcard DM-237 — Urban Flooding Cell versus UFRMP versus UDMA: status distinction
**Front:** How do the urban-flood institutions differ in status?
**Definition (learn):** Three urban-flood instruments at different legal levels: guideline recommendation, sanctioned programme, enabling authority.
**Breakdown:**
- UFC: NDMA 2010 guideline recommendation; needs notification to exist.
- UFRMP: NDMF-financed sanctioned mitigation programme; sanctions are project-level.
- UDMA: statutory enabling power under s. 41A; exists only where notified.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 advanced §3, §10; REVISION-CHART §29 status rule.)
**Links:** DM-228, DM-229, DM-230.
**Trap:** Do not present any of the three as operating coverage without a dated notification or disbursement record.
**Mains hook:** Name the status of each institution precisely. Qualified conclusion: the governance gap is status and implementation, not only the existence of the instrument.
**Back:** *"Recommended, sanctioned, enabled: three rungs, check each before you claim."*

### Flashcard DM-238 — Build Back Better for management systems, not only structures
**Front:** What does Build Back Better require for infrastructure recovery?
**Definition (learn):** BBB applies to management systems around infrastructure as well as to physical design.
**Breakdown:**
- CDRI's BBB principle includes management systems (owner, PDF p.64).
- Implication: consolidate fragmented utility oversight during reconstruction.
- Harder and slower than rebuilding the physical asset identically.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §4; owner 14 advanced §7.)
**Links:** DM-226, DM-269, DM-290.
**Trap:** Rebuilding to the old design is recovery, not Build Back Better.
**Mains hook:** Argue that resilient reconstruction must redesign governance, not only structures. Qualified conclusion: physical rebuilding without management reform repeats the fragmentation that failed.
**Back:** *"Rebuild the pipes and the paperwork: BBB covers management, not just masonry."*

### Flashcard DM-239 — Retrofit versus new-build resilience trade-off (urban)
**Front:** Why is retrofitting existing lifelines the harder priority?
**Definition (learn):** The urban analogue of the earthquake retrofit debate: standards are easier to impose on new construction than on existing networks.
**Breakdown:**
- New-build standards are administratively easier.
- Existing aged water, power and transport networks are costlier to retrofit.
- Owner says existing-stock retrofit is the higher-priority, higher-cost, politically difficult task.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ analysis of owner 14 advanced §7; links to topic 05.)
**Links:** DM-235, DM-223.
**Trap:** Do not treat a new-build code as protection for the existing city stock.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish new-build compliance from existing-stock retrofit. Qualified conclusion: the most vulnerable assets are usually the oldest, so retrofit must be prioritised.
**Back:** *"New builds comply, old pipes fail: retrofit the stock that is already dangerous."*

### Flashcard DM-240 — Informal settlements and lifeline inequity
**Front:** How does lifeline failure distribute unevenly across a city?
**Definition (learn):** Least-resilient lifeline versions often serve the poorest neighbourhoods.
**Breakdown:**
- Informal settlements may have older drainage, intermittent power and informal water access.
- Cascading failure compounds pre-existing social vulnerability (topic 03).
- Owner-implied, not detailed in source.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ analysis of owner 14 advanced §6.)
**Links:** DM-235, DM-239.
**Trap:** Do not assume lifeline failure is uniform across a city.
**Mains hook:** Argue equity as part of resilience. Qualified conclusion: city resilience is measured by its weakest neighbourhood's service continuity.
**Back:** *"The weakest lifeline serves the weakest street: measure resilience there first."*

### Flashcard DM-241 — Urban disaster verdict: service continuity, not money sanctioned
**Front:** What outcome test should an urban-resilience answer apply?
**Definition (learn):** Measure achieved service continuity and reduced loss, not only funds announced or infrastructure sanctioned.
**Breakdown:**
- Sanctions are inputs; service continuity and reduced loss are outcomes.
- The owner's infrastructure chain: criticality mapping, redundancy, resilient codes, continuity plans, rapid repair.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 14 basic §13.2 executable spine.)
**Links:** DM-229, DM-235, DM-269.
**Trap:** A sanctioned project and a membership are not outcomes.
**Mains hook:** End with a verdict on continuity and loss, not allocation. Qualified conclusion: urban resilience is real only when lifelines keep working under the next event.
**Back:** *"Sanctioned is input, working is outcome: judge the continuity, not the cheque."*

### GROUP — 15 Climate Risk, Adaptation and Loss and Damage (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard DM-242 — Slow-onset disasters ("creeping emergencies")
**Front:** Which hazards does the owner class as slow-onset disasters, and why does it matter for preparedness?
**Definition (learn):** Disasters that unfold over months or years, predictable well in advance, whose impact is not felt immediately.
**Breakdown:**
- Named in source: climate change, desertification, soil degradation and drought.
- Also called "Creeping Emergencies".
- Contrast: rapid-onset hazards (earthquake, cyclone, flood, tsunami).
- Slow onset means longer lead time for anticipatory investment, but low political salience.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §1; PDF p.6.)
**Links:** DM-245 (avoid-adapt-address), DM-251 (climate justice), topic 09 drought.
**Trap:** Not every disaster is climate-caused. Only slow-onset hazards and some intensified rapid-onset hazards are climate-linked; earthquake and tsunami have non-climatic causes.
**Mains hook:** Argue that slow-onset risk needs anticipatory social protection and livelihood planning, not only emergency relief after a declaration. Qualified conclusion: lead time is an advantage only if institutions act before the visible shock.
**Back:** *"Creeping, not crashing: the longest warning, the easiest to ignore."*

### Flashcard DM-243 — Warsaw International Mechanism on Loss and Damage
**Front:** What is the Warsaw International Mechanism on Loss and Damage, and what are its three functions?
**Definition (learn):** A UNFCCC mechanism, established at COP19 (2013), addressing climate-change impacts (extreme and slow-onset events) in vulnerable developing countries.
**Breakdown:**
- Anchored in Paris Agreement Article 8 (averting, minimising and addressing loss and damage).
- Function 1: enhance knowledge and understanding of comprehensive risk-management approaches.
- Function 2: strengthen dialogue, coordination, coherence and synergies among stakeholders.
- Function 3: enhance action and support (finance, technology, capacity-building).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §2, §5; PDF pp.64-65.)
**Links:** DM-244 (Suva), DM-245, DM-249 (Loss and Damage Fund).
**Trap:** The three functions are sequenced (know, coordinate, act). Criticism typically targets the third, action and support, not the first two.
**Mains hook:** Use the three functions to show where delivery lags. Qualified conclusion: the mechanism is mature in knowledge and coordination, less so in finance, so the test is action and support.
**Back:** *"Know, coordinate, act: the Warsaw ladder stalls at the third rung."*

### Flashcard DM-244 — Suva Expert Dialogue on Loss and Damage (COP23)
**Front:** What is the Suva Expert Dialogue, and where was it decided?
**Definition (learn):** A dialogue mechanism decided at COP23 (Bonn) after developing countries demanded a separate loss-and-damage agenda item.
**Breakdown:**
- Decided at COP23 (Bonn).
- Facilitates mobilising expertise and enhanced support (finance, technology, capacity-building) for loss-and-damage response.
- A developing-country advocacy outcome.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §2, §5; PDF p.64.)
**Links:** DM-243, DM-249.
**Trap:** A dialogue is not a fund. Existence of a dialogue does not deliver finance.
**Mains hook:** Argue that dialogue without finance is advocacy, not remedy. Qualified conclusion: Suva builds expertise; delivery depends on the action pillar.
**Back:** *"Suva talks, Warsaw counts: a dialogue is not a fund."*

### Flashcard DM-245 — Avoid, adapt, address: the three climate-disaster pillars
**Front:** Explain the avoid-adapt-address sequence in climate-disaster governance.
**Definition (learn):** Three distinct responses to climate risk, each covering the residual left by the previous one.
**Breakdown:**
- Avoid (mitigation): reduce future climate risk generation.
- Adapt: build resilience to unavoidable residual risk.
- Address: loss and damage, where avoid and adapt measures fail.
- Chain: climate-linked intensification -> avoid -> adapt -> address -> displacement gap (advanced).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §3; advanced §2.)
**Links:** DM-242, DM-243, DM-250.
**Trap:** Insurance, relief and adaptation are not synonyms. Each pillar addresses a different residual.
**Mains hook:** Structure any climate-disaster answer on this sequence. Qualified conclusion: the "address" pillar is the least mature; avoid and adapt cannot eliminate residual loss.
**Back:** *"Avoid the cause, adapt the cushion, address the rest: three pillars, no synonyms."*

### Flashcard DM-246 — Disaster displacement: scale and the refugee-status gap
**Front:** What do the owner's figures say about disaster displacement, and why are displaced persons not automatically refugees?
**Definition (learn):** Displacement caused by natural disasters, estimated at about 13.9 million people per year (document-period owner figure).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: about 13.9 million displaced annually; eight of the ten most disaster-prone countries in South and South-East Asia.
- The 1951 Refugee Convention's definition is persecution-based; disaster displacement does not fit.
- Protection depends on domestic law, human-rights obligations and non-binding displacement guidance, not a binding disaster-displacement treaty.
- Named harms: humanitarian discrimination, gender-based violence, extremism-recruitment susceptibility, higher casualties in poorer countries.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §2, §3; PDF p.63. The 13.9 million figure is ⚠️ document-period.)
**Links:** DM-245, DM-251, DM-254.
**Trap:** Displacement is not absence of rights; the gap is in legal category. Do not quote 13.9 million as current without a UNDRR or IDMC source.
**Mains hook:** Name the legal-category gap precisely. Qualified conclusion: domestic protection law is the practical route while the international gap persists.
**Back:** *"Refugee Convention wants persecution, disaster leaves home: a gap in category, not in rights."*

### Flashcard DM-247 — Ecosystem-based DRR (Eco-DRR): examples from the source
**Front:** Which ecosystem measures does the owner link to hazard buffering?
**Definition (learn):** Using natural assets (mangroves, forests, dunes, wetlands, reefs, slopes) as disaster buffers, where adaptation and DRR overlap.
**Breakdown:**
- 2004 tsunami: mangroves, coastal forests, sand dunes and cliffs "provided the best natural barriers"; heavy damage where dunes were mined.
- Tsunami mitigation: bio-shields, mangrove and coastal-forest plantation, coral reefs.
- Cyclone mitigation: saline embankments and coastal bio-shields.
- Flood mitigation: catchment-area treatment and afforestation.
- Non-structural resilient-infrastructure measure: ecosystem-based management.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §5, §7; PDF pp.23-24, 28-29, 36-37, 64.)
**Links:** DM-248, DM-253, DM-223.
**Trap:** The owner does not name the category "Eco-DRR"; the term is standard. Ecosystem measures do not replace engineering in every case.
**Mains hook:** Argue that a single asset delivers buffering, carbon storage and livelihoods. Qualified conclusion: Eco-DRR is the clearest adaptation-DRR convergence, but its benefits need a valuation method to compete with engineered assets.
**Back:** *"Mangrove, dune, reef, forest: nature buffers the hit and feeds the household."*

### Flashcard DM-248 — Wetland restoration in Assam funded as disaster mitigation
**Front:** What mitigation-finance example does the owner give for Eco-DRR?
**Definition (learn):** A sanctioned ecosystem-restoration project booked under disaster mitigation rather than an environment programme.
**Breakdown:**
- Amount: Rs 519.04 crore from the National Disaster Mitigation Fund.
- Purpose: restoration and rejuvenation of 24 wetlands in Assam.
- Source: MHA Lok Sabha reply, 2 December 2025 (owner-stated).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 15 basic §5; ⚠️ not fetched; owner 16 advanced §2.)
**Links:** DM-247, DM-258, DM-263.
**Trap:** Approval is not evidence of restored wetland performance or avoided loss.
**Mains hook:** Use the example to show the test: did the wetland reduce loss? Qualified conclusion: funding a wetland as mitigation makes it accountable to a risk test, not only an ecological one.
**Back:** *"Five-eighteen crore for twenty-four wetlands: sanctioned, not yet proven."*

### Flashcard DM-249 — Loss and Damage Fund: later institutional development
**Front:** What does the advanced file say about the Loss and Damage Fund, and what must be verified?
**Definition (learn):** A later climate-finance instrument for loss and damage, not described in the owner's Warsaw/Suva material.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner's advanced file cites the fund as agreed at COP27 and operationalised at COP28 (⚠️ memory-based; not fetched).
- Any claim about capitalisation, disbursement or India's access needs the specific dated UNFCCC COP decision.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ owner 15 advanced §3, §9; not verified by fetch.)
**Links:** DM-243, DM-244, DM-245.
**Trap:** Do not present Warsaw and Suva as the current fund architecture. Do not state disbursement figures without a COP decision.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish the 2013-2023 dialogue architecture from the later fund. Qualified conclusion: the fund's operational status must be verified before it is treated as delivering finance.
**Back:** *"Agreed at COP27, operational at COP28, verified before quoted: three checks for one fund."*

### Flashcard DM-250 — Adaptation, maladaptation, residual risk and loss and damage
**Front:** Distinguish adaptation, maladaptation, residual risk and loss and damage.
**Definition (learn):** Four related climate-risk terms that are often confused.
**Breakdown:**
- Adaptation: adjustment that reduces harm or uses opportunities.
- Maladaptation: action that unintentionally increases vulnerability or emissions.
- Residual risk: risk remaining after adaptation.
- Loss and damage: adverse impacts not avoided through mitigation or adaptation.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ REVISION-CHART §19; owner 15 advanced §1.)
**Links:** DM-245, DM-243, DM-247.
**Trap:** Loss and damage is not a synonym for disaster relief; it is the residual after avoid and adapt have been tried.
**Mains hook:** Use residual risk to justify the address pillar. Qualified conclusion: maladaptation, such as walls that shift flood risk downstream, must be screened before adaptation is claimed as success.
**Back:** *"Adapt, misadapt, residual, lost: four words, four different outcomes."*

### Flashcard DM-251 — Climate justice: equal hazard, unequal harm
**Front:** What is the owner's climate-justice argument on spatial variation of casualties?
**Definition (learn):** Equivalent hazard magnitude translates into unequal harm because adaptive capacity and wealth differ.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: natural disasters in poorer countries have higher casualties than disasters of similar magnitude in wealthier countries (PDF p.63).
- Justifies loss-and-damage finance claims by developing countries, including India.
- Compounding vulnerability: women (gender-based violence), the poor, displaced youth.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §3; advanced §4, §6.)
**Links:** DM-246, DM-242, DM-243.
**Trap:** Do not say wealthier countries have no losses; the claim is about casualty rates for comparable magnitude.
**Mains hook:** Argue equity through capacity, not hazard alone. Qualified conclusion: loss-and-damage finance is a justice claim as much as a technical one.
**Back:** *"Same shake, different cost: capacity writes the casualty count."*

### Flashcard DM-252 — Climate attribution boundary
**Front:** When can a specific disaster be attributed to climate change?
**Definition (learn):** Causal attribution requires event-attribution evidence; a climate signal is not automatic proof.
**Breakdown:**
- Slow-onset and climate-linked hazards are attributed differently from earthquakes and tsunamis, which have non-climatic causes.
- Cyclones and floods may be intensified by climate change, but attribution needs specific scientific evidence.
- Owner: no dated attribution methodology is detailed in the source.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 15 basic §8; ⚠️ advanced §5.)
**Links:** DM-242, DM-245, DM-251.
**Trap:** "Climate change caused this disaster" is an overclaim unless event attribution is cited. Earthquakes are not climate hazards.
**Mains hook:** Specify which hazards are climate-linked and what evidence supports attribution. Qualified conclusion: a warming signal raises risk; it does not by itself prove causation for one event.
**Back:** *"Signal is not cause: attribute the event with evidence, not the headline."*

### Flashcard DM-253 — Advanced: Eco-DRR accounting and valuation weakness
**Front:** Why is Eco-DRR weakly funded and evaluated?
**Definition (learn):** Ecosystem assets deliver multiple benefits but are funded and evaluated under whichever head claims them.
**Breakdown:**
- Multiplicity: buffering, carbon and livelihoods in one asset.
- Without an agreed method to value avoided loss, Eco-DRR competes badly with engineered assets, whose outputs are easier to count (owner advanced §10).
- Assam wetland example (DM-248) shows the mitigation booking.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ analysis of owner 15 advanced §10.)
**Links:** DM-247, DM-248, DM-258.
**Trap:** Restoration funding is not an outcome; measure avoided loss.
**Mains hook:** Argue for valuation of avoided loss to fund nature-based solutions. Qualified conclusion: the accounting gap, not ecological doubt, is the main barrier to Eco-DRR at scale.
**Back:** *"Engineers count what they build; nature counts only when someone values what it saved."*

### Flashcard DM-254 — Advanced: domestic displacement-protection legislation as alternative
**Front:** What policy option does the advanced file raise for displaced persons not recognised as refugees?
**Definition (learn):** Domestic legal entitlement frameworks for disaster-displaced persons, independent of international recognition.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner's advanced extension, flagged as a policy recommendation beyond the source text.
- Protection gap sits between refugee law and domestic relief entitlements.
- Relevant to India given its own displacement exposure.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ analysis of owner 15 advanced §3, §7.)
**Links:** DM-246, DM-251.
**Trap:** Do not say disaster-displaced persons have no rights; the gap is in legal category.
**Mains hook:** Propose domestic entitlement law as the practical route. Qualified conclusion: while the international gap persists, domestic law is the operational answer.
**Back:** *"Not a refugee, still a citizen: domestic law fills the gap the treaty leaves."*

### GROUP — 16 Disaster Finance, Risk Transfer and Build Back Better (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard DM-255 — Four risk-management strategies
**Front:** What are the four risk-management strategies, and which is insurance?
**Definition (learn):** Four distinct ways of handling risk, which must not be merged.
**Breakdown:**
- Acceptance: retain the risk.
- Avoidance: do not expose.
- Reduction: lower the likelihood or consequence of a hazard.
- Transfer: shift the defined financial burden to another party; insurance is the most common form.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 16 basic §1, §7; PDF p.5.)
**Links:** DM-256, DM-267, DM-268.
**Trap:** Risk reduction and risk transfer are different. Insurance transfers financial loss; physical risk remains.
**Mains hook:** Use the four strategies to classify any finance instrument. Qualified conclusion: transfer complements reduction and cannot replace it.
**Back:** *"Accept, avoid, reduce, transfer: insurance belongs to the last, not the first."*

### Flashcard DM-256 — National Disaster Response Fund (s. 46)
**Front:** What is the National Disaster Response Fund and what is its trigger?
**Definition (learn):** A centrally managed fund under s. 46 of the DM Act for emergency response, relief and rehabilitation expenses, used when State Disaster Response Fund capacity is insufficient.
**Breakdown:**
- Section: s. 46 (owner-stated; Wikipedia confirms the funds sit in ss. 46-50).
- Trigger: needs beyond SDRF capacity.
- Absorbed the National Calamity Contingency Fund (11th Finance Commission).
- Funded via the National Calamity Contingency Duty (see DM-259).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2, §7; PDF p.15. Section mapping ✅ partial via Wikipedia "Disaster Management Act, 2005" (ss. 46-50 mandate funds).)
**Links:** DM-257, DM-259, DM-255.
**Trap:** The National Disaster Response Fund (fund) is not the National Disaster Response Force (NDRF, a force under ss. 44-45). Do not confuse the fund with the force.
**Mains hook:** Name the fund's trigger precisely; do not describe it as automatic central funding. Qualified conclusion: the two-tier, need-triggered escalation puts initial burden on States.
**Back:** *"Fund after the State runs dry, force that does the rescue: never mix the two."*

### Flashcard DM-257 — State Disaster Response Fund (s. 48): sharing ratios
**Front:** What is the SDRF, and what are its Centre-State sharing ratios?
**Definition (learn):** A State-level fund under s. 48 of the DM Act for immediate relief to disaster victims.
**Breakdown:**
- Section: s. 48 (owner-stated).
- Centre:State sharing: 75:25 for general States; 90:10 for North-Eastern and Himalayan States.
- Immediate relief is a narrower category than full rehabilitation or reconstruction.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2, §7; PDF p.15.)
**Links:** DM-256, DM-258, DM-264.
**Trap:** SDRF is not the State Disaster Response Force under s. 44A. The SDRF is financial.
**Mains hook:** Use the sharing ratio to show fiscal federalism. Qualified conclusion: the NEH tilt recognises fiscal and geographic vulnerability but does not change the trigger logic.
**Back:** *"Seventy-five, twenty-five; ninety, ten for the hills and the north-east."*

### Flashcard DM-258 — National and State Disaster Mitigation Funds (ss. 47-48), commencement
**Front:** What are the NDMF and SDMF, and when did s. 47(1) commence?
**Definition (learn):** Ex-ante funds for risk reduction, distinct from relief funds.
**Breakdown:**
- National Disaster Mitigation Fund (NDMF): s. 47.
- State Disaster Mitigation Fund (SDMF): s. 48 (owner-stated).
- Commencement: s. 48 from 1 August 2007; s. 47(1) on 5 February 2021 (owner-stated).
- Wikipedia confirms parts of the Act commenced between 28 July 2006 and 5 February 2021.
- Mitigation finance is therefore about 14 years younger than relief finance.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2; Wikipedia Act page for commencement range.)
**Links:** DM-257, DM-262, DM-265.
**Trap:** Do not claim mitigation funds were established in 2005 with the Act. Use the commencement dates.
**Mains hook:** Explain the late start of the mitigation architecture. Qualified conclusion: funds exist, but mitigation was designed as a smaller, approval-driven window.
**Back:** *"Forty-seven commenced in twenty-twenty-one: mitigation came late, relief came first."*

### Flashcard DM-259 — National Calamity Contingency Duty and the merged NCCF
**Front:** What funded the NDRF before the GST, and what happened to the NCCD?
**Definition (learn):** The National Calamity Contingency Duty is a duty on specified goods that funds the NDRF.
**Breakdown:**
- NCCF (National Calamity Contingency Fund, 11th Finance Commission) was merged into the National Disaster Response Fund.
- NCCD revenue fell from Rs 5,690 crore (2015-16) to Rs 2,500 crore (2018-19) as GST shrank its scope (owner-stated, document-period).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2, §7; PDF p.15. Figures ⚠️ document-period.)
**Links:** DM-256, DM-255, DM-261.
**Trap:** Those NCCD figures are historical; cite a current source for present revenue.
**Mains hook:** Use NCCD erosion as an example of a tax reform undermining disaster finance. Qualified conclusion: a fiscal side-effect needs an alternative revenue source, not a hope that GST covers it.
**Back:** *"Duty fell from five thousand to twenty-five hundred: GST shrank the calamity purse."*

### Flashcard DM-260 — Standing Committee on Finance (2019): recommendations and disposal
**Front:** What did the 2019 Standing Committee on Finance recommend on disaster finance, and what happened to each?
**Definition (learn):** Parliamentary committee report "Central Assistance for Disaster Management and Relief" (Chair: Dr. M. Veerappa Moily).
**Breakdown:**
- Enhanced SDRF/NDRF scales for expenditure heads such as government buildings and transmission stations.
- Separate Disaster Mitigation Fund: adopted via FC15's SDMF/NDMF windows and s. 47(1) commencement (5 Feb 2021).
- 10% of centrally sponsored scheme allocations for permanent restoration: no equivalent in FC16 (owner-stated).
- 15% annual SDRF corpus increase for 2020-25: FC16 settled on 5% annual increase (owner-stated).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 16 basic §3, §8; PDF p.15; disposal ⚠️ owner-stated.)
**Links:** DM-258, DM-262, DM-265.
**Trap:** Do not cite the 2019 recommendations as still pending; the mitigation-fund recommendation was implemented.
**Mains hook:** Report what survived and what was modified. Qualified conclusion: the committee's core idea was adopted, but the proportion and procedure remain the live critique.
**Back:** *"Four asks, three answered: the mitigation fund came, the 15 percent did not."*

### Flashcard DM-261 — Fifteenth Finance Commission (2021-26) disaster corpus
**Front:** What four-window architecture did FC15 create, and what were its corpora?
**Definition (learn):** The first Finance Commission to create a dedicated disaster-risk-management architecture, reported October 2020.
**Breakdown:**
- State corpus total Rs 1,60,153 crore: SDRF Rs 1,28,122 crore; SDMF Rs 32,031 crore.
- National corpus total Rs 68,463 crore: NDRF Rs 54,770 crore; NDMF Rs 13,693 crore.
- Split 80:20 response to mitigation.
- Within the State corpus: response/relief 40%; recovery/reconstruction 30%; preparedness 10%; mitigation 20%.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ owner 16 basic §5 figures, not fetched; FC15 award period 2021-26.)
**Links:** DM-257, DM-258, DM-262, DM-271.
**Trap:** Recovery is 30% and mitigation is 20%; these are routinely swapped.
**Mains hook:** Use FC15 as the baseline before FC16. Qualified conclusion: the architecture was proportional, not transformative, with a fixed 20% mitigation share.
**Back:** *"Forty for relief, thirty for rebuild, twenty for mitigation: swap them and you fail."*

### Flashcard DM-262 — Sixteenth Finance Commission (2026-31) disaster corpus
**Front:** What are the FC16 disaster-fund recommendations for 2026-31?
**Definition (learn):** Current award period, 1 April 2026 to 31 March 2031, chaired by Dr. Arvind Panagariya; report submitted 17 November 2025; Volume I public 1 February 2026.
**Breakdown:**
- SDRF: Rs 1,63,521 crore; SDMF: Rs 40,880 crore; State funds total Rs 2,04,401 crore.
- NDRF plus NDMF national pool: Rs 79,406 crore; all four funds Rs 2,83,807 crore.
- 80:20 SDRF:SDMF split retained.
- Centre:State sharing 75:25 (non-NEH) and 90:10 (NEH) retained.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 16 basic §5, §9; ⚠️ figures owner-cited, not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-261, DM-263, DM-264, DM-265.
**Trap:** Do not invent fixed NDRF and NDMF sub-corpora; the national pool is allocated by rule. Do not cite FC15 as current from 1 April 2026.
**Mains hook:** Cite FC16 as the current award and FC15 as the preceding one. Qualified conclusion: the current fund size is an allocation, not evidence that risk is reducing.
**Back:** *"Sixteen's award, two-eighty-three crore: the current number, with the previous one as the baseline."*

### Flashcard DM-263 — Notified SDRF disasters: the 14-disaster list
**Front:** Which 14 disasters are on MHA's current SDRF list, and what changed for heatwave and lightning?
**Definition (learn):** The national list of disasters eligible for SDRF assistance, as shown on MHA's response-fund page (owner says retrieved 15 August 2026).
**Breakdown:**
- List: avalanche, cloudburst, cyclone, drought, earthquake, fire, flood, frost/cold waves, hailstorm, heatwave, landslide, lightning, pest attack, tsunami.
- Heatwave and lightning are now on the national list.
- The FC16 record (11 State heat-wave and 16 State lightning notifications) is historical context.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 16 basic §2, §7; ⚠️ not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-264, DM-262, DM-270.
**Trap:** A recommendation is not a notification; do not collapse them. Listing is an eligibility boundary, not proof of heat-risk reduction.
**Mains hook:** Use the list as an eligibility change, and test outcome separately. Qualified conclusion: national listing corrects financing eligibility but does not show prevention or cooling access.
**Back:** *"Fourteen on the list, heatwave and lightning now in: listing is eligibility, not safety."*

### Flashcard DM-264 — The 10% local-disaster window of SDRF
**Front:** What is the 10% local-disaster window?
**Definition (learn):** A State may spend up to 10% of its annual SDRF allocation on immediate relief for locally significant disasters outside the national list.
**Breakdown:**
- Requires State notification, transparent norms and State Executive Committee approval.
- Formerly the route for State-specific heatwave and lightning assistance.
- Not the current route for heatwave and lightning after their national listing (owner-stated).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner 16 basic §2, §7; ⚠️ not fetched.)
**Links:** DM-263, DM-257.
**Trap:** The 10% window is for locally significant disasters outside the list, not for listed hazards.
**Mains hook:** Explain that State flexibility exists but is bounded. Qualified conclusion: the window gives flexibility, but it requires notification and transparent norms.
**Back:** *"Ten percent for the local hazard the list forgot: notify it or lose it."*

### Flashcard DM-265 — FC16 allocation rules: weighting, no SDRF-to-SDMF transfer, withholding
**Front:** What allocation and transfer rules did FC16 set for disaster funds?
**Definition (learn):** The rules that govern how SDRF is allocated among States and how funds may move.
**Breakdown:**
- Inter-State allocation weighted 70% past expenditure and 30% Disaster Risk Index.
- Annual increase of 5%.
- Withholding where a State's unspent SDRF balance exceeds its previous three years' allocation.
- Flexibility within SDRF between relief and recovery, but no SDRF-to-SDMF transfer.
- Preparedness and capacity-building shifted to SDMF/NDMF.
