**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 08-basic §5; ⚠️ current coverage not verified).
**Links:** DM-327 (community first response), DM-335.
**Trap:** A volunteer scheme is not a State response force; "first responders" cannot justify shifting State responsibility without training and equipment (REVISION-CHART §7).
**Mains hook:** Use Aapda Mitra as a community-capacity example in a 20-mark answer. Qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Thirty districts, twenty-five States — volunteers, not a force."*

### Flashcard DM-339 — Mumbai floods 2005
**Front:** Give the date, rainfall and toll of the 2005 Mumbai floods.
**Definition (learn):** Extreme rainfall on 26 July 2005 flooded Mumbai.
**Breakdown:**
- Date 26 July 2005 (✅ Wikipedia: Mumbai_floods_of_2005).
- Heaviest 24-hour total 944 mm at Santacruz (✅ Wikipedia); a drainage section gives 993 mm — internal inconsistency noted.
- Deaths approx. 1,094 (✅ Wikipedia infobox and text).
- Owner adds low-lying reclaimed land, drainage and waste blockage as vulnerability (owner adv §13.1).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia: Mumbai_floods_of_2005).
**Links:** DM-329, DM-330, DM-340, DM-344.
**Trap:** Do not use Mumbai 2005 for a cyclone; it was an intense-rain urban flood. Do not quote 993 mm without noting Wikipedia's inconsistency.
**Mains hook:** Use Mumbai 2005 with Chennai 2015 as contrasting cases of the same urban-flood chain. Qualified close: rainfall intensity met drainage and land-use failure.
**Back:** *"Twenty-six July, nine-forty-four millimetres, drains choked."*

### Flashcard DM-340 — Chennai floods 2015
**Front:** Summarise the 2015 Chennai floods: cause, dates, toll and drivers.
**Definition (learn):** Floods caused by heavy northeast monsoon rainfall, worsened by urban encroachment and drainage failure.
**Breakdown:**
- Heavy rain period 8 November – 14 December 2015 (✅ Wikipedia: 2015_Chennai_floods).
- Chennai had 1,049 mm rain in November (✅ Wikipedia).
- Overall deaths 500+; Tamil Nadu 422 official, likely more (✅ Wikipedia).
- A depression formed 8 November and crossed near Puducherry (✅ Wikipedia).
- IISc study: Eastern Ghats upstream blocking held clouds over Chennai on 1 December (✅ Wikipedia).
- CAG (July 2018) called it a "man-made disaster" (✅ Wikipedia).
- Wetlands built over; illegal development; poor drainage design (✅ Wikipedia).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia: 2015_Chennai_floods).
**Links:** DM-330 (NDMA delinking trigger), DM-344 (2024 Q18), DM-323, DM-337.
**Trap:** Chennai 2015 is northeast-monsoon (cyclone-linked depression) rain, not a cyclone landfall flood. Do not state an official death toll above the recorded range.
**Mains hook:** The best 2024 Q18 case. Qualified conclusion: the rain was extreme, but the disaster scale was built by wetland loss and drainage design.
**Back:** *"Nov-Dec 2015, a depression, a 'man-made disaster' per CAG."*

### Flashcard DM-341 — Delhi floods 2023
**Front:** What is the Delhi 2023 flood's evidentiary status?
**Definition (learn):** A 2023 Yamuna-linked urban flood in Delhi, documented in a CWC case study.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner cites CWC "Delhi Floods 2023: A Case Study" (January 2025) as the correct current source for verified features (⚠️ not fetched).
- Owner recommends Delhi 2023 as the second flood with institutional-response evolution against Chennai 2015.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner cites CWC January 2025 — ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-340, DM-335, DM-344.
**Trap:** Do not describe Delhi 2023's features from memory or media; cite the CWC case study or leave the feature blank.
**Mains hook:** Two-flood answer: Chennai 2015 and Delhi 2023 demonstrate causal diversity. Qualified close.
**Back:** *"Delhi 2023: cite the case study, not the headline."*

### Flashcard DM-342 — Concept: riverine versus urban flood causation chain
**Front:** Map the causal chains of riverine and urban floods and the policy tools each needs.
**Definition (learn):** Riverine flood = basin rainfall, snowmelt, river capacity and floodplain processes. Urban flood = intense rain plus impermeable surfaces, blocked drains, wetland loss and encroachment.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural causes: heavy rainfall, sedimentation, cyclone surge, river-course change, tsunami, lake loss (owner, PDF p.26).
- Anthropogenic causes: deforestation, interference by bridges/roads/canals, cross-border rivers from China/Nepal/Bhutan, encroachment, poor waste management (owner).
- Urban-specific: land-use change and surface sealing, floodplain occupation, upstream dam releases, urban heat island, solid-waste blockage, riverbed concretisation (owner).
- Regional severity: Brahmaputra and Ganga most severe; North-West less; Odisha delta areas flood-prone (owner, PDF pp.27-28).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 08-basic §1, §3; ✅ owner 08-adv §3).
**Links:** DM-329, DM-330, DM-331, DM-333, DM-343, DM-344, REVISION-CHART §12.
**Trap:** Urban and riverine flood share the word "flood" but differ in lead time, governance and remedy.
**Mains hook:** 15-mark answer structure: 2–3 causal chains, two named floods, policy and frameworks, qualified close.
**Back:** *"River overflows, city drains fail — match the cause to the tool."*

### Flashcard DM-343 — Kumbh Mela and riverbed concretisation
**Front:** How does management of religious gatherings affect river channels?
**Definition (learn):** Owner identifies poorly managed religious gatherings as a temporary but recurring flood-risk driver.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: inefficient management of Kumbh Mela-type gatherings causes "unwanted concretisation of rivers, which narrows down their channels" (owner adv §4, PDF p.30).
- Unregulated concretisation of riverbeds is listed as an urban-flood factor (owner basic §3).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 08-adv §4, PDF p.30; ⚠️ single source, no event-specific data).
**Links:** DM-342, DM-333.
**Trap:** Do not present Kumbh as a major flood cause without event-level evidence; it is a named mechanism, not a statistic.
**Mains hook:** Use as a fresh anthropogenic example in a "causes" answer, showing depth beyond generic urbanisation.
**Back:** *"Crowds concrete the channel; the channel shrinks, the flood grows."*

### Flashcard DM-344 — PYQ route: 2024 Q18 urban flooding (15 marks)
**Front:** What does 2024 Q18 require and what is the answer route?
**Definition (learn):** 2024 Q18: "Flooding in urban areas is an emerging climate-induced disaster. Discuss the causes... mention the features of two major floods in the last two decades... describe the policies and frameworks" (250 words, 15 marks, owner-quoted).
**Breakdown:**
- Causes: 2–3 chains (intense rain plus sealed surface and drain blockage; floodplain and wetland loss; river/barrage/tide interaction).
- Two floods with features: Mumbai 2005 (DM-339) and Chennai 2015 (DM-340); Delhi 2023 only with CWC source (DM-341).
- Policies: NDMA urban-flood guidelines, floodplain zoning, CWC/IMD forecasting, ULB drainage and waste, UFRMP (DM-337).
- Qualified close: implementation, maintenance and equitable relocation decide outcomes.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 08-basic §10; owner-quoted paraphrase; verbatim not checked against the paper).
**Links:** DM-329, DM-330, DM-339, DM-340, DM-337.
**Trap:** Do not treat "climate-induced" as proof of climate attribution. The examiner expects the land-use mechanism alongside climate context.
**Mains hook:** This is the question itself. Conclude that urban flood disasters are substantially produced by land-use, drainage and governance decisions.
**Back:** *"Causes, two floods, policies, close: C-2-P-C."*

### GROUP — Drought, Heat Waves and Slow-Onset Risk

### Flashcard DM-345 — Four drought types (and the chart's fifth)
**Front:** Name the drought types and state the conflict in the revision chart.
**Definition (learn):** Drought is a serious water shortfall that evolves over months or years and affects a large area (owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Meteorological: rainfall below 90% of average (owner).
- Agricultural: soil-moisture shortfall causing crop failure; areas with over 30% gross cropped area irrigated are excluded from drought-prone classification (owner).
- Hydrological: aquifers, lakes, reservoirs below replenishment (owner).
- Ecological: ecosystem productivity failure (owner).
- REVISION-CHART §13 lists socio-economic drought (water shortage affecting goods and services) instead of the owner's four-type scheme (⚠️ conflict; not settled).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §2, PDF p.36; ✅ REVISION-CHART §13).
**Links:** DM-347, DM-346, DM-360.
**Trap:** "Drought means only rainfall deficiency" is wrong. Name the type because each has a different metric.
**Mains hook:** Use the cascade (meteorological → agricultural → hydrological → ecological) to argue rainfall triggers alone are too narrow.
**Back:** *"Met, Agri, Hydro, Eco — the chart adds Socio-economic."*

### Flashcard DM-346 — IMD five drought situations
**Front:** List IMD's five drought situations and their thresholds.
**Definition (learn):** IMD categories of drought at different time scales.
**Breakdown:**
- Drought Week: weekly rainfall under half of normal.
- Agricultural Drought: four consecutive drought weeks, mid-June to September.
- Seasonal Drought: seasonal rainfall deficient beyond standard deviation.
- Drought Year: annual rainfall deficient by 20% or more.
- Severe Drought Year: deficient by 25–40% or more.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §2, PDF p.39; ⚠️ IMD source not fetched).
**Links:** DM-345, DM-347.
**Trap:** Drought week is not drought year; the time scale differs. Do not treat the thresholds as the declaration rule.
**Mains hook:** Use the scales to separate short-term alerts from annual declarations.
**Back:** *"Week, season, year: half, deviation, twenty, forty."*

### Flashcard DM-347 — Manual for Drought Management (2016, updated 2020)
**Front:** How is drought-affected status declared in India?
**Definition (learn):** Drought status is declared under the Ministry of Agriculture's Manual for Drought Management, a multi-indicator assessment.
**Breakdown:**
- Five indicator families: rainfall, vegetative indices, crop-sowing progression, soil moisture, hydrological indices (owner).
- Grades: Normal / Moderate / Severe (owner).
- No single crop-loss percentage (owner).
- Updated to December 2020 (owner; detailed thresholds not verified).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** DM-345, DM-348, DM-360.
**Trap:** "Declared at >50% crop loss" is a false universal rule.
**Mains hook:** Composite assessment is harder to game than a binary threshold (owner adv §3). Qualified conclusion: a composite index still needs timely ground data.
**Back:** *"Five indicators, three grades, no magic crop-loss number."*

### Flashcard DM-348 — Drought severity zonation and the 68% estimate
**Front:** Name the severity zones and the drought-prone share.
**Definition (learn):** Owner's zonation of drought severity across India.
**Breakdown:**
- Extreme: western Rajasthan, Marusthali, Kachchh (e.g., Jaisalmer, Barmer; under 90 mm annual rainfall).
- Severe: eastern Rajasthan, most of MP, eastern Maharashtra, interior AP/Karnataka, northern interior TN, southern Jharkhand, interior Odisha.
- Moderate: northern Rajasthan, Haryana, southern UP, rest of Gujarat and Maharashtra, Jharkhand, Coimbatore plateau.
- Around 68% of India is drought-prone in varying degrees (owner, document-period).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §3, PDF p.38; ⚠️ document-period, not verified).
**Links:** DM-345, DM-349, DM-350.
**Trap:** Zone maps are document-period; do not assert the 68% figure as current.
**Mains hook:** Use the zonation to argue that a drought policy must be regional, not uniform.
**Back:** *"Extreme west, severe centre, moderate fringe — sixty-eight is old."*

### Flashcard DM-349 — Falkenmark Index
**Front:** What is the Falkenmark Index and what did the owner report?
**Definition (learn):** A water-scarcity threshold of about 1,000 cubic metres per person per year.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: about 820 million Indians across twelve river basins near or below the threshold (document-period, ⚠️).
- Owner calls it an "official" threshold; treat as an international physical-scarcity benchmark, not an Indian statutory standard (⚠️).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §5; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** DM-350 (CWMI), DM-348.
**Trap:** Falkenmark measures physical scarcity; CWMI measures governance. Do not conflate.
**Mains hook:** A State can rank well on governance yet remain under the physical threshold (owner adv §4).
**Back:** *"One thousand cubic metres a head — physical scarcity, not governance."*

### Flashcard DM-350 — NITI Aayog Composite Water Management Index (CWMI)
**Front:** What does CWMI measure?
**Definition (learn):** A NITI Aayog index for evaluating State-level water-management performance.
**Breakdown:**
- Nine sectors and 28 indicators (owner).
- Covers groundwater, water-body restoration, irrigation, farm practices, drinking water, and policy/governance (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §5, PDF p.37; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** DM-349, DM-351.
**Trap:** CWMI rankings are document-period; do not quote State ranks as current.
**Mains hook:** Use CWMI to show governance performance versus Falkenmark's physical scarcity.
**Back:** *"Nine sectors, twenty-eight marks: governance scored, not rainfall."*

### Flashcard DM-351 — Bundelkhand Mentha cultivation
**Front:** What is the Bundelkhand Mentha example and what policy tension does it show?
**Definition (learn):** Water-intensive mint cultivation in a drought-prone region, cited by the owner as a stark example.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner cites Mentha cash cropping in Bundelkhand (PDF p.37).
- Tension: crop profitability versus water security.
- Owner adv: discouraging the crop risks farmer income without an equally profitable alternative (⚠️ inference).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §6, adv §4).
**Links:** DM-348, DM-350, DM-360.
**Trap:** Do not recommend crop diversification as costless.
**Mains hook:** Perverse incentive in a "critically examine" answer. Qualified conclusion: price support for alternatives.
**Back:** *"Mint in Bundelkhand: profit drinks the water."*

### Flashcard DM-352 — IMD heat-wave thresholds
**Front:** State IMD's heat-wave criteria: plains, hills, departures and absolute triggers.
**Definition (learn):** Heat wave is a relative (departure-from-normal) criterion, plus absolute triggers.
**Breakdown:**
- Considered only when maximum reaches at least 40°C (plains) or 30°C (hilly regions).
- Heat wave: departure 4.5°C to 6.4°C from normal. Severe heat wave: departure more than 6.4°C.
- Independent absolute triggers: maximum ≥45°C = heat wave; ≥47°C = severe.
- Coastal stations: departure ≥4.5°C and actual maximum ≥37°C.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §2, cites IMD FAQ 18 June 2024; ⚠️ IMD not fetched).
**Links:** DM-353, DM-359, DM-355.
**Trap:** A fixed "45°C = heat wave" rule is incomplete; departure criteria apply to most stations.
**Mains hook:** Argue that heat is station- and departure-specific, requiring local warning. Qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Four-point-five to six-point-four; forty-five and forty-seven absolute."*

### Flashcard DM-353 — Heat-wave declaration rule
**Front:** When is a heat wave formally declared?
**Definition (learn):** Declaration needs criteria met at two or more stations in a meteorological subdivision for two consecutive days, declared on the second day.
**Breakdown:**
- At least two stations in a subdivision (owner).
- Two consecutive days; declared on the second day (owner citing IMD FAQ).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-352.
**Trap:** A single station hitting 45°C is not a declaration.
**Mains hook:** Use the rule to explain warning lag; qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Two stations, two days, declared on day two."*

### Flashcard DM-354 — Cold wave
**Front:** Define cold wave and state its causes and NDMA plan.
**Definition (learn):** Invasion of very cold air over a large area; owner also records drop of more than 4°C in minimum temperature persisting 3–5 days.
**Breakdown:**
- Peaks in January; driven by western disturbances, La Niña and jet streams (owner).
- Localised disaster; State-specific plans, not a national plan (owner).
- NDMA National Guidelines for Cold Wave and Frost (October 2021) (owner).
- Farmer actions: light irrigation, pruning, smoke-based frost protection, foliar sprays; school conversion for shelter (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ criteria not verified against IMD).
**Links:** DM-352, DM-355.
**Trap:** Cold waves are not handled by a uniform national plan like cyclones (owner).
**Mains hook:** Contrast localised cold-wave planning with heat-wave national eligibility. Qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Cold is local, cyclone is national — plan the State."*

### Flashcard DM-355 — Heat Action Plans (HAPs)
**Front:** What are HAPs, where did they start, and what is their governing guidance?
**Definition (learn):** Locally tailored heat-wave action plans encouraged by NDMA.
**Breakdown:**
- First HAP: Ahmedabad (operational summer 2013) (owner, ✅).
- Three strands: monitoring, preparedness (work-hour regulation, water, health training), long-term (water bodies, vegetation, cool roofs).
- Four criteria: forecasting/early warning; healthcare capacity; community outreach; inter-agency cooperation (owner).
- Coverage: more than 250 cities and districts across 23 heat-prone States as of 7 June 2025 (📰 PIB; ⚠️ not verified).
- Governing guideline: NDMA Guidelines for Preparation of Action Plan — Prevention and Management of Heat Wave (October 2019).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §4-5, PDF p.42).
**Links:** DM-352, DM-356, DM-357.
**Trap:** A HAP is an administrative plan, not a binding legal instrument; coverage measures adoption, not mortality reduction.
**Mains hook:** HAP equity: named channels SEWA, ASHA, anganwadi, Mahila Arogya Samiti (owner adv §6). Qualified conclusion: plan adoption is not delivery.
**Back:** *"Ahmedabad 2013 led; 2019 guidelines, 250 places — adoption, not outcome."*

### Flashcard DM-356 — National Action Plan on Heat-Related Illness and cooling-centre guidelines
**Front:** Name the two health and cooling documents that sit beside HAPs.
**Definition (learn):** Companion instruments for heat-health and cooling infrastructure.
**Breakdown:**
- National Action Plan on Heat-Related Illness (MoHFW/NCDC, July 2021) (owner).
- National Guidelines for Cooling Centers (NDMA, November 2025) (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-355, DM-357.
**Trap:** A cooling centre on paper is not accessible cooling; relief eligibility does not prove access (REVISION-CHART §29).
**Mains hook:** Argue that heat outcomes depend on health and cooling channels as much as on HAP existence.
**Back:** *"Health plan 2021, cooling centres 2025 — access is the test."*

### Flashcard DM-357 — Heatwave and lightning in the SDRF list
**Front:** What is the current national status of heatwave and lightning for SDRF purposes?
**Definition (learn):** MHA's current response-fund page lists heatwave and lightning among 14 nationally notified SDRF disasters.
**Breakdown:**
- MHA page retrieved 15 August 2026, per owner (📰; ⚠️ not fetched).
- Earlier position: State-specific notifications and the 10% local-disaster window; the Sixteenth Finance Commission (1 February 2026) recorded 11 State heat-wave and 16 State lightning notifications (historical context).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ unverified).
**Links:** DM-355, DM-358, DM-356.
**Trap:** An FC recommendation is not a notification; keep the 11/16 figures as historical.
**Mains hook:** Relief eligibility changed; implementation did not. Qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Fourteen notified, heat and lightning in; FC16 was the history."*

### Flashcard DM-358 — Thunderstorm and lightning guidelines and Damini
**Front:** Name the national guideline and public app for thunderstorm and lightning.
**Definition (learn):** Lightning is a rapid-onset hazard, with usable lead time of minutes.
**Breakdown:**
- NDMA Guidelines on Prevention and Management of Thunderstorm and Lightning / Squall / Dust / Hailstorm and Strong Winds (March 2019) (owner).
- Public lightning-warning app: Damini, from IITM Pune (Ministry of Earth Sciences) (owner).
- Behavioural measure: seek a proper building; avoid isolated trees and open fields (owner).
- Lightning is also a forest-fire ignition source (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-314, DM-383, DM-357.
**Trap:** Lightning is nowcasting, not forecasting; institutional evacuation is not the operative measure.
**Mains hook:** Use lightning to show that individually addressed alerts matter more than institutional evacuation for minute-scale hazards.
**Back:** *"Damini warns in minutes: find a building, leave the tree."*

### Flashcard DM-359 — Wet-bulb temperature and the 2025 Prelims question
**Front:** Define wet-bulb temperature and explain why it is not IMD's heat-wave threshold.
**Definition (learn):** Wet-bulb temperature combines air temperature and humidity; it is the lowest temperature reachable by evaporation, so it indicates whether sweat can cool the body.
**Breakdown:**
- Around 35°C wet-bulb is a theoretical upper benchmark for sustained human heat dissipation under protective assumptions (owner; ⚠️ general physiology, not fetched).
- Dangerous stress occurs well below 35°C depending on exertion, health and acclimatisation (owner).
- Not IMD's heat-wave declaration threshold; do not substitute for the 40°C/30°C or departure criteria.
- 2025 Prelims GS-I Q64: "wet-bulb temperature crossing 35C — implications" (owner; answer key not recorded in this card).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §13; key status not reproduced here).
**Links:** DM-352, DM-353.
**Trap:** Confusing wet-bulb with dry-bulb or with IMD declaration thresholds is the trap.
**Mains hook:** Humid-heat risk in coastal and outdoor-work settings; qualified conclusion on cooling access.
**Back:** *"Wet-bulb: sweat's cooling limit, not IMD's declaration."*

### Flashcard DM-360 — Concept: slow-onset cascade and anticipatory action
**Front:** Why do drought and heat wave need anticipatory action rather than only post-declaration relief?
**Definition (learn):** Slow-onset hazards unfold over months or years and cascade through systems.
**Breakdown:**
- Cascade: rainfall deficit → soil moisture → crop failure → livelihood loss → distress migration and health stress (owner adv §2).
- Heat chain: high temperature + humidity + urban heat island + outdoor work + weak housing → illness and mortality (REVISION-CHART §13).
- Institutional visibility lag: declaration is threshold-triggered (owner adv §1).
- Anticipatory action is the paradigm for slow-onset hazards (owner adv §5, ⚠️ analytical extension).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 09-basic §1, 09-adv §2; ✅ REVISION-CHART §13).
**Links:** DM-345, DM-347, DM-355, DM-351.
**Trap:** Relief after declaration is not the same as anticipatory protection.
**Mains hook:** 20-mark slow-onset evaluation: compare declaration systems with livelihood and health needs; end on preventable illness and livelihood loss, not declared disasters.
**Back:** *"Slow hazard, slow relief: anticipate before the declaration."*

### GROUP — Landslides, Avalanches and GLOF Risk

### Flashcard DM-361 — Landslide: definition and share of land area
**Front:** Define a landslide and state the owner's share of India's land affected.
**Definition (learn):** Down-slope movement of rock, debris or earth under gravity: a form of mass wasting.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner says landslides affect at least 15% of India's land area (document-period).
- Indian plate moves north about 5 cm per year against the Eurasian plate, making Himalayan rock friable (owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
- Heavy rain is the main natural cause (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §1-3, PDF pp.31-32; ⚠️ 15% and 5 cm/year not verified).
**Links:** DM-362, DM-366, DM-376.
**Trap:** Landslide, avalanche, cloudburst and GLOF are not the same hazard.
**Mains hook:** Name the hazard precisely in any mass-movement answer.
**Back:** *"Mass wastes down the slope: rock, debris, earth."*

### Flashcard DM-362 — Landslide vulnerability zonation
**Front:** List the owner's landslide vulnerability zones.
**Definition (learn):** Zonation ranks areas by landslide susceptibility from very high to safe.
**Breakdown:**
- Very High: young Himalaya, Andaman and Nicobar, high-rainfall steep slopes in the Western Ghats, Nilgiris and North-East, frequent earthquake zones, intense human activity.
- High: similar factors, lower intensity; excludes Assam plains.
- Moderate/Low: Trans-Himalayan Ladakh and Spiti, Aravali, rain-shadow Western and Eastern Ghats, Deccan plateau, and mining-linked areas.
- Other/safe: Rajasthan, Haryana, UP, Bihar, most of West Bengal and Assam, southern coast.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §2, PDF pp.31-32; ⚠️ not verified against GSI).
**Links:** DM-369 (GSI mapping), DM-372 (mining list), DM-370.
**Trap:** Mining-linked landslides are a separate sub-category in the owner's list, not a Himalayan category.
**Mains hook:** Use the zone to decide construction, drainage and evacuation (owner 15-marks spine). Qualified conclusion: mapping is a decision tool, not a forecast.
**Back:** *"Very High to Safe: Himalaya, Ghats, mining sit in the red."*

### Flashcard DM-363 — Cloudburst definition
**Front:** Define cloudburst and state its scale.
**Definition (learn):** Extremely fast, localised heavy rainfall caused by saturated clouds unable to release rain until the drops become too heavy and fall together suddenly.
**Breakdown:**
- Around 10 cm or more per hour over roughly 20–30 sq km (owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
- Mechanism: strong upward warm-air currents; Bay of Bengal monsoon low-pressure clouds move north over the Ganga plains onto the Himalaya (owner).
- Single disasters may involve several cloudbursts (Uttarakhand 2013, owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §2, PDF p.33; ⚠️ thresholds not verified).
**Links:** DM-364, DM-371, DM-374.
**Trap:** Cloudburst is not ordinary seasonal rain; the scale is small and short.
**Mains hook:** 10-mark cloudburst answer: distinguish from seasonal rain; explain uplift; give monitoring and drainage actions; state the lead-time limit.
**Back:** *"Ten centimetres an hour over twenty to thirty square kilometres — sudden, small, savage."*

### Flashcard DM-364 — Cloudburst prediction and the absence of NDMA guidelines
**Front:** Can cloudbursts be predicted, and does NDMA have specific guidelines?
**Definition (learn):** Owner states cloudburst prediction is very difficult because of its small space-time scale; NDMA has not released cloudburst-specific guidelines.
**Breakdown:**
- Prediction "very difficult... due to its very small scale in space and time" (owner, PDF p.34-35).
- Management by extension of flood and landslide guidelines (owner, PDF p.33).
- Advanced: cloudburst is hard, not categorically impossible, at short lead times (owner adv §5).
- Measures: settle on hard rock, avoid valleys, rain-gauge networks, drainage, bioengineering (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §4, §8; ✅ owner 10-adv §3, §5).
**Links:** DM-363, DM-365, DM-377.
**Trap:** "Cloudbursts are predictable with current technology" is wrong per the owner; "unpredictable" is equally imprecise.
**Mains hook:** Argue institutional accountability gap (no cloudburst-specific budget line in the owner's account). Qualified close.
**Back:** *"No cloudburst guidelines: flood and landslide rules by extension."*

### Flashcard DM-365 — Avalanche and DGRE
**Front:** Define avalanche and name India's dedicated avalanche-warning institution.
**Definition (learn):** Rapid down-slope movement of snow and ice released when gravitational stress on an unstable snowpack exceeds its strength.
**Breakdown:**
- Triggers: weak snowpack layer, slope (typically 30–45°), snow load, temperature change, rain-on-snow, wind loading (owner ⚠️).
- Institution: DGRE (Defence Geoinformatics Research Establishment, DRDO) issues forecasts and warnings for the Himalaya and Ladakh (owner ⚠️).
- Protective measures: avalanche zoning, route closure protocols, snow fences and deflection walls (owner ⚠️).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (⚠️ owner-only; DGRE role not fetched).
**Links:** DM-366, DM-361, DM-376.
**Trap:** An avalanche is not a landslide; it is snow and ice, and DGRE is not GSI.
**Mains hook:** Governance split across at least three institutions by hazard type (owner adv §3). Qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Snow, not rock: DGRE warns, GSI maps landslides."*

### Flashcard DM-366 — Glacial Lake Outburst Flood (GLOF): definition and triggers
**Front:** Define GLOF and list the stated triggers.
**Definition (learn):** A GLOF is an outburst flood caused by the failure of a dam containing a glacial lake (✅ Wikipedia: Glacial_lake_outburst_flood).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural dams: glacier ice; terminal moraine; ice-marginal and sub-glacial lakes (✅ Wikipedia).
- Triggers: erosion; water-pressure buildup; avalanche of rock or snow; earthquake or cryoseism; volcanic eruption under ice; large ice displacement into the lake (✅ Wikipedia).
- Climate-driven melting and permafrost thaw raise GLOF risk (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner adds roads, mass tourism and hydropower as accelerants (owner 10-adv §2, PDF p.79).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia: Glacial_lake_outburst_flood; ✅ owner 10-basic §2).
**Links:** DM-367, DM-368, DM-373, DM-361.
**Trap:** GLOF is not a landslide or avalanche, though all are Himalayan mass-movement or cryosphere hazards.
**Mains hook:** Use GLOF to show cascade risk (glacial retreat → lake → downstream flood) and development as accelerant. Qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Dam of ice or moraine fails: the lake bursts downstream."*

### Flashcard DM-367 — NDMA GLOF guidelines (2020) and National GLOF Risk Mitigation Programme
**Front:** What is the NGRMP outlay, coverage and lake count?
**Definition (learn):** NDMA's GLOF guidelines (October 2020) and a mitigation programme for Himalayan States.
**Breakdown:**
- Outlay ₹150 crore: ₹135 crore from NDMF plus ₹15 crore State contribution (owner, MHA/PIB 4 Feb 2025).
- States: Arunachal Pradesh, Himachal Pradesh, Sikkim, Uttarakhand.
- Official dynamic list: 195 high-risk glacial lakes as of 4 December 2024 (owner).
- Mitigation measures: remote-sensing monitoring, dam remediation, hazard mapping, evacuation sites, community-based early warning (owner, PDF p.79).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ PIB not reachable).
**Links:** DM-366, DM-368, DM-373.
**Trap:** Compare outlay with lake count before calling coverage adequate; lake lists are dynamic.
**Mains hook:** Qualified conclusion: ₹150 crore against 195 dynamic lakes.
**Back:** *"Fifteen-oh-crore for one-nine-five lakes: the math is the critique."*

### Flashcard DM-368 — National Landslide Forecasting Centre (NLFC) and Regional Landslide Forecasting System
**Front:** When was NLFC inaugurated and how many districts run forecasts?
**Definition (learn):** GSI's centre issuing landslide forecasts; the regional system covers selected districts.
**Breakdown:**
- NLFC inaugurated 19 July 2024; first regional forecasts 20 July 2024 for Darjeeling, Kalimpong and the Nilgiris (owner).
- By December 2025: 21 districts, 4 operational and 17 experimental (owner, PIB/MoES 17 December 2025).
- Daily next-48-hour forecasts at taluk or sub-divisional scale; Bhusanket portal and Bhooskhalan app (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-369, DM-370, DM-361.
**Trap:** Experimental districts are not a service guarantee. Do not say "India has landslide early warning" without the operational split.
**Mains hook:** Argue the move from susceptibility mapping (where) to forecasting (when), with only four districts operational (owner adv §10).
**Back:** *"Four live, seventeen trial: forecast is not coverage."*

### Flashcard DM-369 — GSI National Landslide Susceptibility Mapping
**Front:** What did GSI complete and over what area?
**Definition (learn):** GSI's national landslide susceptibility mapping at 1:50,000 scale.
**Breakdown:**
- About 4.3 lakh sq km of landslide-prone terrain in parts of 19 States/UTs (owner).
- Classifies terrain as low, moderate or high susceptibility (owner).
- Outputs served through Bhukosh and NGDR portals (owner).
- Dated Ministry of Mines/PIB 20 August 2025 per owner (⚠️ not verified).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-362, DM-368.
**Trap:** A susceptibility map is not a site-specific forecast or an enforcement tool.
**Mains hook:** 2019 GS-III zonation question: mapping as preventive governance with enforcement trade-off.
**Back:** *"Four-lakh-three-thousand square kilometres mapped, not monitored."*

### Flashcard DM-370 — NDMA landslide strategy and guidelines
**Front:** Name NDMA's landslide strategy and the underlying guidelines with years.
**Definition (learn):** National framework documents for landslide risk management.
**Breakdown:**
- National Landslide Risk Management Strategy (September 2019) (owner).
- Guidelines on Management of Landslides and Snow Avalanches (June 2009) (owner).
- Measures include construction restrictions in high-vulnerability zones, terrace farming replacing jhumming in the North-East, retaining walls, landslide insurance (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-362, DM-368, DM-374.
**Trap:** Name the strategy and guideline years precisely; do not conflate 2009 guidelines with 2019 strategy.
**Mains hook:** 2021 GS-III question on NLRMS: name the strategy and explain zonation to enforcement chain.
**Back:** *"2009 guideline, 2019 strategy: map, restrict, afforest."*

### Flashcard DM-371 — Uttarakhand 2013 disaster
**Front:** Give the date, toll and cascade of the 2013 Uttarakhand disaster.
**Definition (learn):** A multi-hazard disaster in June 2013 in the Himalaya.
**Breakdown:**
- Main day 16 June 2013; rain from 16 June; body search continued into September 2013 (✅ Wikipedia: 2013_North_India_floods).
- Over 89% of casualties in Uttarakhand; deaths recorded 6,054 (✅ Wikipedia infobox).
- Over 5,700 presumed dead per Uttarakhand government as of 16 July 2013 (✅ Wikipedia).
- A mid-day cloudburst centred on Uttarakhand (✅ Wikipedia).
- Rapid ice and snow melt flooded Chorabari Lake and then Kedarnath town (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: several cloudbursts, two major (PDF p.33).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia: 2013_North_India_floods; ✅ owner 10-adv §4).
**Links:** DM-363, DM-364, DM-366, DM-374.
**Trap:** Do not use "2013 cloudburst" alone; the event was multi-hazard and included regional states beyond Uttarakhand.
**Mains hook:** A single disaster combines cloudburst, flash flood and landslide; qualified conclusion on cascade-aware planning.
**Back:** *"Sixteenth June, six thousand fifty-four: cloudburst, flood, Kedarnath."*

### Flashcard DM-372 — Mining-linked landslides and the State list
**Front:** Which States did the owner name for mining- and subsidence-linked landslides?
**Definition (learn):** Owner separates mining and subsidence landslides from general slope instability.
**Breakdown:**
- Jharkhand, Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Andhra Pradesh, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Goa, Kerala (owner, PDF p.32).
- Adjacent to the 2025 mining-environmental-hazard question (owner: adjacent-only, not a direct DM question).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §2, §6; ✅ owner 10-adv §4).
**Links:** DM-362, DM-361.
**Trap:** Do not treat a mining question as a direct DM question without subsidence specifics.
**Mains hook:** Use the State list as evidence for a mining-hazard answer.
**Back:** *"Ten States dig, slopes slide."*

### Flashcard DM-373 — Himalayan development as GLOF accelerant
**Front:** Which development activities does the owner identify as accelerating glacial melt?
**Definition (learn):** Anthropogenic radiative-balance change from mass tourism, roads and hydropower accelerates melting and GLOFs.
**Breakdown:**
- "Mass tourism" and "developmental interventions such as roads and hydropower projects" (owner, PDF p.79).
- Black carbon surface heating also cited (owner).
- Hydropower siting near glacial lakes is the key trade-off (owner adv §7).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §3, PDF pp.78-79; ✅ owner 10-adv §1, §7).
**Links:** DM-366, DM-367, DM-376.
**Trap:** The development that sustains livelihoods is also a documented accelerant; do not argue "stop development" or "build freely" as a single answer.
**Mains hook:** Risk-informed siting as the conclusion (owner adv §7).
**Back:** *"Tourists, roads and dams warm the ice: development feeds the flood."*

### Flashcard DM-374 — Five worst Indian cloudburst events
**Front:** List the five worst Indian cloudburst events the owner names.
**Definition (learn):** Owner's list of major cloudburst events.
**Breakdown:**
- Great Musi flood of 1908 near Hyderabad.
- Mumbai cloudburst 2005.
- Jammu and Kashmir cloudburst 2010.
- Uttarakhand cloudbursts of 1998 and 2013.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §6, PDF p.34; ⚠️ not verified; Mumbai 2005 corroborated in DM-339).
**Links:** DM-363, DM-371, DM-339.
**Trap:** Do not present the list as a complete national ranking.
**Mains hook:** Use cases to test the cloudburst mechanism with named events.
**Back:** *"Musi, Mumbai, Kashmir, two Uttarakhand — the five named."*

### Flashcard DM-375 — Copenhagen cloudburst master plan
**Front:** What did Copenhagen's cloudburst plan aim to do?
**Definition (learn):** An international model cited for cloudburst stormwater management.
**Breakdown:**
- Aim: decouple 30–40% of excess stormwater from the combined sewer through concretisation, canal creation and greening (owner, PDF p.34).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §5; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-364, DM-330.
**Trap:** A foreign model is not an Indian implementation; use as comparison only.
**Mains hook:** Use Copenhagen to argue for blue-green urban drainage.
**Back:** *"Copenhagen splits thirty to forty percent of stormwater from the sewer."*

### Flashcard DM-376 — Concept: four Himalayan mass-movement hazards
**Front:** Distinguish landslide, cloudburst, avalanche and GLOF by mechanism, warning chain and institution.
**Definition (learn):** Each hazard has its own mechanism and warning or mitigation chain.
**Breakdown:**
- Landslide: gravity-driven rock or debris movement; GSI NLFC; zonation (DM-361 to DM-369).
- Cloudburst: extreme local rainfall; no specific NDMA guidelines; flood and landslide by extension (DM-363, DM-364).
- Avalanche: snowpack instability; DGRE warnings (DM-365).
- GLOF: dam failure of glacial lake; NDMA GLOF guidelines and NGRMP (DM-366, DM-367).
- Himalayan risk chain: young geology + steep slopes + extreme precipitation + seismicity + road/construction alteration + weak drainage (REVISION-CHART §14).
- Owner adv: governance split by hazard type across at least three institutions (owner 10-adv §3).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §8, 10-adv §3; ✅ REVISION-CHART §14).
**Links:** DM-361, DM-363, DM-365, DM-366, DM-377.
**Trap:** Landslide, avalanche, cloudburst and GLOF are the same hazard. Also: engineering cannot make every slope safe; avoidance may be preferable (REVISION-CHART §14).
**Mains hook:** 20-mark cascade answer: earthquake → landslide → blockage → outburst; cloudburst → flash flood → landslide; glacial retreat → GLOF. Qualified conclusion: risk-informed siting, not blanket anti-development.
**Back:** *"Slide, burst, avalanche, outburst: four hazards, four chains."*

### Flashcard DM-377 — PYQ route: 2019 zonation and 2021 Himalaya versus Western Ghats
**Front:** What are the landslide PYQ routes and their answer structures?
**Definition (learn):** 2019 GS-III Q18 (15 marks): landslide hazard zonation mapping. 2021 GS-I Q4 (10 marks): causes of landslides in the Himalaya and Western Ghats (differentiate). 2021 GS-III Q18 (15 marks): landslide causes, effects and NLRMS (owner PYQ integration, quoted paraphrase).
**Breakdown:**
- 2019: inputs (slope, geology, rainfall, seismicity, land use) → zones → no or conditional construction, drainage, evacuation; name GSI/NLRMS.
- 2021 GS-I: two-column comparison (geology and seismicity; rainfall and weathering; human intervention; response tool), then shared conclusion.
- 2021 GS-III: causes, effects and NLRMS.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 10-basic §13, 10-adv PYQ block).
**Links:** DM-362, DM-370, DM-376.
**Trap:** A 2021 Himalaya answer must differentiate the two regions, not list one common cause.
**Mains hook:** Use the 2-column comparison; close with risk-sensitive siting not blanket restriction.
**Back:** *"Zone in 2019, differentiate in 2021, strategy in 2021: map, compare, name."*


### GROUP — Forest Fire Risk Management

### Flashcard DM-378 — Fire triangle and the human-caused share
**Front:** What is the fire triangle for forest fire, and what share is human-caused?
**Definition (learn):** A forest fire needs fuel, oxygen and heat; fuel and oxygen are naturally present, so heat is the controllable trigger.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: more than 95% of forest fires are caused by negligence or unknowingly by humans (PDF p.47; ⚠️ document-period figure).
- Heat "really initiates fire" (owner).
- Fire is not always unnatural; owner says fires are often good for regeneration (owner basic §8).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §1, §8, PDF p.47; ✅ REVISION-CHART §15 fire triangle).
**Links:** DM-380, DM-379, DM-393.
**Trap:** "Forest fires are always unnatural" is wrong. The DM concern is uncontrolled combustion.
**Mains hook:** Use the heat-ignition link to direct prevention toward use practices, fire lines, patrols and incentives rather than planting more trees. Qualified conclusion: the 95% figure needs FSI verification.
**Back:** *"Fuel and air are free; heat is human: control the spark."*

### Flashcard DM-379 — Surface, ground and crown fire
**Front:** Distinguish surface, ground and crown fires.
**Definition (learn):** Fire types classified by the fuel layer they burn.
**Breakdown:**
- Surface fire: burns along the ground, consuming litter, twigs, dry grass (owner).
- Crown fire: burns the canopy, often sustained by a surface fire; especially dangerous in conifers because of resin (owner).
- Ground fire: burns organic matter below the surface (REVISION-CHART §15 only).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §2, PDF p.47; ✅ REVISION-CHART §15).
**Links:** DM-378, DM-381.
**Trap:** Ground fire is not in the owner's two-type list; the chart adds it. Do not merge crown and ground fire.
**Mains hook:** Fire type governs suppression risk and crew safety.
**Back:** *"Ground smoulders, surface runs, crown leaps."*

### Flashcard DM-380 — Natural and anthropogenic heat sources
**Front:** List natural and human heat sources of forest fire.
**Definition (learn):** Sources that supply the initiating heat.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: lightning, rolling-stone friction, dry bamboo-clump rubbing, volcanic explosion (owner).
- Deliberate: shifting cultivation, fire to induce tendu-leaf flush or fodder growth, encroachment, concealing illicit felling (owner).
- Accidental: farm-residue burning, campfires, vehicle sparks, resin tapping, discarded bidis and cigarettes (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §3, PDF p.47).
**Links:** DM-378, DM-358 (lightning), DM-390 (community exclusion).
**Trap:** Deliberate fire is not always carelessness; owner adv says structural exclusion from forest governance drives deliberate practices.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish deliberate and accidental causes in a 10-mark causes answer.
**Back:** *"Lightning, bamboo, Jhum, bidi: nature sparks, people spark."*

### Flashcard DM-381 — Topography and fire spread on slopes
**Front:** How does terrain alter forest-fire spread?
**Definition (learn):** On slopes, heated air rises along the slope and carries flames upward.
**Breakdown:**
- Fire starting downhill spreads upward fast (owner).
- Fire starting uphill is less likely to spread downhill (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §3, PDF p.47; ⚠️ general fire-behaviour point, not separately fetched).
**Links:** DM-379, DM-382.
**Trap:** The direction of spread on slopes is an answer-level detail; do not state it as universal for wind-driven fire.
**Mains hook:** Use in a terrain-based suppression answer. Qualified close: wind overrides terrain in some events.
**Back:** *"Fire climbs the hill, not down it."*

### Flashcard DM-382 — India's forest-fire distribution
**Front:** How is forest-fire incidence concentrated across India?
**Definition (learn):** Owner's document-period distribution pattern.
**Breakdown:**
- Fires occur annually in around half of India's 647 districts and in nearly all States (owner).
- 20 districts (3% of India's land area) accounted for 44% of forest-fire detections, 2003–2016 (owner; joint MoEFCC–World Bank report).
- Himalayan coniferous belt (fir, spruce, deodar, pine) and Ganga–Yamuna watershed especially prone (owner).
- Forest exposure: 54.40% occasional, 7.49% moderately frequent, 2.40% high incidence (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §3, §6, PDF p.47; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-381, DM-383, DM-391.
**Trap:** Percentages are document-period; the district figure is not current.
**Mains hook:** Use the concentration to argue for targeted resources in high-incidence districts (owner adv §4).
**Back:** *"Twenty districts, three percent of land, forty-four percent of fires."*

### Flashcard DM-383 — FAST 3.0 and the satellite alert chain
**Front:** Describe the FAST 3.0 chain and its launch date.
**Definition (learn):** FSI's fire alert system fed by NASA satellite data, processed through ISRO's National Remote Sensing Centre (Hyderabad) and FSI.
**Breakdown:**
- NASA active-fire data → ISRO NRSC → FSI, which labels fires by forest-range convention (owner).
- About 40,000 registered users, government and public, updated in near real time (owner).
- Alerts sent by SMS and e-mail (owner, FSI).
- Launch 16 January 2019 (owner; 📰 not confirmed by the FSI page fetched on 30 Oct 2026, which contained no date).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §2; ⚠️ launch date unverified).
**Links:** DM-384, DM-385, DM-393.
**Trap:** An alert is a detection measurement, not a suppression capacity (owner adv §10). FAST 3.0 is not identical to the Large Forest Fire Monitoring Programme (DM-384).
**Mains hook:** 15-mark technology question: detection is strong, suppression is unquantified. Qualified close: alert-to-response time is the metric.
**Back:** *"NASA sees, ISRO relays, FSI warns forty thousand."*

### Flashcard DM-384 — Pre Warning Alert System and Large Forest Fire Monitoring Programme
**Front:** Trace FSI's detection tools from 2016 to 2019.
**Definition (learn):** FSI's indigenous early-warning and large-fire programmes.
**Breakdown:**
- 2016: indigenous Pre Warning Alert System using forest cover, forest type, temperature, rainfall and recent fire history (owner).
- 2017: shifted to a 5 km × 5 km grid (owner).
- 2019: Large Forest Fire Monitoring Programme (beta) using SNPP-VIIRS near-real-time data, part of FAST 3.0 (owner).
- MODIS-based alerts from 2004; SNPP-VIIRS from 2017 (owner, FSI page; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §4, PDF p.48; ⚠️ dates unverified).
**Links:** DM-383, DM-385.
**Trap:** The Large Forest Fire Monitoring Programme is a part of FAST 3.0, not the whole.
**Mains hook:** Trace the technology-to-institution chain precisely.
**Back:** *"Sixteen warns, seventeen grids, nineteen large fires."*

### Flashcard DM-385 — Van Agni geoportal
**Front:** What is Van Agni?
**Definition (learn):** FSI's single-point geoportal for near-real-time monitoring of large forest fires, operational from 2019.
**Breakdown:**
- Carries pre-fire and near-real-time alerts (owner).
- Part of the same FSI alert architecture as FAST 3.0, not a competing system (owner).
- FSI portal link confirmed on the FSI forest-fire page (✅ FSI page fetched 30 Oct 2026, link present).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ✅ portal existence confirmed; date ⚠️).
**Links:** DM-383, DM-384.
**Trap:** Do not cite a later FAST version number; none is officially documented in the owner.
**Mains hook:** Part of the detection layer; qualify with the suppression gap.
**Back:** *"Van Agni: one geoportal, one fire map."*

### Flashcard DM-386 — Forest Fire Prevention and Management (FPM) Scheme
**Front:** What is the FPM Scheme?
**Definition (learn):** MoEFCC's continuing centrally sponsored scheme supporting State and UT forest fire prevention and management.
**Breakdown:**
- Supports States and UTs on prevention and management (PIB/MoEFCC, 22 July 2024, per owner; ⚠️ not verified).
- Funding and budget levels require a dated MoEFCC source (owner adv §9).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-387, DM-390.
**Trap:** Do not confuse FPM with FAST 3.0; FPM is funding and prevention, FAST is detection.
**Mains hook:** Use FPM as the funding and community-partnership limb.
**Back:** *"FPM funds prevention; FAST spots the smoke."*

### Flashcard DM-387 — National Master Plan for Forest Fire Control
**Front:** Name the four components of the National Master Plan for Forest Fire Control.
**Definition (learn):** MoEF's integrated fire-management plan.
**Breakdown:**
- Prevention of human-caused fires.
- Joint Forest Fire Management (participatory prevention).
- Prompt multi-point detection, patrolling and communication networks.
- Remote-sensing prioritisation.
- Recommends a National Fire Danger Rating System and Fire Forecasting System (owner, PDF p.54).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §4-5, PDF p.54; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-386, DM-390, DM-391.
**Trap:** Joint Forest Fire Management is part of the plan; the gap is implementation, not design (owner adv §3).
**Mains hook:** Argue that the community-exclusion gap is implementation, not plan design.
**Back:** *"Prevent, Join, Detect, Sense — four parts, one plan."*

### Flashcard DM-388 — Burnt Scar Assessment
**Front:** What is burnt-scar assessment and what method does FSI use?
**Definition (learn):** Assessing forest area affected by fire to gauge damage.
**Breakdown:**
- Historically used high-temporal IRS AWiFS data (2015–16) (owner).
- FSI developing a semi-automated burnt-area and severity classification (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §2; ⚠️ method status unverified).
**Links:** DM-383, DM-393.
**Trap:** A development method is not an operational national damage-accounting system (owner adv §9).
**Mains hook:** Use to argue that damage accounting lags detection.
**Back:** *"Scar after fire, scored by satellite, still in development."*

### Flashcard DM-389 — Forest fire line
**Front:** What is a forest fire line?
**Definition (learn):** A British-period practice of removing forest litter along boundaries in summer to stop fire spreading between compartments.
**Breakdown:**
- Summer clearing along the forest boundary (owner).
- Related prevention: firebreaks (small clearings or ditches) (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §2, PDF p.48; ⚠️ historical detail not verified).
**Links:** DM-387, DM-378.
**Trap:** Fire line and firebreak overlap; the owner's definition is the boundary-litter clearance.
**Mains hook:** Use in a prevention answer. Qualified close: fire-line upkeep needs labour.
**Back:** *"Clear the litter line before the fire crosses."*

### Flashcard DM-390 — Community exclusion and the crisis-only critique
**Front:** What is the owner's critique of forest-fire community governance?
**Definition (learn):** Forest-dependent communities are excluded from routine resource governance but relied on for firefighting in crises.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner quote: "We never ask village communities to participate in managing forest resources, but expect their support at times of crisis" (PDF p.49).
- Owner diagnosis: "the major cause of this failure is the piecemeal approach to the problem" (PDF p.49).
- Community dependence on fuelwood, grazing, NTFP, shifting cultivation and tendu leaf creates fire-inducing practices (owner adv §2).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-adv §1-2, PDF p.49).
**Links:** DM-380, DM-387, DM-386, DM-393.
**Trap:** Do not attribute deliberate fires to carelessness alone; the structural driver is exclusion.
**Mains hook:** 20-mark governance answer: community exclusion versus crisis reliance; qualified conclusion on joint management.
**Back:** *"Ask in crisis, exclude in peace: that is the piecemeal fire."*

### Flashcard DM-391 — National Institute of Forest Fire Management (recommended)
**Front:** What institution did the owner recommend for forest-fire management?
**Definition (learn):** A recommended National Institute of Forest Fire Management with satellite centres.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: "a National Institute of Forest Fire Management with satellite centres in different parts of the country" (PDF p.49).
- Current implementation status not verified (owner adv §3).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-adv §3, PDF p.49; ⚠️ status unverified).
**Links:** DM-387, DM-382.
**Trap:** A recommended institute is not an existing institute.
**Mains hook:** One of the missing elements in the owner's list (DM-393).
**Back:** *"Recommended institute, satellite centres: verify before you cite."*

### Flashcard DM-392 — Wildland–urban interface
**Front:** What is the wildland–urban interface and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Settlements on forest fringes where fire meets human property.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner names it as needing tools to minimise impacts on human life and property (PDF p.49).
- Owner treats it as a growing, under-detailed emerging risk (owner adv §7).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-adv §7, PDF p.49).
**Links:** DM-393, DM-382.
**Trap:** It is not a standard rural-forest fire approach.
**Mains hook:** Name the interface as a distinct emerging zone in a 20-mark governance answer.
**Back:** *"Where forest meets house, the fire meets people."*

### Flashcard DM-393 — Concept: detection versus suppression gap in forest fire
**Front:** Why does forest-fire governance remain weak even with FAST 3.0?
**Definition (learn):** Detection is satellite-based, near-real-time and nationally uniform; suppression depends on State forest crews, equipment and access time, which no dated national source quantifies.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner's missing list: national focus, technical resources, a dedicated institute, strategic fire centres, inter-Ministry coordination, funding, HR development, fire research and extension programmes (PDF p.49; owner adv §8).
- Owner names research gaps: ignition sources, burn severity, season, fire size; post-fire runoff; narrow monitoring reach (owner adv §5).
- Alert with no crew in reach is a measurement of failure, not mitigation (owner adv §10).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 11-basic §13, 11-adv §1, §8, §10).
**Links:** DM-383, DM-390, DM-391, DM-392.
**Trap:** FAST 3.0 alone is not an answer (owner basic §13 firewall).
**Mains hook:** 15-mark technology answer: thesis that India detects more than it suppresses; close with alert-to-response and burnt-area outcomes.
**Back:** *"Detect fast, suppress slow: close the gap with crews and fire centres."*

### GROUP — Industrial, Chemical, Nuclear and CBRN Emergencies

### Flashcard DM-394 — Industrial hazard: definition and sources
**Front:** Define an industrial hazard and list its sources.
**Definition (learn):** Threats to people and life-support systems from mass production of goods and services, becoming disasters when they exceed coping or absorptive capacities (owner, PDF p.51).
**Breakdown:**
- Sources: any production stage: extraction, processing, manufacture, transport, storage, use, disposal (owner).
- Accident causes: process or system failure; natural calamity; sabotage or terrorism (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §1-2, PDF p.51).
**Links:** DM-396, DM-398, DM-409.
**Trap:** An industrial accident is not a disaster until it exceeds coping capacity (REVISION-CHART §3).
**Mains hook:** Use the lifecycle to argue prevention beyond the plant gate. Qualified close.
**Back:** *"Extract, make, move, store, use, dump — each step can fail."*

### Flashcard DM-395 — Chemical Accidents (Emergency Planning, Preparedness and Response) Rules, 1996
**Front:** What do the 1996 Chemical Accident Rules establish?
**Definition (learn):** Rules defining "chemical accident" and "major chemical accident" and setting up a Central Crisis Group.
**Breakdown:**
- Central Crisis Group chaired by the Environment Ministry Secretary (owner).
- Monitors post-accident situations, analyses accidents, suggests preventive steps (owner).
- Provision for State, district and local crisis groups (owner, PDF p.52).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §2, §5; ⚠️ rule text not fetched).
**Links:** DM-396, DM-398, DM-403, DM-410.
**Trap:** This is a hazard-specific parallel structure, not the NDMA–SDMA–DDMA cascade; the coordination question with DM Act machinery is open (owner adv §3).
**Mains hook:** A 15-mark CBRN answer must name this body, not "NDRF should respond."
**Back:** *"Nineteen ninety-six: Environment Secretary chairs the crisis group."*

### Flashcard DM-396 — Statutory evolution of industrial-hazard law
**Front:** List the statutory chain for industrial hazard.
**Definition (learn):** Chemical-class laws predated the EPA; Bhopal then prompted new rules.
**Breakdown:**
- Explosives Act 1884; Petroleum Act 1934; Factories Act 1948; Insecticide Act 1968 (owner).
- Environment (Protection) Act 1986 (umbrella).
- MSIHC Rules 1989 (post-Bhopal).
- Chemical Accident Rules 1996.
- Hazardous Wastes (Management, Handling and Transboundary Movement) Rules 2008 (owner).
- Public Liability Insurance Act 1991.
- NGT (2010) (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §2, §3, adv §12; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** DM-395, DM-397, DM-398.
**Trap:** The Epidemic Diseases Act 1897 governs epidemics, not chemical disasters (owner basic §8); keep it out of the chemical chain.
**Mains hook:** "Dense law, weak enforcement" (owner adv §1). Qualified close.
**Back:** *"Eighty-four, thirty-four, forty-eight, eighty-six, eighty-nine, ninety-six: chain the laws."*

### Flashcard DM-397 — Public Liability Insurance Act 1991 and Environmental Relief Fund
**Front:** What principle does the PLI Act 1991 establish and what fund did it create?
**Definition (learn):** The Act gives immediate or interim relief and establishes no-fault liability.
**Breakdown:**
- Company liable "even if it had done everything in its power to prevent the accident" (owner, PDF p.52).
- Civil compensation liability, not criminal (owner basic §8).
- Compensation credited to the Environmental Relief Fund (2008) (owner).
- Funded partly by NGT-ordered compensation (owner adv §9).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §2, §8; ⚠️ fund details not verified).
**Links:** DM-396, DM-398, DM-399.
**Trap:** "No fault" does not mean criminal liability for every accident.
**Mains hook:** Strict liability addresses post-Bhopal slow liability determination (owner adv §3).
**Back:** *"No fault, still pay: insurance for the victim, not the verdict."*

### Flashcard DM-398 — Manufacture, Storage and Import of Hazardous Substances (MSIHC) Rules 1989
**Front:** What did the Bhopal disaster directly prompt?
**Definition (learn):** The MSIHC Rules, 1989, prompted by the Bhopal disaster (owner). ⚠️ Their parent Act (EPA 1986) is from general knowledge, not the owner text.
**Breakdown:**
- Governs manufacture, storage and import of hazardous substances (owner, PDF p.51).
- Followed by the Hazardous Wastes Rules 2008 (owner).
- Later the Chemical Accident Rules 1996 (DM-395).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §2; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** DM-399, DM-396, DM-395.
**Trap:** MSIHC is not the chemical emergency-planning rule; that is the 1996 Rules.
**Mains hook:** Trace the post-Bhopal regulatory reaction in a chemical-accident answer.
**Back:** *"Eighty-nine rules, after eighty-four's gas."*

### Flashcard DM-399 — Bhopal gas disaster (1984)
**Front:** Give the date, gas, place and toll of the Bhopal gas disaster.
**Definition (learn):** A release of methyl isocyanate (MIC) at Union Carbide India Limited's pesticide plant in Bhopal.
**Breakdown:**
- Leak 2–3 December 1984; main release early on 3 December (✅ Wikipedia: Bhopal_disaster).
- About 30 tonnes of MIC escaped in 45–60 minutes; about 40 tonnes within two hours (✅ Wikipedia).
- Tolls differ by source: official immediate 2,259 (Varma 2005); Madhya Pradesh government 3,787; 1991 official certification 3,928; Sambhavna estimates of 8,000 in the first weeks plus further deaths; infobox "at least 3,787" (✅ Wikipedia).
- Injuries about 558,125 per a 2006 government affidavit (✅ Wikipedia).
- UCIL majority-owned by Union Carbide Corporation; UCC sold its stake in 1994 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia: Bhopal_disaster; owner 12-basic §3 for MSIHC linkage).
**Links:** DM-398, DM-397, DM-394.
**Trap:** Quote the toll as a range with source; do not give a single number. Owner does not provide toll.
**Mains hook:** Bhopal as the prompt for post-accident regulation; qualified conclusion: liability and enforcement lag the disaster.
**Back:** *"Methyl isocyanate, three December, thirty tonnes in an hour."*

### Flashcard DM-400 — Major Accident Hazard (MAH) units
**Front:** What is an MAH unit and what count does the owner report?
**Definition (learn):** A classified high-risk industrial unit.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: about 1,861 MAH units across 301 districts, 25 States and 3 UTs (document-period, PDF p.52).
- Buffer-zone and "no man's zone" legislation recommended (owner, PDF p.53).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §2; ⚠️ count document-period, not verified).
**Links:** DM-394, DM-410.
**Trap:** Do not quote 1,861 as current.
**Mains hook:** Settlement encroachment on buffer zones is an equity issue (owner adv §6).
**Back:** *"Eighteen sixty-one, document-old: verify before citing."*

### Flashcard DM-401 — Four nuclear protection principles
**Front:** Name the four protection principles against radiation.
**Definition (learn):** Owner's four principles for nuclear hazard protection.
**Breakdown:**
- Limit exposure time.
- Maximise distance from source.
- Shielding (lead, concrete, water barriers).
- Containment (closed-system confinement).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §2, §7, PDF p.55).
**Links:** DM-402, DM-403.
**Trap:** Containment is confinement of the source, not the same as shielding.
**Mains hook:** Use the principles precisely in a radiological answer.
**Back:** *"Time, distance, shield, contain: the radiation quartet."*

### Flashcard DM-402 — AERB and DAE Crisis Management Group
**Front:** What do AERB and DAE do in nuclear emergencies?
**Definition (learn):** AERB is the nuclear regulator under the Atomic Energy Act 1962; DAE is the nodal agency for man-made radiological emergencies.
**Breakdown:**
- AERB grants siting, construction, commissioning and decommissioning consent after safety review (owner).
- AERB reviews emergency-preparedness plans, including transport of radioactive sources (owner).
- DAE Crisis Management Group, chaired by the Additional Secretary, coordinates with NCMC, NEC and NDMA (owner, PDF p.55).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §5; ⚠️ AERB and CMG not fetched).
**Links:** DM-401, DM-403, DM-395.
**Trap:** The Chemical Crisis Group is chemical-specific; the CMG is nuclear-specific; there is no biological parallel named (owner adv §8).
**Mains hook:** CBRN answer: name each specialist command separately.
**Back:** *"AERB licenses, DAE commands, CMG coordinates."*

### Flashcard DM-403 — NDMA guidelines for chemical, nuclear and medical emergencies
**Front:** Name the three NDMA guidelines for this hazard family with years.
**Definition (learn):** The NDMA guideline set for chemical, nuclear and medical preparedness (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Guidelines on Chemical Disasters (April 2007).
- Guidelines on Management of Nuclear and Radiological Emergencies (February 2009).
- Guidelines on Medical Preparedness and Mass Casualty Management (October 2007).
- Owner adv: the guidance is about two decades old and predates present inventories and NaTech siting (owner adv §10).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-395, DM-401, DM-409.
**Trap:** Cite the year and name, not "NDMA guidelines" generically.
**Mains hook:** Guidance ageing while exposure changed is a freshness critique.
**Back:** *"Oh-seven, oh-nine, oh-seven: chemical, nuclear, medical."*

### Flashcard DM-404 — National Oil Spill Disaster Contingency Plan and oil-pollution law
**Front:** Name the oil-spill contingency plan, its agency and the treaties.
**Definition (learn):** Multi-agency oil-spill response coordinated by the Indian Coast Guard.
**Breakdown:**
- Indian Coast Guard's National Oil Spill Disaster Contingency Plan (NOSDCP); 25th meeting cited at Vadinar, Gujarat, 23 November 2023 (owner).
- Merchant Shipping Act 1958 covers prevention and containment of sea oil pollution (owner).
- India is party to SOLAS, MARPOL and the Bunker Convention (2001) (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §5, PDF p.57; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-405, DM-406, DM-407.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Merchant Shipping Act with the EPA; oil at sea follows the shipping regime.
**Mains hook:** Match the instrument to the hazard, never conflating chemical, nuclear and oil regimes (owner basic §11).
**Back:** *"Coast Guard plans the oil; Merchant Shipping, SOLAS, MARPOL, Bunker."*

### Flashcard DM-405 — Oil-spill response techniques and Indian bioremediation
**Front:** List oil-spill response techniques and the Indian bioremediation products.
**Definition (learn):** Containment, dispersion, burning and bioremediation.
**Breakdown:**
- Containment with floating barriers.
- Chemical dispersants (surfactants).
- In-situ burning.
- Bioremediation: Oilzapper and Oilivorous-S, TERI-developed; Oilivorous-S jointly with Indian Oil Corporation (owner, PDF p.57).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §4, §7; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-404, DM-407.
**Trap:** Dispersants and burning are not interchangeable with bioremediation.
**Mains hook:** Use Indian innovations in a recovery answer.
**Back:** *"Barrier, disperse, burn, digest: TERI's Oilzapper and Oilivorous-S."*

### Flashcard DM-406 — Deepwater Horizon (2010)
**Front:** Give the date, release and duration of the Deepwater Horizon oil spill.
**Definition (learn):** The 2010 Gulf of Mexico well blowout and oil release, used as an international benchmark.
**Breakdown:**
- Explosion 20 April 2010 (✅ Wikipedia: Deepwater_Horizon_oil_spill).
- Federal estimate 4.9 million barrels, ±10% (✅ Wikipedia).
- Oil flowed for 87 days (✅ Wikipedia).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ Wikipedia: Deepwater_Horizon_oil_spill).
**Links:** DM-404, DM-407.
**Trap:** Owner cites it as an international comparator, not evidence on India's regime (owner adv §4).
**Mains hook:** Use scale to show consequence severity, not regulatory context.
**Back:** *"Four-point-nine million barrels, eighty-seven days, April twentieth."*

### Flashcard DM-407 — CPCL Chennai refinery oil spill (December 2023)
**Front:** Summarise the owner's CPCL Chennai oil-spill account and its verification status.
**Definition (learn):** Owner cites a December 2023 Chennai Petroleum Corporation Ltd refinery spill.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: reached at least 20 sq km into the sea; damaged Ennore creek and Kosasthalaiyar river over a week-long event (owner, PDF p.56).
- Verification: the fetched Wikipedia Ennore page covers a 2017 ship collision, not the CPCL event; the CPCL figures are unverified (⚠️).
- Do not conflate the 2017 Ennore spill (LPG tanker collision, 28 January 2017, 34,000 sq m per Coast Guard) with the CPCL spill.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §6; ⚠️ unverified).
**Links:** DM-404, DM-405, DM-406.
**Trap:** The 2017 Ennore incident is a different event; do not mix dates or area.
**Mains hook:** A contemporary domestic refinery case; quote the area only with an official source.
**Back:** *"CPCL in December twenty-three: twenty square kilometres, owner says, verify first."*

### Flashcard DM-408 — Kandla 2001 earthquake and acrylonitrile release (NaTech)
**Front:** What NaTech example does the owner give from the 2001 Kandla earthquake?
**Definition (learn):** An earthquake triggering a chemical release at an industrial site.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: acrylonitrile release during the 2001 Kandla earthquake (owner, PDF p.51).
- Owner: phosphoric-acid-sludge containment damaged during the 1999 Odisha cyclone (owner, PDF p.51).
- Not verified against an external source in this build (⚠️).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-basic §3, PDF p.51; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** DM-409, DM-326, DM-394.
**Trap:** These are risk-assessment lessons, not proof that every facility failed similarly (owner basic §13).
**Mains hook:** Use NaTech to argue plant safety cannot ignore quake or cyclone exposure.
**Back:** *"Quake releases acrylonitrile; cyclone wets the sludge."*

### Flashcard DM-409 — Concept: NaTech cascading industrial risk
**Front:** What is NaTech and why does it matter in industrial safety?
**Definition (learn):** Natural-hazard-triggered technological accident: a natural hazard turns contained industrial risk into an active chemical emergency.
**Breakdown:**
- Examples: 2001 Kandla earthquake (DM-408); 1999 Odisha cyclone (DM-326).
- Industrial risk assessment cannot be siloed from natural-hazard topics (owner adv §2).
- Coastal and cyclone-exposed industrial belts raise NaTech exposure (owner adv §10).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-adv §2, §10).
**Links:** DM-408, DM-326, DM-312, DM-394.
**Trap:** A well-managed plant can still fail under an unrelated natural hazard.
**Mains hook:** 20-mark governance answer: enforcement and cascade screening decide safety. Qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Nature trips the plant: NaTech turns risk into release."*

### Flashcard DM-410 — Concept: enforcement gap, buffer zones and CBRN command
**Front:** Why does industrial and CBRN governance fail even with dense law?
**Definition (learn):** Dense statutory regime plus weak enforcement, harmonisation gaps and settlement encroachment on buffer zones.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner's gap list: classification and labelling harmonisation; no cryogenic storage rules; no risk-assessment legislation; no victim-compensation statute beyond PLI Act; weak international harmonisation (owner adv §8, PDF p.53).
- Buffer-zone legislation and relocation of settled colonies recommended (owner, PDF p.53); relocation is socially costly (owner adv §7).
- Chemical, nuclear and biological emergencies need separate regimes (REVISION-CHART §16; owner basic §13).
- Core rule: general DM coordination and specialised technical command operate together (REVISION-CHART §16).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** (✅ owner 12-adv §1, §6, §8; ✅ REVISION-CHART §16).
**Links:** DM-395, DM-400, DM-402, DM-403, DM-409.
**Trap:** "NDRF should respond" is not a CBRN answer; the Environment Secretary's crisis group, the DAE CMG and NCDC each have roles. Epidemic Diseases Act 1897 is for epidemics.
**Mains hook:** 20-mark critique: thesis that law is necessary, but enforcement, land use and cascade screening decide safety.
**Back:** *"Law is dense, enforcement thin: buffer, command, screen."*
